# Hisar — Design

Status: **Draft, Phase 0**. This document is the source of truth for the security model, data model and sync protocol. Change it before changing the code.

## 1. Goals and non-goals

**Goals**

- End-to-end encrypted password manager for developers, run as a hosted service (`api.tryhisar.com`): one server binary + Postgres.
- Person-to-person sharing by email: anyone can share a vault they own with anyone else, and nobody can list the accounts on the server.
- Zero-knowledge server: the server never sees the master password, the Secret Key, any decryption key or any plaintext item data.
- Offline-first clients: every client keeps a full encrypted copy and works with no network.
- Shared vaults between people. Account recovery only through the Emergency Kit (§6): a forgotten master password cannot be recovered by anyone.
- An admin dashboard on every server at `/admin` (§5.1): admins sign in with their Hisar account, and the browser runs the same Go crypto compiled to WebAssembly. It can never read vault contents, reset a master password or recover a vault, because the server holds none of those keys.
- Clients: desktop (first), browser extension, mobile (iPhone and Android), CLI.
- One implementation of the crypto, written in Go, shared by all clients.

**Non-goals (for now)**

- Billing, SSO/SCIM, enterprise policy. Organizations and teams exist (§5.11), without any of these.
- Open registration: sign-up stays invite-only (§5.1).
- Protecting an unlocked device from local malware (out of scope for every password manager).
- Hiding metadata from the server completely (see §2.3).

## 2. Threat model

### 2.1 Assets

Item contents (passwords, notes, TOTP seeds, passkeys, attachments), item metadata (titles, URLs), vault names, and the users' master passwords and Secret Keys.

### 2.2 Adversaries and expected outcome

| Adversary | Capability | Expected outcome |
|---|---|---|
| Network attacker | Reads/modifies traffic | TLS + OPAQUE: learns nothing, cannot impersonate either side |
| Server breach (DB dump) | Full copy of DB and backups | Ciphertext only. Offline guessing impossible without the Secret Key (128-bit) |
| Malicious/compromised server (live) | Arbitrary responses | Cannot read data or forge items (AEAD + AAD). Can withhold or roll back data (detected, §7.5). Can substitute public keys during sharing (mitigated by fingerprint verification, §5.6). Can claim an invitation was accepted (§5.8) or an account is in a team (§5.11), so that owners' apps wrap vault keys to an account it controls: apps trust the server's records of who accepted and who is in which team, and only pin and show the keys they wrap to |
| Stolen locked device | Disk image | Needs master password; Secret Key on disk alone is not enough |
| Stolen unlocked device / local malware | Memory, keystrokes | Out of scope. Minimised by auto-lock and short key lifetime |
| Phishing site | Lures user to wrong origin | Extension autofills only on exact origin match (§9.2) |
| Another user | Legit account | Sees only vaults shared with them or given to their teams, and only the people they share a vault with, who invited them, or who are in an organization with them (§5.8, §5.11). Cannot learn whether an email has an account. An organization admin manages its rosters and gets no vault key for that; an admin who adds themselves to a team gets its vaults like any member, a roster change every member of the organization sees and the audit log records |

### 2.3 Accepted metadata leakage

The server sees: account emails, the emails people invite or share with, which users can access which vaults, number of items per vault, ciphertext sizes, timestamps, device list, IPs. Item titles, URLs and vault names are encrypted. For organizations (§5.11) it sees their names, team names, who is a member of which organization and team with which role, the emails invited to them, and which teams were given which vaults with which role. For favorites (§4.7) it sees that an account saved its preferences record, when and how large it is, never which items are starred. For share links (§5.9) it sees who created a link, its size, expiry, view limit and views, whether it needs a passphrase, and the IP addresses that open it; never what was shared or the key. Website icons (§4.5) are fetched by each device from the website itself, so the server never learns those domains, but the websites see the device's IP address asking for their icon; Settings → Appearance turns that off.

## 3. Cryptographic primitives

All from the Go standard library or `golang.org/x/crypto`. No custom primitives.

| Purpose | Primitive | Go package |
|---|---|---|
| Password stretching | Argon2id | `golang.org/x/crypto/argon2` |
| Key derivation | HKDF-SHA256 | `crypto/hkdf` |
| Symmetric AEAD | XChaCha20-Poly1305 (24-byte random nonce) | `golang.org/x/crypto/chacha20poly1305` |
| Public-key wrapping | HPKE base mode, hybrid post-quantum KEM ML-KEM-768 + X25519, HKDF-SHA256, ChaCha20-Poly1305 (RFC 9180) | `crypto/hpke` |
| Signatures (key fingerprints, invites) | Ed25519 | `crypto/ed25519` |
| Authentication | OPAQUE (RFC 9807), ristretto255 suite | `github.com/bytemare/opaque` |
| Randomness | OS CSPRNG | `crypto/rand` |
| Share links only (§5.9), which a browser decrypts with WebCrypto | AES-256-GCM (12-byte random nonce), PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 for the optional passphrase | `crypto/aes`, `crypto/cipher`, `crypto/pbkdf2` |

Every ciphertext is versioned (`v1`) so primitives can be rotated later.

## 4. Key hierarchy

```
master password ──Argon2id──► k_pw ─┐
                                    ├─HKDF──► AUK  (Account Unlock Key) ──decrypts──► keyset
Secret Key ───────HKDF────────► k_sk ┘   └──HKDF──► auth secret ──► OPAQUE login

keyset = { sym key K_user, KEM seed (ML-KEM-768+X25519), Ed25519 signing key }
   └─ KEM public key ◄── HPKE-wraps ── vault key K_vault (one per vault, per member)
                                              └─ XChaCha20-Poly1305 ── items, attachments keys
```

### 4.1 Secret Key

- 128 bits from `crypto/rand`, generated on the user's first device. Never sent to the server.
- Display format: `H1-AAAAAA-XXXXX-XXXXX-XXXXX-XXXXX-XXXXXX` — version, 6-char account ID, 26 Crockford base32 chars.
- Stored on each device in the OS keychain (macOS Keychain, Windows Credential Manager, libsecret, iOS Keychain, Android Keystore).
- Printed on the Emergency Kit together with the server URL and email. The A4 PDF is generated client-side (`core/emergencykit`, gopdf with the embedded Go fonts) and has a blank line for the master password, which is never printed. Its QR code holds `hisar://sign-in?server=…&email=…&secretKey=…`.
- Desktop saves the kit through a native save dialog: right after sign up (the Secret Key stays in memory until the user confirms or the app locks), and later from Settings after re-entering the master password. The CLI has `hisar emergency-kit [-o file]`. Files are written with mode 0600, and the CLI never overwrites an existing file.

### 4.2 Deriving AUK and the auth secret

```
pw      = NFKD(master password), UTF-8
salt    = random 16 bytes, per account, stored on server (public)
k_pw    = Argon2id(pw, salt, t=24, m=64 MiB, p=4, len=32)
k_sk    = HKDF-SHA256(ikm=SecretKey, salt=accountID, info="hisar/v1/secret-key", len=32)
ikm     = k_pw || k_sk
AUK     = HKDF-SHA256(ikm, salt=nil, info="hisar/v1/auk",  len=32)
authKey = HKDF-SHA256(ikm, salt=nil, info="hisar/v1/auth", len=32)
```

- Argon2id parameters are stored per account so they can be raised later. 64 MiB is chosen to fit inside the iOS AutoFill extension memory limit, so cost is tuned with `t`. t=24 takes ≈ 270 ms on an M2 Pro; re-check on phones in Phase 4.
- Clients enforce `MinArgon2Params` and `MaxArgon2Params` in `core/crypt`. The accepted ranges remain 2 to 64 passes, 32 MiB to 1 GiB, and 1 to 16 lanes for existing accounts and exports. Values outside those bounds produce a readable error before derivation. The upper bound can still exhaust a constrained device. Lowering it requires a migration or an approved per-device resource policy.
- `authKey` is the OPAQUE password input. OPAQUE's internal key-stretching function is set to identity because stretching already happened. A server that steals the OPAQUE record still needs the Secret Key to guess the password.
- AUK and `authKey` are independent HKDF outputs: the server learns nothing about AUK from the login.

### 4.3 Keyset

Generated at signup on the first device:

- `K_user`: 32 random bytes, encrypts the user's private data that is not in a vault: share link labels (§5.9) and the account preferences record with the favorites (§4.7).
- Hybrid KEM keypair (ML-KEM-768 + X25519, derived from a 32-byte seed): receives vault keys. The hybrid stays secure as long as either half holds, so recorded traffic and DB dumps remain safe against a future quantum computer.
- Ed25519 keypair: signs the KEM public key bound to the account ID, and invites, so others can verify it (§5.6).

The keyset is stored as three 32-byte seeds (`K_user`, KEM seed, Ed25519 seed); the key pairs are derived from them.

The private half is serialised and encrypted:

```
encKeyset = XChaCha20-Poly1305(key=AUK, nonce=random24, aad="hisar/v1/keyset"||accountID, plaintext=keyset)
```

The server stores `encKeyset`, the KEM public key, the Ed25519 public key and the signature `Ed25519.Sign("hisar/v1/kem-key" || accountID || kemPub)`.

### 4.4 Vault keys

- Each vault has a random 32-byte `K_vault`.
- For every member, the server stores `0x01 || HPKE.Seal(memberKEMPub, info="hisar/v1/vault-key" || vaultID || memberAccountID, K_vault)`.
- Removing a member rotates `K_vault` (`session.RemoveVaultMember`, `POST /vaults/{id}/rotate`, owner only):
  - The owner's client generates a new key, re-encrypts the vault name and every item, including tombstones, under a new revision, and wraps the new key for each remaining member. A tombstone is sealed again with the empty content every deletion sends, without opening the old one: no app reads a tombstone's content, so one that does not open cannot block the rotation. Each member's signed KEM key is verified, and the client's `MemberVerifier` hook (key pinning in the CLI and the desktop app) must accept it.
  - The server applies it in one transaction. It returns 409 unless the submitted items match the stored items and revisions exactly, so concurrent edits are never lost; the client then retries, up to 3 times, reloading the vault's key before each attempt, since another owner may have rotated it. It also returns 409 unless the request's `keyGeneration` is the vault's current one (required for organization vaults, checked for any vault when sent), so two owners rotating from the same key cannot both succeed. It replaces all access grants, gives every item a new seq so all devices resync, and drops the vault's item history, because that history is encrypted with the old key.
  - The rotating owner must keep owner access. Rotation and vault deletion requests may be up to 32 MiB, and only with a live session; the server refuses any other body above 4 MiB from its headers, before reading it, and most endpoints accept at most 2 MiB.
  - Copies the removed member downloaded before the rotation stay readable to them; that is unavoidable.
- Organization vaults (§5.11) use the same per-member wrapping: a team is a roster on the server, never a key. The server decides who is entitled to an organization vault from its teams; an owner's app wraps `K_vault` for each entitled account that has no wrapped key yet, and when an account loses its entitlement the server deletes its wrapped key at once and marks the vault for rotation, which an owner's app then performs as above, keeping every remaining member and role.

### 4.5 Items

An item has two encrypted parts so lists and search don't decrypt every secret:

- **overview**: title, category, URLs, tags, icon, subtitle (username).
- **details**: fields, passwords, notes, TOTP seeds, passkeys, history of field values.

**Categories.** `overview.category` is the item's type, set when the item is created and kept by every edit:

| Category | Holds | Shown as |
|---|---|---|
| `login`, or empty (items saved before categories) | username, password, websites, one-time password, passkeys, custom fields, notes | a login; with servers, the only category iOS AutoFill offers and the browser extension fills into login forms (addresses fill address forms, below) |
| `secure-note` | notes and custom fields; saving moves a username, password, website or one-time password into custom fields (Username, Password, Website, One-time password; secrets concealed), so a note never hides a secret its form does not show and nothing is lost | a notes-first view with Copy and a secret reference (`hisar://<vault>/<note>/notes`) |
| `credit-card` | the custom fields Cardholder, Type, Card number, Expiry (`MM/YY`), Security code, PIN, the billing address's Street, City, Region, Postal code and Country, and Billing address (a one-line address saved before cards had those), first and in that order, then other custom fields and notes; the number, security code and PIN are concealed; saving, and importing, move login data into custom fields as for a secure note | the brand and last four digits ("Visa •••• 4242"), the number and codes masked until revealed; lists show only the brand and last four digits |
| `identity` | the custom fields First name, Middle name, Last name, Birth date (`YYYY-MM-DD` when given year first), Email, Phone, Street, City, Region, Postal code, Country, Address (a one-line address saved before identities had those), Passport number, Driver's license number and National ID number, first and in that order, then other custom fields and notes; the document numbers are concealed; saving and importing move login data into custom fields as for a secure note | the full name, the document numbers masked until revealed; lists show the name, or the email |
| `api-credential` | the custom fields Service, Key ID, Secret, Host and Expiry (`YYYY-MM-DD` when given year first), first and in that order, then other custom fields and notes; the secret is concealed; saving and importing move login data into custom fields as for a secure note | the service and host, the secret masked until revealed, a warning once the expiry has passed; lists show the service and host |
| `address` | the custom fields Street (one or more lines), City, Region, Postal code and Country (two letters written in capitals), first and in that order, then other custom fields and notes; nothing is concealed; saving and importing move login data into custom fields as for a secure note | the address's values; lists show the city and country, or the street's first line |
| `software-license` | the custom fields Product, Version, License key, Licensed to, Email, Purchase date (`YYYY-MM-DD` when given year first) and Download URL, first and in that order, then other custom fields and notes; the license key is concealed; saving and importing move login data into custom fields as for a secure note | the version and licensed-to name, the license key masked until revealed, the download URL opened in the browser (http and https only); lists show the version and licensed-to name, or the email or product |
| `database` | its own username and password, as a login's, and the custom fields Engine, Host, Port, Database and Options, first and in that order, then other custom fields and notes; a known engine is stored by its ID (`postgresql`, `mysql`, `mariadb`, `sqlserver`, `oracle`, `mongodb`, `redis`; aliases such as `postgres` and `mssql` are accepted), any other as typed; a host typed with its scheme or port is split as a server's | the engine, host, port, database and options, the login fields and the connection string, masked until revealed (for another engine, a custom field named Connection string stands in); lists show the engine, address and database name |
| `server` | its own username, password, one-time password and websites (the admin console), as a login's, and the custom fields Host, Port and SSH key (the ID of an `ssh-key` item in the same vault), first and in that order, then other custom fields and notes; a host typed with its scheme or port (`ssh://build.example.com:2222`, `db.example.com:5432`) is split into host and port | the host and port, the login fields, the admin console opened in the browser and the linked SSH key, which opens; lists show the host, port and username; the extension and iOS AutoFill offer it on its admin console like a login |
| `ssh-key` | §9.6 | §9.6 |

- **Creating:** desktop **+** → Secure note, Credit card, Identity, API credential, Address, Software license or Server, iOS **+** → New secure note, New credit card, New identity, New API credential, New address, New software license or New server (or a vault's Add item), `hisar add --type secure-note|credit-card|identity|api-credential|address|software-license|server`. The backend refuses a category it does not know.
- **Credit cards** keep their values as named custom fields rather than a details section of their own, so an app that does not know cards shows them as fields and keeps them when it edits the item, and `hisar get -f "card number"` and `hisar://<vault>/<card>/security code` work as for any field. `core/item/card.go` holds the schema: `ReadCard`/`WriteCard` map a `Card` to the fields (names matched ignoring case), and `NormalizeCard` writes a known type the same way whatever its spelling ("amex", "mc"), fills an empty type in from the number, groups the number as printed on the card and writes the expiry as `MM/YY`; the desktop and iPhone editors apply the same formatting as the user types. `DescribeCard` names the brand from the number's first digits (Visa, Mastercard, American Express, Discover, Diners Club, JCB, UnionPay, Maestro, Troy) and warns about a failed Luhn check, an unreadable expiry or an expired card; the editors show the warnings, and a card saves anyway. The editors' type picker follows the number until the user picks a different type, and then hints when the two disagree. Bitwarden's `brand` and 1Password's card type are imported as the type. Card autofill in the extension is not part of this.
- **Identities** follow the same pattern (`core/item/identity.go`): `ReadIdentity`/`WriteIdentity` and `NormalizeIdentity`, which trims the values and writes a year-first birth date as `YYYY-MM-DD`, keeping any other form as typed because `04/12/1990` may mean April or December. The generic part, a record struct mapped to named fields (`NamedField`, `readRecord`, `writeRecord`), is `core/item/schema.go`; another type of this kind is a struct, its field list and a `Normalize` function. Identities have no warnings. An identity with a street, or two of city, region and postal code, is offered for address autofill in the browser extension.
- **Addresses** follow it too (`core/item/address.go`), and identities and cards keep their address in the same five fields: one helper builds the field list for all three, so the names and order match. `Address.Normalize` trims the values and each street line, drops empty lines and writes a two-letter country in capitals ("us" from 1Password), keeping a country name as typed. Before this, identities and cards kept one free-text "Address" or "Billing address"; that value stays a named field, shown as "(old format)" in the editors while it is set, never parsed or rewritten on its own; **Use as street** moves it into the street. An older app shows the five fields as custom fields and keeps them. Names match ignoring case, so a custom field called "City" on an identity becomes its city; the 1Password importer renames a Driver License's issuing `country` and `state` for that reason. `hisar add` reads the street a line at a time, ending with an empty line.
- **API credentials** follow it too (`core/item/apicredential.go`). Their fields are what a script needs, so they work as secret references: `hisar://Work/Stripe/secret`, `hisar://Work/Stripe/key%20ID`, `…/host`, which `hisar run` injects and masks like any field (§9.4.1). The expiry shares the birth date's normalizing (`NormalizeDate`), and `DescribeAPICredentialExpiry` warns once its day has passed in the device's time zone; an expiry in another form, such as "never", is kept and gets no warning. The item's "Copy secret reference" button names the secret.
- **Software licenses** follow it too (`core/item/license.go`): `hisar://Personal/Sketch/license%20key` resolves the key. The purchase date shares the birth date's normalizing. The download URL is a field, not the item's website, so the extension and iOS AutoFill never offer a license on that site; the apps open it in the browser when it is http or https, adding `https://` to one typed without a scheme. Search, in the apps and for coding agents, finds a license by product, version, licensed-to name and email, never by the key. A login with a "License key" field, which is how 1Password licenses were imported before, counts as a duplicate of a license with the same title and key, so importing the file again skips it. Licenses imported as logins before this type existed stay logins.
- **Databases** (`core/item/database.go`) keep their username and password as the item's own login data, as servers do, so `hisar://Work/Prod db/password` and `hisar get` work as for a login; they have no website and AutoFill and the extension never offer them. Their **connection string is never stored**: `Database.ConnectionString` builds it from the engine, host, port, database name and options and the item's username and password, so it always carries the current password. It is a URL, `<scheme>://<user>:<password>@<host>:<port>/<database>?<options>`, with the username and password escaped, an IPv6 host in brackets, and the port and parts left out when empty; the schemes are `postgresql`, `mysql` (MySQL and MariaDB), `sqlserver`, `oracle`, `mongodb` and `redis`, and SQL Server names the database in the query (`?database=…`). An engine Hisar does not know, or a database without a host, has no connection string. `connection string` is a built-in field for references, `hisar get -f` and Copy on a database, and coding-agent search lists its reference so agents never assemble one. Wherever no string is built (any other item, or a database with an engine Hisar does not know or no host) the label names a custom field instead, so a login imported with a "Connection string" field keeps it and a DB2 database can keep its JDBC URL in one; a built string always wins over such a field. Each named field resolves as `hisar://Work/Prod db/host` and so on, so `hisar run` can pass the whole string or its parts (§9.4.1). Lists and search show the engine, address and database name, coding agents search the host and database name, and summaries never carry the connection string or options. A 1Password database matches on import a login with its title and username, as such items were imported before this type existed; that login stays a login.
- **Servers** (`core/item/server.go`) keep their username and password as the item's own login data, so `hisar://Work/Build/password`, `hisar get`, coding-agent references and one-time passwords work as for a login, and their admin console is the item's website: `Item.OffersLoginFill` makes logins and servers the items the extension and iOS AutoFill offer, and a save offer on the admin console sees the server's credentials as already saved. Passkeys stay in logins only. The host, port and linked key are named fields; `hisar://Work/Build/host` resolves the host. The SSH key link holds the key item's ID and must name an SSH key of the server's own vault (`SaveItem` refuses anything else), so everyone who sees the server sees the key; a link whose key was deleted is kept so the server stays editable, and the apps show it as missing. The server's details name the key's title and fingerprint; share links leave the link out. Lists show the address (host and port) and username, and search matches the address, host, port and username; coding agents search the host. `hisar add --type server` links a key by its title. On import, a 1Password server's admin console becomes its website only when the console has no username or password of its own (or repeats the server's): a console with its own credentials stays custom fields, since as the website the extension would fill the server's password on it and a browser save there would overwrite that password. A server matches a login whose first website is its host and port on import, so a 1Password server imported as a login before this type existed is skipped; it stays a login.
- **Converting:** a login with nothing but notes and custom fields (`item.HasOnlyNotes`), such as one imported before secure notes existed, shows "Convert to secure note" (`app.Service.ConvertToSecureNote`, driven by `ItemDetail.canConvertToSecureNote`). Nothing is converted without the user asking.
- **Older apps** show a secure note as a login with only notes and keep its category when they edit it.

```
aad      = "hisar/v1/item" || vaultID || itemID || revision || part      (part ∈ {"overview","details"})
ct_part  = XChaCha20-Poly1305(key=K_vault, nonce=random24, aad, plaintext=JSON(part))
```

**One-time passwords (TOTP).** `details.oneTimePassword` holds an `otpauth://totp` URI, in the encrypted details part.

- **Saving:** a pasted setup key or link is normalized into the URI (`core/totp`): base32 secret, SHA-1/256/512, 6–8 digits, a period of 1–300 s. Anything else is refused.
- **Codes:** `core/totp` generates them per RFC 6238 and is tested against its Appendix B vectors.
- **Desktop:** `GetOneTimeCode` returns the code with its expiry, and the item shows it with a countdown and asks for the next one when it expires. `CopyField("one-time code")` copies it. Item lists only carry `hasOneTimePassword`, never the secret.
- **CLI:** `hisar get <item> -f code`, or the secret reference `hisar://<vault>/<item>/otp` with `hisar read` and `hisar run` (§9.4.1).
- **Browser extension:** `logins.code` returns only the code and its expiry, for a login that matches the page (§9.2). The extension fills two-factor fields and split code boxes from the inline menu, shows codes in the popup, and copies the code after filling a login that has one.
- **iOS:** the item shows the code with a countdown ring and copies it like other fields; the editor takes a setup key or link. The AutoFill extension offers one-time codes on iOS 18 and copies the code when it fills a password (§9.3).
- **Import:** one-time passwords from 1Password, Bitwarden and CSV exports become `details.oneTimePassword` (§9.7).

**Passkeys.** `details.passkeys` holds the item's WebAuthn credentials (§9.8): `[{credentialId, relyingPartyId, userHandle, userName, userDisplayName, privateKey, prfSecret?, createdAt}]`. Binary values are base64 in the JSON; `privateKey` is the PKCS#8 DER of an ES256 (P-256) key.

- The overview carries only `passkeyRelyingPartyIds`, the sites of the item's passkeys, so lists can mark an item as having a passkey without its details.
- The PRF secret of a passkey Hisar created is not stored; it is derived from `privateKey` (§9.8), so the stored fields are the same with or without PRF. A passkey imported through Credential Exchange (§9.7) keeps the exporting authenticator's hmac-secret credential (`credWithUV`) as `prfSecret`, and PRF uses it instead, so sites get the outputs they got before the import. Older apps ignore the field and would derive another secret.
- Editing an item never changes its passkeys: the backend copies them (and the overview marker) from the cached item, so editors that show passkeys read-only (desktop, iOS) or not at all keep them. Only "Delete passkey" and the WebAuthn signals (§9.8) remove one; the signals also update user names.

**Icons.** `overview.icon` is the icon the user chose (`core/item/icon.go`): `{preset, color}`, one of 30 preset IDs (`item.IconPresets`: bank, card, wallet, …, heart) in one of 10 colors (`item.IconColors`, or none for the neutral tile), or `{image}`, a PNG of at most 128 × 128 pixels and 24 KiB, which wins over a preset. Without one, an item shows its automatic icon: a login's website icon (below), else its category's icon or its initials.

- **Uploads:** `PrepareItemIcon` (`core/itemicon.NormalizeImage`) reads PNG, JPEG, GIF, WebP or ICO (its largest entry) up to 5 MiB and 4096 pixels a side, crops it to a centered square and scales it down (never up) to 128, 96 or 64 pixels until the PNG fits. SVG is refused. `SaveItem` refuses an image outside the limits, and `item.NormalizeIcon` drops unknown colors; an unknown preset ID, from a newer app, is kept and drawn as the category icon.
- **Size:** 24 KiB is about 32 KiB of base64, inside the server's 64 KiB overview limit, so the server is unchanged. The icon is in the overview, so lists draw it without the details, it syncs, works offline, and export, **Duplicate** and **Move** carry it.
- **Older apps** decode the overview into a struct without `icon` and drop it when they save the item; nothing else breaks. The Mac's and iPhone's preset lists are checked against core's by a test (`mobile/hisarkit/iconpresets_test.go`).

**Website icons.** A login (no category, or `login`) without an icon of its own shows its first https website's icon. It is never stored in the item, so the app never writes an item the user did not edit (no new revisions, no conflicts, and it works in vaults the user only reads), and it never reaches the server.

- **Fetching** (`core/itemicon.Fetcher`): the device asks the website itself, never the Hisar server or a third-party icon service. It reads `https://<domain>/` (at most 512 KiB), takes the `apple-touch-icon` and `icon` links of the head, largest declared size first, skips SVG and `data:` links, and falls back to `/favicon.ico`; at most four images of at most 1 MiB each, 5 s per request and 10 s in all, three https-only redirects, no cookies and the User-Agent `Hisar`. The image goes through `NormalizeImage`.
- **Only public websites:** the domain must be a public DNS name on port 443: IP literals, `localhost`, single-label names and `.local`, `.internal`, `.lan`, `.home.arpa` are refused, and the dialer refuses any address that resolves to loopback, private, link-local, CGNAT or multicast space, judging NAT64 (`64:ff9b::/96`) and 6to4 (`2002::/16`) addresses by the IPv4 address they carry and refusing local-use NAT64 and Teredo outright, so a login for a router or an intranet console, or a name rebound to one, never makes the app connect into the local network. No proxy is used, so the address checked is the website's.
- **Cache** (`app.GetWebsiteIcon`): each device keeps `{image, fetchedAt}` per domain in the offline cache's `website_icons` table, sealed with keys derived from K_user (`crypt.DeriveWebsiteIconKeys`, HKDF info `hisar/v1/website-icon-cache` with the account ID as salt): the record is XChaCha20-Poly1305 under AAD `hisar/v1/website-icon || lookup`, and its key `lookup` is HMAC-SHA256 of the domain, so the cache file names no website. An icon is asked again after 30 days, a website without one after 7, and a failed fetch (offline, a timeout) not again in that session for an hour. Concurrent requests for a domain share one fetch. The keys are forgotten on lock; the cache file goes with the rest on sign-out.
- **Setting:** **Show website icons** (Settings → Appearance on every app, a device setting, `websiteIconsDisabled` in the state file, on by default). Turning it off forgets the cached icons. While locked, or for anything but a public https website, `GetWebsiteIcon` returns nothing.
  An icon request retains the fetch context authorized while the session is unlocked and icons are enabled. It checks that context before fetching and before caching or returning the result. Turning icons off cancels that context and separates later requests from pending fetches, even if icons are enabled again before a fetch finishes.
- **Privacy:** fetching tells each website, from the device's IP address, that someone is looking at an item for it (§2.3); that is the price of the feature, and the switch turns it off.

Binding vault ID, item ID and revision in the AAD stops a malicious server from moving an item into another vault, swapping overview/details between items, or mixing parts of different revisions.

All `||` concatenations of variable-length fields are length-prefixed (4-byte big-endian length before each field), so field boundaries are unambiguous.

### 4.6 Attachments

Each file gets a random `K_file`. The file is chunked (64 KiB chunks) and each chunk is encrypted with `K_file`. The chunk index and an is-last flag go into the AAD. `K_file` is stored inside the item's details, so it inherits the vault key.

### 4.7 Favorites (the account preferences record)

Users star items, and **Favorites** at the top of the sidebar (Mac) and of the Vaults screen (iPhone) lists them across every vault.

**Decision: favorites are per user, synced across the user's own devices, and end-to-end encrypted.** Starring an item in a shared vault must not change the sidebars of the other people in the vault, so a favorite is not a field of the item overview. A list kept only on the device would not carry from the Mac to the iPhone, so it is not device state either. It lives in the account's own encrypted preferences record, which no one else can read.

- **Record.** One per account: `JSON({"favoriteItemIds": [itemID, …]})`, sealed with `K_user` (§4.3), which survives master password and Secret Key changes (§5.7):

  ```
  aad = "hisar/v1/account-preferences" || accountID
  ct  = XChaCha20-Poly1305(key=K_user, nonce=random24, aad, plaintext=JSON(preferences))
  ```

  Keys a newer app adds are kept, as they are, by an older app that saves the record. The server stores `(account_id, revision, ciphertext)` in `account_preferences` and never learns which items are favorites; deleting the account deletes the row.
- **Item IDs only.** A favorite names the item ID, not the vault: moving items to another vault keeps their IDs (§5.10), so a moved favorite stays a favorite.
- **Sync.** `GET /account/preferences` returns `{revision, ciphertext}` (revision 0 and no ciphertext before the first save). `PUT /account/preferences {baseRevision, ciphertext}` stores revision `baseRevision + 1` and answers `{revision}`, or 409 when another device saved in between; the record is at most 1 MiB. A save pushes `account-preferences-changed` to the account's event streams (§7.2), and the apps sync and refresh as for `vault-changed`.
- **Merging.** `session.UpdateFavorites` sends a change, the items to star and to unstar, on top of the record the device last read. On 409 it reads the record again and applies the same change to it, up to 5 times, so two devices that star different items at the same time keep both, and one device unstarring an item while another stars a different one keeps both changes. For the same item, the last save wins. A record that fails authentication, is too short, or has an unsupported envelope version never blocks a save. The save seals the record the device last read, with the change, over it. Writers must keep this record in a v1 envelope and put new data in new plaintext JSON fields. A future envelope change needs a new record or a minimum-app-version gate before any client writes it, because older apps replace records they cannot open. Local key-size errors and JSON errors in authenticated plaintext remain hard errors.
- **Gone items.** Favorites of items that were deleted, are in a vault the user left or lost, or no longer exist anywhere are left out of every list and never cause an error. They are dropped from the record lazily: the next time the Mac or the iPhone saves a favorite, it first syncs every vault and removes the IDs it found in none of them (not while offline).
- **Offline.** The record is cached with the account's other encrypted records (§9.1), so favorites show offline; starring needs the server, like other changes.
- **Apps.** `core/app`: `ItemDetail.isFavorite` on every item and summary, `ListFavoriteItems` (the starred items of every vault with each one's vault, without secrets) and `SetItemFavorite`. Readers can star items too: a favorite changes nothing in the vault. The Mac, the iPhone and `hisar` show favorites as described in §9.1, §9.3 and §9.4.
- **Import.** Favorites the other password manager marked become the importing user's favorites (§9.7).
- **Export.** The export (§9.11) holds vaults and items only, so favorites are not exported; the record stays with the account.

## 5. Accounts and devices

### 5.1 Invites

Sign-up is invite-only: there is no open registration. An invite is an email address plus a one-time token (the server stores its SHA-256 hash in `invites`), valid for 7 days. The sign-up link is `https://<HISAR_PUBLIC_URL host>/invite/<token>` (`http` only for a loopback host, in development); the token alone is the invite code the apps accept.

- **Any signed-in user** invites a person by email with `POST /invites {email}`. The server emails the invite itself (§8.5); the response is always `202 {"message": "Invitation sent"}` and never contains the link or the code. When the email already has an account, nothing is created or sent and the answer is the same, so invites do not reveal who has an account. Invites from users always create `member` accounts.
- Without email configured on the server (§8.4), `POST /invites` answers `501 email is not configured on this server`.
- Rate limits: 10 invites per account and 20 per IP address per day.
- **The operator** creates invites with `hisar-server invite --email … [--role admin|member]`, which prints the link and needs no email. The audit log records `operator_invite` with the email and role and no account.
- **Admins** manage invites in the admin dashboard's **Invites**, whatever the invite policy (§8.3). `GET /admin/invites` lists the pending invites with an email, newest first, each with the account that sent it (`null` for the operator's); an invite is pending until it is used or expires, one rule in `inviteState`. Its `id` is the hex of the stored token hash, so it names the invite without being its code. `POST /admin/invites {email, role}` creates an `admin` or `member` invite and, unlike `POST /invites`, answers 409 "An account with this email already exists." because admins can see accounts. `DELETE /admin/invites/{id}` deletes a pending invite, whose link then shows the invalid-invite page. `POST /admin/invites/{id}/resend` replaces it in one transaction with a fresh one for the same email and role, sent by the admin, so the old link stops working. Create and resend answer `{invite, link, emailed, emailError}`: the link appears only in that answer, for the admin to copy, and the server emails it at once when email is set up, reporting an SMTP failure instead of failing the request. The audit log records `admin_invite_created`, `admin_invite_revoked` and `admin_invite_resent` with the email and role. The setup invite is never listed, revoked or resent.
- **The setup invite** creates the first admin from the browser. When `hisar-server serve` starts with no account, it deletes any unused setup invite, creates a new admin invite without an email and logs one line, `Open <HISAR_PUBLIC_URL>/admin/setup#<token> to create the first admin.` The token sits in the URL fragment, so it never reaches the access log or a `Referer` header; a restart replaces it. Whoever holds it signs up with the email they enter. Once the first account exists, from the setup page or from `hisar-server invite`, the setup invite is deleted, so it cannot make a second admin.

**Invite page.** `GET /invite/{code}` (the link in the email) is a page rendered by `hisar-server` from an embedded template, in the share link page's style (§5.9), light and dark with the system, and usable at phone width.

- **Valid invite:** who invited the person (the inviter's name and email, which the email already told them; operator invites name nobody), the address the invite is for, and when it expires. Then **Download for Mac** (`https://tryhisar.com/download`) and **Download for Linux** (`https://tryhisar.com/download#linux`), **Open in Hisar** (`hisar://invite/<code>?server=<public origin>`), the invite code with **Copy**, and for the terminal how to get `hisar` (on a Mac it comes with the app: Settings → Developer tools; on Linux the Hisar package installs it; on a server, the download page) and `hisar signup <invite link>` with **Copy**. The copy buttons appear only with JavaScript; without it the code is still selectable text.
- **Used, expired or unknown code, or a malformed one:** one page, "This invite isn't valid", with the same status (404) and bytes, so the page tells nobody whether a code or an email exists. The lookup is one query whatever the state. Nothing on the page lists or looks up accounts.
- **Headers:** the share link page's set. The style and script are inlined and allowed by their SHA-256 hashes (`default-src 'none'; script-src 'sha256-…'; style-src 'sha256-…'; img-src data:; base-uri 'none'; form-action 'none'; frame-ancestors 'none'`); there are no inline style attributes or event handlers. `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer` keeps the code out of the download site's logs; `Cache-Control: no-store`, `X-Robots-Tag: noindex, nofollow`. Rendering escapes every value from the database, and the style and script are put in only around the rendered data, so a name can never become either.
- **Rate limit:** 30 page loads per IP address, refilled at one every 2 seconds (429 with a plain-text page), like the public share link reads. Codes are 256 random bits, so the limit protects the server, not the codes.
- **Opening the apps.** The Mac (`CFBundleURLTypes` in `Info.plist`, Wails' `ApplicationLaunchedWithUrl` event) and the iPhone (the `hisar` scheme it already registers for the Emergency Kit's `hisar://sign-in`) handle `hisar://invite/<code>?server=<origin>`: signed out, they open Create account with the invite filled in, as the bare code for `api.tryhisar.com` or as `<server>/invite/<code>` for another server, which the form then shows ("Server: hisar.example.com"). Signed in or locked, they say to sign out first instead. Universal links (`https://…/invite/…` opening the app directly) wait for the Apple Developer membership (§12 #7).

**Roles.** `accounts.role` is `admin` or `member`. In the apps both are the same: any user invites (unless the server's invite policy says admins only, §8.3), shares and manages their own devices, and the apps neither show the role nor offer admin screens. Admins manage the server from the admin dashboard at `/admin`, and the operator's `hisar-server` command (`invite`, `migrate`) stays available on the server.

**Admin dashboard.** `hisar-server` embeds and serves a Vue app (`admin/`). An admin signs in with their email, Secret Key and master password: a Web Worker runs `hisar-admin-wasm`, `core/session` compiled to WebAssembly, so neither secret leaves the tab and the two-second key derivation never blocks the page. The sign-in creates a device named "Admin dashboard" (platform `web/admin`); the tab keeps only its session token, account ID, device ID and the admin's name in `sessionStorage`, so a reload stays signed in and a new tab asks again, and **Sign out** revokes that device. An account that is not an admin has its new device revoked at once and sees "This account is not an admin on the Hisar server." The server decides, never the dashboard: every `/api/v1/admin` route runs `requireSession` and then `requireAdmin`, and answers 403 `admin only` to any account that is not an admin (§8.2). An admin path with no route is 404 for everyone, like any other unknown path. Changing another account's role and deleting another account also need a fresh proof of the admin's own master password (below).

- **Page:** `GET /admin` and every path below it answer `index.html` with `Cache-Control: no-store`. The hashed files under `/admin/assets/` are cached for a year (`immutable`) and sent as the brotli or gzip copy the build wrote when the browser accepts it; the WebAssembly is `application/wasm`. A server built without the dashboard (a plain `go build`) answers 404 "The admin dashboard was not built into this server."; `make build` and the Docker image build it.
- **Headers:** `Content-Security-Policy: default-src 'none'; script-src 'self' 'wasm-unsafe-eval'; style-src 'self'; connect-src 'self'; img-src 'self' data:; worker-src 'self'; base-uri 'none'; form-action 'none'; frame-ancestors 'none'` (`'wasm-unsafe-eval'` compiles the WebAssembly and allows no `eval`), `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer`, `X-Robots-Tag: noindex, nofollow` and `Cross-Origin-Opener-Policy: same-origin`.

**Accounts.** **Accounts** lists every account by email, 50 at a time (`GET /api/v1/admin/accounts?query=&cursor=`, keyset paging on the email; `query` keeps the accounts whose email or name contains it, ignoring case; a `query` or `cursor` longer than 320 characters, not UTF-8 or with a NUL character is 400): email, name, role, `state` (`active` or `suspended`), sign-up date, last activity (the latest `last_seen_at` of its devices), and how many devices are signed in and how many vaults it can open. **Account** (`GET /api/v1/admin/accounts/{id}`) adds every device the account signed in with, revoked ones included. The dashboard never sees vault names, items or keys, and the list carries no credentials. An admin acts on another account with:

- **Suspend…** (`POST …/{id}/suspend`): sets `accounts.suspended_at`, closes the account's event streams and drops its phone approvals. Its devices and sessions stay: every request of such a session, and the event stream, answers 403 "This account is suspended. Contact the admin of the Hisar server." (`apitypes.AccountSuspendedMessage`, which `apiclient` turns into `ErrAccountSuspended` and never confuses with another 403), and the heartbeat ends a stream that is still open. The Mac and iPhone apps lock on that answer and show the sentence when unlocking, keeping the Secret Key, the state and the local cache (never the sign-out of §5.7); `hisar` prints it and keeps its state. After reactivation the same devices work again with the same sessions, without the Emergency Kit; **Sign out everywhere…** is the separate action that removes them. Its share links stop opening and answer as if they were gone until the account is reactivated (§5.9). Its vaults, organization memberships and team access stay as they are, so its vaults keep working for everyone else. Signing in again with the right credentials answers the same 403 only after the OPAQUE proof, so the state is never shown to someone without the password; a wrong password gets the ordinary answer. The new session is created under a shared lock on the account row, so a suspension that commits meanwhile waits for it and then refuses it like the others. **Reactivate** (`POST …/reactivate`) clears the column; the account's devices reach the server again, and its share links that have not expired open again. Suspending a suspended account and reactivating an active one change nothing and add no audit event. The dashboard shows a suspended account's signed-in devices as Suspended, not Active.
- **Change role…** (`PUT …/{id}/role {role, proof}`) makes the account `admin` or `member`; the next request of each of its sessions uses the new role.
- **Sign out everywhere…** (`POST …/{id}/sign-out`) revokes every device of the account and closes its streams; **Sign out** on one device (`DELETE …/{id}/devices/{device}`) revokes that device and closes the streams of its sessions.
- **Delete account…** (`POST …/{id}/delete {email, proof}`, the account's email typed in, 400 otherwise) deletes the account exactly as its own deletion does (§5.7.1), organizations included, without its proof: the same transaction after the account row is locked, the same events, and the email "Your Hisar account was deleted", which says an admin of the Hisar server deleted it.

Role changes and deletions carry `proof`, a re-authentication of the admin (§5.7 step 3): `hisar-admin-wasm`'s `reauthenticate` runs `POST /account/reauthenticate` from the dashboard's session with the Secret Key and master password the dialog asks for, and the server accepts the proof once, from that session only (403 otherwise). An admin cannot suspend, delete or change the role of their own account (409 "Use Settings → Account in the Hisar app for your own account."), and no change made from the dashboard may leave the server without an active admin (409 "The Hisar server needs at least one admin."): suspension, deletion and demotion lock the account and every active admin in ID order, so two admins demoting each other at once cannot both succeed. Every change from the dashboard (suspend, reactivate, role change, sign-out of all or one device, deletion) checks in its own transaction, with the acting admin's and the account's rows locked in ID order, that the acting admin is still an active admin (403 "admin only" otherwise); a role change or deletion also runs the record and session checks of §5.7.1 on the admin's proof: 409 when the admin's master password or Secret Key changed after the proof, 401 when the admin's session ended. An admin who deletes their own account in the apps (§5.7.1) is not checked, so the operator then creates a new admin with `hisar-server invite --role admin`. The audit log records `admin_account_suspended`, `admin_account_reactivated`, `admin_role_changed` (with the role), `admin_signed_out`, `admin_device_revoked` and `admin_account_deleted`, each by the admin's account with `detail.account` set to the other account, and `login_refused_suspended` for a refused sign-in.

**Setup.** A fresh server is set up from `/admin/setup#<token>` in four steps, kept as `setupState` in the instance settings (§8.3) and never editable from Settings: `needsAdmin`, `needsChecks`, `needsInvitePolicy`, `done`. Each step's endpoint accepts only its own state and moves to the next; `done` is final, and a server that already had accounts when it first stored the state starts at `done`. `GET /api/v1/setup` answers the state to anyone, before sign-in; the other steps need the new admin's session.

1. **Create the first admin** (name, email, master password): `hisar-admin-wasm`'s `signUp` runs the sign-up of §5.2 with the setup invite as the "Admin dashboard" device and makes the Emergency Kit. The server moves to `needsChecks` when the first account exists, whichever invite created it. A used or replaced invite shows "This setup link is no longer valid. Use the link in the latest server log." The page reads the invite again when the fragment changes, so pasting the latest setup URL into the same tab works without a reload.
2. **Save your Secret Key**: the Secret Key and **Download Emergency Kit**; **Continue** waits for the download and "I saved my Emergency Kit". Once the account exists, the page shows the Secret Key whatever failed after it: when the kit fails, it asks the admin to write the key down; when the Personal vault could not be created (`signUp` answers `personalVaultError`, `session.SignUp` returning the session with the error), it says so and that a vault can be created later from the apps. The key exists only in the page, so leaving or reloading it asks first until the kit is confirmed.
3. **Check the server**: the instance checks of §8.8; **Continue** goes on past warnings and never past a failed check, and the server checks again before it moves to `needsInvitePolicy`.
4. **Choose who can invite people** (**Anyone with an account** or **Admins only**), saved as **Settings → Sign-up** saves it, and setup is `done`. The dashboard opens on **Overview**, signed in.

Opening `/admin/setup` later shows the step the server is at to its signed-in admin, the sign-in screen before it, and once setup is `done` the dashboard itself, never the account form.

### 5.2 Signup (new user, first device)

1. Client generates the Secret Key, salt and keyset, then derives AUK and `authKey`.
2. OPAQUE registration with `authKey`.
3. Client uploads salt, Argon2 params, `encKeyset`, public keys, signature.
4. Client creates a **Personal** vault. Share invitations sent to the email before the account existed (§5.8) show up in the app to accept.
5. Client generates the Emergency Kit PDF and requires the user to confirm it was saved.
6. On a Mac with Touch ID (an iPhone with Face ID), the next screen asks "Unlock with Touch ID?" with **Turn on Touch ID** and **Not now**. One click turns it on (§5.4) without asking for the master password again: sign-up keeps a copy of AUK in memory (`session.SignUpKeepingAccountUnlockKey`) until the offer is answered, the app locks or signs out, or onboarding ends (`ForgetNewAccountSecretKey`), and only the Touch ID check is asked.

If a registration answer is lost, the client tries signing in with the generated credentials before declaring failure. Personal-vault creation, local state-save, and keychain-save failures still surface as errors. The base keychain-failure message includes the generated Secret Key and asks the user to write it down. Removing this message is held until a delivery screen is approved, because existing app bridges discard structured results on error. Personal-vault and state-save errors retain a structured partial result that those bridges may discard. Durable staging and an explicit incomplete-onboarding recovery flow remain unresolved.

The account form rates the master password with the same strength meter as the item editor (Weak, Fair, Good, Strong). It refuses fewer than 10 characters and passwords estimated below 28 bits (common words and patterns such as `password1234`), with "This password is too easy to guess. Add a few more words or characters."; a Weak password above that is allowed with a hint, because the Secret Key also protects the account. The server only checks the length.

The admin dashboard's setup page (§5.1) runs the same steps in its Web Worker as the "Admin dashboard" device. Its form refuses fewer than 10 characters and a repeated password that does not match; it has no strength meter.

### 5.3 Login on a new device

1. User enters email, Secret Key (or scans the Emergency Kit QR code) and master password. The server is `api.tryhisar.com` unless the user opens **Use a different server…**, which shows the Server field. A remembered or scanned server stays behind that button too, and the line "Signs in to <server>." names the server the form will use. Signing out (and a session the server ended) keeps only the server this device last signed in to (`lastServer` in the state file's device settings, which `State.Reset` fills from the account's server on every sign-out, so sessions from before the field existed keep theirs too; reported as the signed-out status's `server`), never the email, so signing back in goes to the same server, a self-hosted one included.
2. Client fetches salt and params, derives `authKey`, runs OPAQUE login and gets a session. A server answers an unknown email with a fake salt and record, so signing in to the wrong server fails like a wrong password; the error names the server asked ("incorrect email, master password or Secret Key for api.tryhisar.com; check that this is your account's server"). OPAQUE identities: client = account ID, server = the constant `"hisar"` (`core/pake`; `bytemare/opaque` v0.18.0 cannot decode key material with an empty identity). The server's OPAQUE key material and an HMAC key live in `hisar-secrets.json`, created on first start.
3. Client downloads `encKeyset` and decrypts it with AUK.
4. The device registers (name, platform, public device key). Other devices see "new device signed in".

### 5.4 Unlock (known device)

The device already has the Secret Key in the keychain and a local copy of `encKeyset` (the desktop cache, §9.1). Unlock works offline: derive AUK, decrypt the keyset. Online, the client takes the account record from the server and refreshes the cached copy.

**Touch ID (macOS, MVP).** Target design, once the app carries a provisioning profile with a keychain access group (§12 #7): the desktop app stores AUK in a Keychain item protected by `SecAccessControl` with `kSecAccessControlBiometryCurrentSet` and `kSecAttrAccessibleWhenUnlockedThisDeviceOnly` (never synced to iCloud), so macOS itself releases it only after Touch ID, and re-enrolling fingerprints invalidates it.

**Interim implementation (login keychain).** Keychain items with biometric access control live in the data protection keychain, which a Mac app reaches only with a keychain access group from a provisioning profile, so until then:

- Enabling Touch ID requires the master password and a Touch ID check. The app derives AUK, generates a random 32-byte wrapping key, stores `Seal(wrappingKey, AUK, "hisar/v1/biometric-unlock:" || accountID)` in the local state file, and stores the wrapping key as a generic password in the login Keychain via `Security.framework` (so the item's ACL trusts the Hisar binary, not a shared CLI tool). The ACL names the app's designated requirement: releases are signed with the team's Developer ID Application identity (589NVZWDWZ), whose requirement stays the same across updates; an ad-hoc build's requirement is its code hash, so after every new ad-hoc build macOS asks for keychain access again.
- Unlocking with Touch ID: `LAContext.evaluatePolicy(.deviceOwnerAuthenticationWithBiometrics)` succeeds, then the app loads the wrapping key, opens AUK and decrypts the keyset. The fingerprint check is enforced by the app, not by the Keychain; this is the weaker part that the target design removes.
- If the sealed key no longer opens the keyset (master password changed elsewhere) or the Keychain item is missing, Touch ID unlock is turned off and the master password is required.
- The upgrade keeps the UI and the `Biometric` interface (`core/app`); only the storage in `desktop/biometric` changes.

Common to both:

- The master password is still required after every app launch and at least every 14 days (`LastPasswordUnlockAt` in the state file).
- **In-window prompt**: like 1Password, the lock screen embeds Apple's `LAAuthenticationView` (`LocalAuthenticationEmbeddedUI`, macOS 12+) in Hisar's own window instead of the system dialog. The UI measures a slot on the lock screen and the app places the native view over it; "Use master password" cancels the evaluation (`LAContext.invalidate`).
- Touch ID also approves SSH agent requests (§9.6). The iPhone unlocks with Face ID the same way, and Android with a strong biometric through `BiometricPrompt` (§9.3).

### 5.5 Sessions

- After OPAQUE, the server issues an opaque random session token (256 bits). The server stores only its SHA-256 hash, together with the device ID and expiry (30 days by default, sliding; the session lifetime of §8.3).
- All API calls use `Authorization: Bearer <token>` over TLS.
- Devices can be listed and revoked by their owner.

### 5.6 Public key authenticity

Sharing a vault means wrapping `K_vault` to the recipient's KEM public key, which the server delivers. A malicious server could substitute its own key. Mitigations:

- Each user's KEM public key is signed with their Ed25519 key, bound to their account ID.
- Clients pin (trust on first use) every other member's Ed25519 key in their local state when they first share with them, which is when the owner's app completes an accepted share invitation (§5.8; `localstate.State.CheckMemberPin`, used by the CLI and the desktop app). Completing a share, changing a member's role and key rotation stop if a pinned key changes; the desktop app also marks that person in its lists.
- The UI shows a short security code per person (Settings → People and the vault's sharing panel) that the two people can optionally compare once, in person or over another channel: the first 8 bytes of SHA-256 of their Ed25519 public key as 4 groups of 4 uppercase hex digits, e.g. `3F2A 91C0 7B44 E1D8` (`crypt.FormatKeyFingerprint`).

### 5.7 Changing the master password and replacing the Secret Key

Settings → Security on the Mac and the iPhone has **Change master password…** and **Replace Secret Key…**; the CLI has `hisar account change-password` and `hisar account replace-secret-key [-o file]`. Both need the current master password and a connection; the server never sees either secret.

1. The client fetches the account (`GET /account`), derives the current AUK and `authKey` from the current master password and Secret Key, and decrypts the keyset; a wrong password fails here, before anything is sent. The keyset's public keys must match the account's and the open session's.
2. It picks a new random salt, the Argon2 parameters (the defaults, never below what the account already uses) and, for a replacement, a new Secret Key with the same account ID. It derives the new AUK and `authKey` and re-encrypts the **same** keyset with the new AUK (§4.3). `K_user`, the KEM and signing keys, every vault key and every item stay as they are, so shared vaults keep working and other people's pinned keys (§5.6) do not change.
3. **Re-authentication.** `POST /account/reauthenticate {ke1, registrationRequest}` (session; per-IP auth limit and 5 per account, refilled every 30 s) starts an OPAQUE login against the current record with the current `authKey` and, in the same call, answers the OPAQUE registration request for the new `authKey`. The server keeps the login state for one minute, bound to the session that started it and to a SHA-256 of the record it was checked against; it can never be finished on `/auth/login/finish`, so it never creates a session.
4. `PUT /account/credentials {reauthenticationId, ke3, change, record, salt, argon2, encKeyset}` (`change` is `master-password` or `secret-key`). A wrong proof answers 403 (never 401, which clients read as "signed out") and is audited as `reauthentication_failed`. In one transaction the server locks the account row, checks that the record is still the one the proof was made against (409 otherwise: changed on another device meanwhile) and that the session is still live, replaces the record, salt, parameters and `encKeyset`, revokes every other device and deletes their sessions. It closes their event streams at once, records `credentials_changed` and emails the account: "Your Hisar master password was changed" or "Your Hisar Secret Key was replaced", with the number of devices signed out and no secrets.
5. The client updates its cached account record; after a replacement it stores the new Secret Key in the keychain and keeps it in memory, like at sign-up (§5.2), until the new Emergency Kit is saved or Hisar locks. Touch ID / Face ID unlock is sealed again with the new AUK (§5.4), since the user just typed the master password.

If the cached account cannot be replaced after a committed credential change, the app detaches and deletes that cache before attempting a fresh verified attachment. Failed deletion, refresh, or local state save is reported in the successful result's `localError` warning, alongside any `keychainError`. Returning a method error would make the desktop and HisarKit bridges discard the committed result and its replacement Secret Key. Finalization retains the new key in memory, attempts keychain storage, and saves state. The existing replacement screen still shows the new key and Emergency Kit actions, with the warning. A local failure does not roll back the committed remote change.

A lost credential-change answer is treated as committed only when the server holds the exact newly generated encrypted keyset. The lost answer's signed-out-device count remains unknown. Session and app results use -1 in the existing integer field, and the CLI and app display helpers say the count could not be recovered instead of reporting zero.

**Other devices.** Every other device of the account is signed out by the change, so no device keeps working with the old secrets. The apps say so before the user confirms: "Your other devices will be signed out. Sign in on them with the new master password" (or "with your master password and the new Secret Key from the new Emergency Kit; the old Emergency Kit stops working"). A device learns it was signed out from a 401: its event stream ends at once, and an unlock or any request then gets 401. The desktop and iPhone backend then forgets the account on that device (`forgetLocalAccount`: the Secret Key and Touch ID key in the keychain, the encrypted cache, the session and pins in the state file) and shows the sign-in screen with "This device was signed out. If your master password or Secret Key was changed on another device, sign in with the new one." A device that stays offline can still be unlocked from its cache with the old password until it next reaches the server; that is the limit of an offline-first client.

Replacing the Secret Key does not replace the keyset: someone who already holds the old Secret Key, the master password and a copy of the old `encKeyset` (a device cache) can still open the keyset. Rotating the keyset itself would mean re-wrapping every vault key and re-pinning with every person; it is not part of this.

### 5.7.1 Deleting an account

Mac Settings → Account → **Delete account…**, iPhone Settings → Account → **Delete account** (App Store guideline 5.1.1(v)) and `hisar account delete` (interactive; `--email` for scripts).

- **Before confirming** the app shows `GET /account/deletion-preview`: the vaults the user owns with no other owner, which are deleted with their items, and for each how many people lose access; the vaults with another owner (or owned by someone else), where only the user's access is removed and the vault stays; the live share links that stop working; the invitations sent and received that are withdrawn; and the devices that are signed out. Vault names are encrypted, so the server lists IDs and types and the client names them. The dialog recommends exporting first (§9.11): "Export your items first in Settings → Export." on the Mac, "… in Settings → Account → Export data." on the iPhone, and `hisar export` in the CLI.
- **Confirming** needs the master password, proved to the server with the same OPAQUE re-authentication as §5.7 (`POST /account/reauthenticate {ke1}`), and the account's email typed in. `POST /account/delete {reauthenticationId, ke3, email}`: a different email is 400, a wrong proof 403.
- **Server.** One transaction, after the same record and session checks as §5.7: delete the vaults the user owns alone (their items, history, access and share invitations cascade), the share invitations the user sent, accepted or that were addressed to their email, the sign-up invites addressed to the email or sent by the user and not yet used, the share links, sessions, devices, the remaining vault access (vaults with another owner) and the account. Migration `00006_account_deletion` drops the foreign keys from `vaults.created_by`, `vault_access.granted_by` and `items.updated_by` to `accounts`, so vaults that stay with another owner may name the deleted account there, as `item_history.updated_by` already could. Phone approvals of the account are dropped from memory and its event streams closed.
- **Afterwards** the people who shared a vault with the user get `vaults-changed`, and the owners and recipients of the withdrawn invitations `share-invitations-changed`, so their apps drop the vault or the member at once (there is no other notice). The audit log records `account_deleted` with the account ID only, and the account's email gets "Your Hisar account was deleted", which also says the encrypted backups are kept 30 days and the audit log up to a year (§8.6).
- No key rotation happens for the shared vaults that stay: the deleted account can no longer fetch anything, and its devices are wiped. Organization vaults it held a key to are marked for rotation (§5.11).
- The app forgets the account like a sign-out (§5.7) and returns to sign-in; the iPhone also removes its AutoFill identities.
- **By an admin.** An admin of the Hisar server deletes another account from the admin dashboard (§5.1) with the account's email typed in and their own master password proved: the same transaction from the locked account row on, without the account's proof, the same events, and the email says an admin of the Hisar server deleted it. The audit log records `admin_account_deleted` by the admin instead of `account_deleted`.

### 5.8 Sharing a vault by email

Sharing is person to person: the owner of a shared vault types an email address and a role. Nobody can list the accounts on the server, and the server answers every share the same way, so sharing cannot be used to find out who has an account.

1. **Invite (owner).** `POST /vaults/{id}/share-invitations {email, role}` (an owner of a shared vault; personal vaults cannot be shared, and organization vaults only through their teams, §5.11) stores a share invitation (`share_invitations`: vault, inviter, email, role, state, expiry after 7 days; one per vault and email, and a repeat replaces the role and expiry and sets it back to pending) and answers `202 {"message": "Invitation sent"}`. After answering, in the background:
   - when the email has an account, the server sends that account `share-invitations-changed` (§7.2) and an email saying who shared a vault with them (vault names are encrypted, so it says "a vault");
   - otherwise it creates a sign-up invite for the email (§5.1) and emails it, saying a vault is waiting. Without email configured nothing is sent; the invitation still waits for an account with that email.
   - Only errors that depend on the owner's own data are reported: the email is the owner's own (`400`), or belongs to someone who already has access to the vault (`409`; the owner sees the vault's members anyway). Rate limits: 50 share invitations per account and 100 per IP address per day.
2. **Accept or decline (recipient).** `GET /share-invitations` returns `received`: the pending, unexpired invitations to the caller's email, with the inviter's name and email, the role and the vault ID. `POST /share-invitations/{id}/accept` marks the invitation accepted by the caller's account; nothing secret is published. `POST /share-invitations/{id}/decline` deletes it. Both notify the vault's owners with `share-invitations-changed`. Until the share is completed the recipient has no access. `GET /share-invitations` also returns `waiting`: the invitations the caller accepted that no owner's app has completed yet, with the inviter's name and email, the role, the vault ID and `acceptedAt`, so the recipient's app can say who it is waiting for and since when.
   - **Telling the owner.** An owner whose app is unlocked and online completes the share within seconds (step 3). Otherwise, with email configured, the server emails the inviter 2 minutes after the accept (`Config.ShareAcceptedEmailDelay`) if the invitation is still accepted with the same `accepted_at` and the inviter is still an owner of the vault: "<Name> accepted your invitation in Hisar", saying to open and unlock Hisar to finish sharing. The server cannot read vault names, so the email says "a vault". It goes only to the inviter, who already knows the recipient's address, and at most 50 such emails per inviter per day; the timer lives in memory, so a restart drops it (the apps still show the banner below).
3. **Complete (owner's app).** `GET /share-invitations` also returns `sent`: the invitations of every vault the caller owns, with `state` (`pending` or `accepted`), and for accepted ones the recipient's account ID, name, email and signed public keys (only there: never for pending invitations or for vaults the caller does not own). On every sync, and at once on `share-invitations-changed`, the owner's app (`session.CompleteAllAcceptedShare`) verifies each accepted recipient's signed KEM key, pins their signing key (§5.6), wraps the vault key exactly as for any other member (§4.4) and calls `PUT /vaults/{id}/access/{accountID}`. The server accepts a new member there only with an accepted invitation for that vault and account, deletes the invitation in the same transaction and sends the recipient `vaults-changed`. The app then lists the person with their security code to compare.
   - `sent` entries carry `acceptedAt` once accepted. While the owner's app is **locked** it cannot complete anything (the vault key needs the keyset), but the session token is in the local state, so the lock screens on the Mac and the iPhone read `GET /share-invitations` (`ListAllShareAwaitingUnlock`; quietly nothing when offline or signed out) and show "Ada accepted your invitation to a vault — unlock to finish sharing" for each accepted invitation. The vault name is encrypted and unknown while locked, so it is not shown there. Unlocking completes the shares at once; if one still fails (a changed key, §5.6), the vault list's banner says "Ada accepted your invitation to Work. Hisar couldn't finish sharing" with the reason.
   - The recipient's vault list shows one banner per `waiting` entry: "Waiting for Ada's Hisar to finish sharing · accepted 2 h ago"; it disappears when `vaults-changed` brings the vault.
4. **Cancel (owner).** `DELETE /share-invitations/{id}` (an owner of the vault) withdraws a pending or accepted invitation.

`PUT /vaults/{id}/access/{accountID}` otherwise only changes the role of an existing member, and a key rotation (§4.4) can only keep existing members, so no vault is ever shared without the recipient accepting.

**People.** `GET /members` returns the caller and every account that shares at least one vault with them, with signed public keys; it is the member source for sharing, role changes, removal and rotation. There is no list of every account anywhere (API, desktop, iOS, CLI, extension); only the operator's `hisar-server` reads the database directly. The desktop's Settings → People shows those people with their security codes, the invitations the user sent (Cancel) and received (Accept, Decline), and the invite form; a banner above the vault list shows received invitations.

### 5.9 Share links

A share link hands one item, or a piece of text, to anyone, including people without a Hisar account: `https://<server>/s/<id>#<key>`. Browsers never send the fragment, so the server stores only ciphertext it cannot read. Links expire and can be limited to a number of views.

**What can be shared.** The item's title and the fields the user picks: username, password, websites, notes and custom fields; by default the username and the password (those the item has). Never an SSH private key, a passkey or a one-time password secret: `core/sharelink` has no name for them and never copies them into a payload. A custom field whose value is an `otpauth://` URI is a one-time password secret too (a login that becomes another category keeps its one-time password in such a field, §4.5), so it is neither offered nor shared when asked for by name. A text share has a title and one text field. Attachments are not supported.

**Crypto** (`core/crypt/sharelink.go`, `core/sharelink`). The recipient's browser decrypts with WebCrypto, which has neither XChaCha20-Poly1305 nor Argon2id, so share links use AES-256-GCM and PBKDF2 instead of the primitives of §3. Each content key encrypts exactly one message, so a random 96-bit nonce is safe.

```
linkKey       = 32 random bytes                       (the URL fragment, base64url without padding)
contentKey    = HKDF-SHA256(ikm = linkKey, salt = "", info = "hisar/v1/share-link/content", len = 32)
with a passphrase:
passphraseKey = PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256(NFKD(passphrase), salt = random 16 bytes, iterations = 600 000, len = 32)
contentKey    = HKDF-SHA256(ikm = linkKey || passphraseKey, salt = "", info = "hisar/v1/share-link/content-with-passphrase", len = 32)
ciphertext    = 0x01 || nonce(12) || AES-256-GCM(contentKey, nonce, aad = "hisar/v1/share-link", JSON(payload))
payload       = {version: 1, title, fields: [{kind, label, value, isConcealed}]}
                kind ∈ {username, password, url, notes, custom, text}
```

- Without a passphrase the link alone opens it. With one, both are needed: a leaked link alone is not enough, and the server, which stores the salt but never sees the link key, cannot guess the passphrase offline. Someone holding the link and a copy of the server's row can guess the passphrase at the PBKDF2 cost, so the apps ask for at least 8 characters and say to send the passphrase another way.
- PBKDF2 rather than Argon2id: WebCrypto has PBKDF2 built in, while Argon2id would mean shipping a WebAssembly build inside the page. 600 000 iterations is OWASP's current advice for PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256. The server hands out the salt and the iteration count, so the page and `core/sharelink` refuse fewer than 600 000 or more than 10 000 000 iterations, and a salt that is not 16 bytes.
- The JSON payload is at most 48 KiB, so the ciphertext stays below the server's 64 KiB.
- The creator's lists show what each link shares without the server learning it: the creating client also uploads a label, `XChaCha20-Poly1305(K_user, aad = "hisar/v1/share-link-label" || accountID, JSON{title, vaultID, itemID, fieldLabels, isText, linkKey})` (§4.3), which only the creator's devices open. It holds the link key, so **Copy link** works later on any of the creator's devices; the passphrase is never stored.

**Server.**

| Endpoint | Body → result |
|---|---|
| `POST /share-links` (session) | `{ciphertext, encryptedLabel, expiresInSeconds, maxViews, passphrase: {salt, iterations} or null}` → 201 `{id, createdAt, expiresAt, maxViews, viewCount, hasPassphrase}`. `expiresInSeconds` is 3600, 86400, 604800 or 2592000 (1 hour, 1 day, 7 days, 30 days); `maxViews` is 1–100, or 0 for unlimited; the ciphertext is 1 byte to 64 KiB and the label at most 4 KiB; 400 otherwise |
| `GET /share-links` (session) | the caller's live links, newest first: `[{id, encryptedLabel, createdAt, expiresAt, maxViews, viewCount, hasPassphrase}]` |
| `DELETE /share-links/{id}` (session) | revokes: 204 for the caller's own link, 404 for anything else |
| `GET /public/share-links/{id}` (no session) | counts one view → `{ciphertext, passphrase, expiresAt, maxViews, viewCount}`; the last allowed view also deletes the link. 404 `{"error": "not found"}` for an ID that never existed or is malformed, for an expired, used-up or revoked link, and for a link of a suspended account (§5.1): the same status and body, so they cannot be told apart |

- IDs are 128 random bits chosen by the server, as 22 base64url characters.
- A view is counted by one `UPDATE share_links SET view_count = view_count + 1 WHERE id = $1 AND expires_at > now() AND (max_views = 0 OR view_count < max_views) RETURNING …`. Postgres re-checks the condition after taking the row lock, so concurrent opens never get more views than allowed. When that view was the last one, the same transaction deletes the row.
- An expired link is gone for readers at once; `hisar-server` deletes expired rows every 10 minutes. Revoking deletes the row.
- Table `share_links` (migration `00005_share_links`); deleting an account deletes its links.
- Rate limits: 100 new links per account per day and at most 500 live links per account (429); public reads 30 per IP address, refilled at one every 2 seconds (429). IDs cannot be guessed, so the read limit protects the server, not the links.
- The creator's event streams get `share-links-changed` (`{}`) when one of their links is created, viewed, used up or revoked, so lists refresh (§7.2).
