- **Framework.** Sparkle (pinned version and SHA-256 in `scripts/download-sparkle.sh`) is downloaded from its GitHub release at build time, never committed, and embedded in `Hisar.app/Contents/Frameworks`; the app links it with the rpath `@executable_path/../Frameworks` (Go build tag `sparkle`, set for every packaged macOS build; `wails3 dev` and other platforms build without it and report updates as unavailable). `scripts/sign-desktop-app.sh` signs Sparkle's helpers, the framework and then the app, inside out, with the hardened runtime and a timestamp when `HISAR_SIGNING_IDENTITY` names an identity, ad-hoc otherwise. Releases use the team's Developer ID Application identity and are notarized: the release workflow imports the identity from the repository secrets `MACOS_SIGNING_CERTIFICATE` (the base64 `.p12`) and `MACOS_SIGNING_CERTIFICATE_PASSWORD` into a keychain it deletes when the job ends, and `scripts/notarize-desktop.sh` notarizes and staples the app (before the DMG and the Sparkle zip are made from it) and then the signed DMG with the App Store Connect API key in `APPLE_NOTARY_KEY` (the base64 `.p8`), `APPLE_NOTARY_KEY_ID` and `APPLE_NOTARY_ISSUER_ID`. A release fails without them; a custom build without the certificate is ad-hoc and not notarized.
- **Trust.** Every update archive is signed with an EdDSA (Ed25519) key; `SUPublicEDKey` in `Info.plist` holds the public key, and with `SUVerifyUpdateBeforeExtraction` Sparkle checks the signature before it even unpacks the archive and refuses it if it does not verify; the unpacked app must also be code signed (Sparkle allows moving from ad-hoc to a team identity, not dropping the signature). The private key never enters the repository: the release workflow reads it from the repository secret `SPARKLE_PRIVATE_KEY` into a temporary file that it deletes when the job ends. The server only relays, so a compromised server can withhold updates or offer an older signed build (Sparkle never installs a lower `CFBundleVersion`) but cannot ship code.
- **Feed.** `Info.plist` has no `SUFeedURL`; the feed is `<server>/api/v1/desktop/updates/appcast.xml` of the signed-in server, and Sparkle sends the session's `Authorization: Bearer <token>` header with the appcast request and the download. While signed out, update checks are skipped (a manual check says to sign in). `SUEnableAutomaticChecks` is on with a daily `SUScheduledCheckInterval`; Settings → General → Updates shows the version, has **Check for updates** and an **Automatically check for updates** switch, stored in `desktop.json` (it survives signing out).
- **Versions.** Releases are `MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH`, each part at most 999. `CFBundleShortVersionString` is the version and `CFBundleVersion`, which Sparkle compares, is the build number `MAJOR × 1 000 000 + MINOR × 1 000 + PATCH` (0.2.1 → 2001, 1.0.0 → 1000000), so it grows with every release.
- **Pipeline.** A run of the workflow "Release" with the app `desktop` (README.md, "CI and releases") tags `desktop-<version>`, and its workflow "Release desktop" (GitHub-hosted macOS runner) builds the DMG and also `Hisar-<version>.zip` (`ditto -c -k --sequesterRsrc --keepParent`), signs the zip with Sparkle's `sign_update`, and writes `update.json`: `{version, buildNumber, archive, edSignature, length, minimumSystemVersion, releaseNotes, releaseNotesFormat ("markdown" or "plain-text"), publishedAt}`. The release notes are the tag's annotation message: the title and the changes since the previous release. The same run builds the Linux packages on amd64 and arm64 Linux runners (`scripts/package-desktop-linux.sh`, §9.1.3) with `SHA256SUMS-linux-<arch>`, and the Flatpak. None of them is in `update.json`, so the Mac update feed is unchanged. Once every build has finished, the run creates the GitHub Release of the tag as a prerelease, with all the files at once. The contract an update source reads:
  - Release: tag and release `desktop-<version>` in `tosunlab/hisar`, titled "Hisar <version>" ("… (custom build)" for a label).
  - Assets: `Hisar-<version>.dmg`, `Hisar-<version>.zip` (the signed Sparkle archive, the `archive` in `update.json`), and `update-unpublished.json` until the version is published.
  - Published: approving the run's Publish job renames `update-unpublished.json` to `update.json`, turns the prerelease into a release and marks it latest. A version is offered to apps only when its release has an asset named exactly `update.json`; a source should also skip draft and prerelease releases and tags with a label, and must not rely on GitHub's single "latest" release, which every app's publish moves.
  - A published release is never rebuilt: every release is a new tag, and the run refuses a tag whose release is no longer a prerelease, so `update.json` always matches its zip.
  A custom build (`desktop-1.2.3-label`) is only the DMG, gets the build number just below `1.2.3` so that release updates it, and can't be published (servers also ignore versions with a label).
- **Server.** The server reads the contract above from the GitHub Releases of `HISAR_DESKTOP_UPDATE_GITHUB_REPOSITORY` (default `tosunlab/hisar`) through the REST API, with `HISAR_DESKTOP_UPDATE_GITHUB_TOKEN` as a bearer token when set (§8.4). It lists every page of releases (up to 1000), keeps the `desktop-MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH` releases that are neither draft nor prerelease and have an `update.json` asset, reads `update.json` of the newest ones, and builds an appcast of the newest 5 valid releases (newest first, `sparkle:format` for the notes), cached for 10 minutes. Each page is asked with its `ETag` (`If-None-Match`), so an unchanged list costs no rate limit, and an `update.json` asset is read once (a new upload gets a new asset ID). When GitHub fails the server keeps serving the cached appcast and retries after a minute; when GitHub answers 403 or 429 for its rate limit, it waits for `Retry-After` or `X-RateLimit-Reset` (at most an hour) before asking again. Enclosure URLs point back to the server (`HISAR_PUBLIC_URL`), so the download also needs a session and apps never talk to GitHub. `GET /desktop/updates/{version}/{file}` accepts only a `MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH` version of a release in the appcast and that release's update archive (the `archive` its `update.json` names); the release's other assets are never served, since a private repository may hold files that are not for every account. It fetches the archive through the API (`Accept: application/octet-stream`, following the redirect to GitHub's storage) and streams it with its `Content-Length` without buffering it; the token is never forwarded to another host on redirects. Both endpoints have their own per-IP rate limit.

**Update notices (Linux).** Sparkle is macOS only, so the Linux build uses `updater.Notifier`: it reads the same signed-in appcast, compares the newest `sparkle:shortVersionString` with the version `scripts/package-desktop-linux.sh` stamped (`-X …/desktop/updater.stampedVersion`, compared with `core/appversion`; a development build has none and checks nothing), and Settings → General → Updates shows "Hisar X is available" with **Download**, which opens the `desktop-X` GitHub release. It installs nothing: the user installs the new package. "Automatically check for updates" checks a minute after launch and then daily. `UpdateStatus.isNotifyOnly`, `hasChecked`, `availableVersion` and `downloadURL` carry it to the UI.

#### 9.1.2 Staying available: open at login and the menu bar

`ssh`, `git` (push, fetch, commit signing) and `hisar` reach Hisar through `~/.hisar/agent.sock` and `~/.hisar/cli.sock`, which exist only while the app runs. An SSH client cannot start an app: with Hisar not running it fails at once (`connect to agent … Connection refused`, then `Permission denied (publickey)` or a signing error). So the app starts at login and keeps running when its window closes.

- **Open at login.** Settings → General → "Open Hisar at login", on by default. The first launch of a build with this switch registers the login item once and records that in `desktop.json` (`openAtLoginConfigured`). After that Hisar registers only when the user turns the switch on; turning it off unregisters it and records `openAtLoginDisabled`, so Hisar never registers itself again. Both are device preferences that sign-out keeps.
  - macOS 13 and later: `SMAppService.mainAppService` (ServiceManagement through cgo). Settings shows what the system reports, not only the switch: on; "needs your approval" (Hisar was turned off in System Settings → General → Login Items) with **Open Login Items** (`+[SMAppService openSystemSettingsLoginItems]`); or not registered (removed there; turning the switch off and on registers it again). A development build that is not inside `Hisar.app`, and macOS 12, show it as unavailable and never register.
  - Linux: an XDG autostart entry, `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/autostart/com.tryhisar.hisar.desktop` (`~/.config/autostart` by default), with `Exec=<the app's executable> --hidden`. It counts as on while that file exists without `Hidden=true` or `X-GNOME-Autostart-enabled=false`; off removes the file.
- **Hidden launch.** When the app is opened at login (macOS: the launch Apple event carries `keyAELaunchedAsLogInItem`; Linux: `--hidden`) or by `hisar` (`--hidden`, §9.4), it opens no window. The SSH agent, the CLI socket and the browser bridge run, Hisar is locked as after every start, and the first request opens the approval window, which unlocks it (§9.6).
- **Closing the window keeps Hisar running.** Closing the main window hides it; the sockets stay up and auto-lock works as before. The menu bar item (Wails system tray; the tower as a template image) has **Open Hisar**, **Lock** (**Unlock…** while locked and **Sign In…** while signed out, both open the window) and **Quit Hisar**. Clicking the Dock icon, opening Hisar.app again or, on Linux, starting the binary again shows the main window, never the hidden approval window; a second instance hands over to the running one (Wails single instance) instead of taking over the sockets.
- **Dock icon.** On macOS the app is an accessory app (no Dock icon, no app menu) while its main window is hidden and a regular app while it is shown; the activation policy follows every show and hide of the main window. The approval window shows without a Dock icon.
- **Quitting** is explicit: Hisar → Quit Hisar (⌘Q) or **Quit Hisar** in the menu bar item. Both stop the sockets. Sparkle's install-and-relaunch goes through the same terminate path (§9.1.1), and the relaunched app opens its window as after any normal launch.
- Linux desktops without a tray (GNOME without the AppIndicator extension) show no menu bar item; Hisar still keeps running, and starting it again shows the window.

#### 9.1.3 Linux

The Linux app is the same Wails app on GTK 4 and WebKitGTK 6.0, for amd64 and arm64, in three forms:

- **`.deb` and `.rpm`** (`scripts/package-desktop-linux.sh`, `desktop/build/linux/nfpm/nfpm.yaml`; Ubuntu 24.04+, Debian 13+, Fedora 40+): `/usr/bin/Hisar`, `/usr/bin/hisar`, `/usr/lib/hisar/hisar-browser-host`, a desktop entry and its icon.
- **AppImage** (the same script): the three binaries and an `AppRun`, using the system's GTK 4 and WebKitGTK 6.0, for distributions with those but no `.deb` or `.rpm`. It does not bundle WebKitGTK: WebKit starts its processes from the path it was built with, so a bundled copy would run the system's processes against its own library. `appimagetool` 1.9.1 and the type-2 runtime 20251108 are pinned and checked by SHA-256.
- **Flatpak** (`scripts/package-desktop-flatpak.sh`, `desktop/build/linux/flatpak/com.tryhisar.hisar.yml`): built in the GNOME 51 SDK, run on the GNOME 51 runtime from Flathub, and released as a single-file bundle (`flatpak install --user Hisar-….flatpak`). Its sandbox may use `~/.hisar`, `~/.ssh`, `~/.local/share/hisar`, `~/.local/bin`, `~/.config/autostart` and the Chromium browsers' directories under `~/.config`, and talk to the Secret Service, the idle monitors, the tray watcher and notifications.

Neither an AppImage (mounted at a new path each launch) nor a Flatpak (inside its sandbox) has files the rest of the system can run, so at every start the app copies the browser host and `hisar` to `~/.local/share/hisar/bin` with a `hisar-app` launcher that starts the package again (`desktop/portable`). The browser manifests, Settings → Command-line tool (which links `~/.local/bin/hisar` to the copy), the login item (`Exec=…/hisar-app --hidden`), and `hisar` and the browser host when they start the app all use those copies. A Flatpak's login item goes into the user's own `~/.config/autostart`, not its sandbox's.

CI installs the `.deb` and runs the AppImage, starts each hidden on Xvfb and checks that `hisar` and `ssh-add` reach it (`scripts/test-desktop-linux-package.sh`), and builds and installs the Flatpak in Flathub's builder image.

Works as on the Mac: the vault, the SSH agent on `~/.hisar/agent.sock` with approvals, `hisar` through the app (peer credentials with `SO_PEERCRED`, terminal sessions from `/proc`), `hisar run` and `hisar mcp`, the browser extension in Chromium-based browsers (manifests under `~/.config`; the host starts the app when it is not running), passkeys through the extension, phone approvals, open at login (XDG autostart, §9.1.2) and auto-lock on the session's idle time (§9.1, from GNOME's or the freedesktop idle monitor over D-Bus).

Differs:

- **No biometric unlock.** `desktop/biometric` has no Linux backend, so Hisar unlocks with the master password, and Settings says so. fprintd or polkit is a later option.
- **The Secret Key needs a Secret Service** on the session bus (GNOME Keyring, KWallet). Without one, signing in fails with `localstate.ErrNoSecretService`, which says to start one or set `HISAR_SECRET_KEY`.
- **Updates are announced, not installed** (§9.1.1, "Update notices"): the user installs the new package.
- **Approvals name programs, not apps.** Linux has no Dock apps or code signatures, so an approval shows the process chain (`ssh ← zsh`), an SSH session or `tmux`, without an app name or icon (§9.6).
- **The approval window is placed by the desktop**: GTK 4 has no way for an app to move its own window (and Wayland forbids it), so `windowplacement` does nothing on Linux.
- **No tray icon on GNOME without the AppIndicator extension** (§9.1.2): Hisar keeps running, and starting it again shows the window.
- **The Flatpak sees other programs as PID 0**, in another PID namespace: it cannot read a caller's terminal session or executable. So `hisar` through a Flatpak Hisar is approved for each connection on its own (a terminal approval does not carry over), SSH approvals show no program, and the browser socket serves a host it cannot see. Any of the user's processes outside the sandbox looks the same, so in the Flatpak the extension's pairing, which the user approves in Hisar, is the only check that the browser socket's caller is the extension; only a Flatpak Hisar accepts such a peer, and the other builds refuse one (a container, another sandbox). The `.deb`, `.rpm` and AppImage have none of these limits. The Flatpak owns no well-known bus names but its own: the tray registers with the StatusNotifierWatcher under its unique name.
- **Not covered:** browsers installed as a Flatpak or a Snap (their sandbox cannot run a host from outside it) and Firefox (the extension has no Firefox build).
- The UI says "this computer" and "system tray" where the Mac's says "this Mac" and "menu bar".

### 9.2 Browser extension — TypeScript, MV3

- No crypto and no keys in the extension. It talks to the desktop app through the browser's native messaging API; the desktop app must be running (the host starts it when it is not).
- Pairing: on first connect, the desktop shows a code that the user confirms in the extension. The native host manifest allows only our extension IDs.
- Autofill logins, and servers on their admin console, only when the page origin matches an item URL (below). Addresses belong to no site and are filled only on an explicit choice in the inline menu or popup (below). Never autofill automatically into cross-origin iframes. Fill on explicit user action by default.
- Detects new and changed logins and offers to save them.
- Chromium browsers first (Chrome, Brave, Edge, Arc, Vivaldi, Chromium), from one MV3 build. Firefox later from the same code; Safari needs a team-signed app (§12 #7). Distributed as an unlisted Chrome Web Store item.

**Extension ID.** The manifest's `key` holds the public key, so the ID is `mmipiffoogblblocokimpemdopmdjghf` both unpacked and from the store (the private key is uploaded once with the first store submission and is kept outside the repository). The native host manifest allows only `chrome-extension://mmipiffoogblblocokimpemdopmdjghf/`.

**Chain.** `extension (service worker) ⇄ hisar-browser-host ⇄ Hisar desktop app`:

- The browser starts `hisar-browser-host` (shipped in `Hisar.app/Contents/MacOS/`) with the caller's origin as its first argument and speaks native messaging over stdio: each message is a 32-bit length in native byte order followed by UTF-8 JSON. Hisar caps messages at 1 MiB in both directions.
- The host connects to the desktop app on `~/.hisar/browser.sock` (directory 0700, socket 0600) and relays frames unchanged, in the same framing. Its first frame is `{"type":"connect","extensionOrigin":"chrome-extension://…/"}`; the desktop refuses other origins. When the socket is missing, the host launches the app (macOS: `open -g -b com.tryhisar.hisar`; Linux: `Hisar --hidden` next to the host, else `/usr/bin/Hisar`, in a session of its own) and retries for 10 s, then answers each request with `desktop-unavailable`.
- When the packaged host exists next to the app binary, the desktop accepts only connections whose peer process is that executable (peer PID → executable path, as the SSH agent does).
- The desktop installs the host manifest `com.tryhisar.hisar.json` (`type: stdio`, `path`, `allowed_origins`) into `NativeMessagingHosts` of every installed Chromium browser at launch, and shows the result in Settings. On Linux these are the user data directories under `~/.config`. Browsers installed as a Flatpak or a Snap are not covered: their sandbox cannot run a host installed outside it. Firefox is not either: the extension has no Firefox build.

**Messages.** Requests are `{"id": "<uuid>", "type": "…", "token": "…", …parameters}`; responses are `{"id", "ok": true, "result": {…}}` or `{"id", "ok": false, "error": {"code", "message"}}`. Error codes: `invalid-request`, `not-paired`, `pairing-failed`, `signed-out`, `locked`, `offline`, `not-found`, `no-match`, `desktop-unavailable`, `update-required` (§8.7), `wrong-password` (`unlock.password`), `internal`. Every type except `hello`, `pair.start` and `pair.finish` requires the pairing `token`.

| Type | Parameters | Result |
|---|---|---|
| `hello` | `instanceID`, `browser`, `extensionVersion` | `protocolVersion` (1), `isPaired`, `state` (`signed-out`, `locked`, `unlocked`), `features` (what the app answers beyond the first version: `addresses`, `vault`, `unlock-password`, `cards`, `identities`, `save-options`, `create-items`; an older app sends fewer or none, and the extension then offers only what it announces) |
| `pair.start` | `instanceID`, `browser` | `{}`; the desktop window shows "Pair <browser>?" with a 6-digit code |
| `pair.finish` | `instanceID`, `code` | `token`. The code is valid for 2 minutes and 5 attempts |
| `status` | — | `state`, `email`, `isOffline` |
| `unlock` | — | `{}`; brings the desktop window forward (on the Touch ID screen when it is ready) |
| `unlock.password` | `password` | `state`, `email`, `isOffline`; unlocks with the master password typed in the popup. `wrong-password` for a wrong one; after 5 wrong ones within 5 minutes from one paired browser, that browser waits out the 5 minutes (an attempt counts before its password is checked, so attempts sent at once over several connections cannot pass the limit) |
| `logins.match` | `url` | `logins`: `[{vaultID, itemID, title, username, vaultName, hasOneTimePassword}]`, sorted by title |
| `logins.fill` | `vaultID`, `itemID`, `url` | `username`, `password`, `hasOneTimePassword`; the desktop checks the URL match again |
| `logins.code` | `vaultID`, `itemID`, `url` | `code`, `expiresAt` (RFC 3339), `period`; the same URL match as `logins.fill`. The secret never leaves the desktop; `not-found` when the item has no one-time password |
| `logins.check` | `url`, `username`, `password` | `status` (`new`, `changed`, `unchanged`) and, unless `new`, `vaultID`, `itemID`, `title` |
| `logins.save` | `url`, `username`, `password`, optional `vaultID` + `itemID`, optional `title` | `vaultID`, `itemID`. Without an item, creates a login titled `title`, else the site's host, in `vaultID` (refused unless the user may write to it), else the Personal vault; with one, updates its username and password |
| `vaults.list` | — | `vaults`: `[{vaultID, name, isPersonal}]`, the vaults the user may write to, the personal one first |
| `password.generate` | optional `length`, `digits`, `symbols` | `password` |
| `addresses.list` | — | `addresses`: `[{vaultID, itemID, title, summary, source, vaultName}]`: address items with any value, then identities with a fillable address (`source` `address` or `identity`), each sorted by title. `summary` is only the city and country; a card's billing address is not offered |
| `addresses.fill` | `vaultID`, `itemID` | `street` (lines separated by `\n`), `city`, `region`, `postalCode`, `country`; `not-found` for anything `addresses.list` does not offer |
| `identities.list` | — | `identities`: `[{vaultID, itemID, title, summary, vaultName}]`, identities with a name, email or phone, by title; `summary` is only the name and email |
| `identities.fill` | `vaultID`, `itemID` | `firstName`, `middleName`, `lastName`, `email`, `phone`, `birthDate`, and `address` (as `addresses.fill`) when it is fillable. Document numbers never leave the app |
| `cards.list` | — | `cards`: `[{vaultID, itemID, title, brand, last4, expiry, vaultName}]`, credit cards with a number, by title |
| `cards.fill` | `vaultID`, `itemID` | `cardholder`, `number` (digits only), `expiryMonth` (2 digits), `expiryYear` (4 digits), `securityCode`, `brand` |
| `items.list` | optional `query`, `category`, `isFavoriteOnly` | `items`: `[{vaultID, itemID, category, title, subtitle, vaultName, url, isFavorite, hasOneTimePassword}]` of every vault, by title; `query` matches what the app's search reads. Never a secret |
| `items.get` | `vaultID`, `itemID` | The summary and `fields`: `[{id, label, kind, value}]` with `kind` `text`, `secret`, `url`, `notes` or `one-time-code`; a secret's and a code's `value` is left out. An SSH key's private key and passkeys are never listed |
| `items.reveal` | `vaultID`, `itemID`, `fieldID` | `value` of that one field; for a one-time password, its current code |
| `items.code` | `vaultID`, `itemID` | As `logins.code`, for any item with a one-time password, without a URL |
| `items.icon` | `url` | `png` (base64) from the website icon cache, or nothing (§ website icons) |
| `items.favorite` | `vaultID`, `itemID`, `isFavorite` | `{}` |
| `items.create` | `vaultID`, `item` (`category` `login`, `secure-note`, `credit-card` or `identity`, `title` and that category's fields) | The new item's summary, as `items.list` gives it |

The desktop keeps paired browsers (`instanceID`, browser name, SHA-256 of the token, paired and last-used times) in its state file; Settings lists them with Remove. Signing out removes them.

**URL matching** (desktop, `core/browserprotocol`). Item URLs without a scheme are read as `https://`. A page matches an item URL when the ports match (default ports normalized) and the scheme is the same, or the item says `http` and the page is `https`. By default the hosts must share their registrable domain (eTLD+1 from the public suffix list, so `accounts.google.com` matches `google.com`, but two `github.io` sites do not); the Settings option "Only fill on the exact host" requires equal hosts. IP addresses and `localhost` always need equal hosts.

**Android app links** (`core/item/androidapp.go`, `core/app/androidapps.go`). A login or server can be linked to Android apps, so Android AutoFill offers it inside those apps. A link is the app's package name **and** the SHA-256 of its signing certificate, since a sideloaded app can take any package name. It is stored among the item's URLs as `androidapp://<package>?sha256=<64 lower-case hex>`: every app that predates links keeps the URL when it edits the login, and none fills it into a page, because only `http` and `https` URLs match pages. `ItemDetail.URLs` holds only the websites and `ItemDetail.AndroidApps` the links (the certificate as upper-case colon hex, as `apksigner` shows it); `SaveItem` keeps the stored links when the editor sends none, and `AddAndroidApp` ("Always use for this app") and `RemoveAndroidApp` change them. An `androidapp://` URL without a valid certificate, such as a Bitwarden import, stays a website and never matches an app. `ListLoginsForAndroidApp` offers only logins linked to exactly that package and certificate; Hisar never guesses a site from a package name. Android's Digital Asset Links check reads `ListLoginWebsites` (each https-reachable host of the logins' websites and its registrable domain), fetches that host's `assetlinks.json`, and fills an app the site vouches for through the URL matching above. The Mac and iPhone show links read-only in the item.

**Extension behaviour.**

- Popup: when not paired, the pairing flow; when locked, a master password field (with an app that announces `unlock-password`) and "Unlock in the Hisar app"; otherwise the vault (with an app that announces `vault`; an older one gets only the logins matching the current tab) and tabs for Cards, Identities (and addresses) and the password generator (Copy, Fill into the focused field).
  - The vault view searches every vault (`items.list`), with chips for All, Favorites, Logins, Cards, Identities and Notes. The logins matching the current tab are suggested above the rest, with Fill, Copy username and Copy password; logins filled most recently come first, in the same order the inline menu lists them and the fill shortcut takes the first of. A row opens the item: its fields, with secrets hidden until Reveal or Copy asks `items.reveal` for that one field, Open website (http and https only), a favorite star, its one-time code, and Fill when it matches the tab. New item creates a login for the page (its site and a generated password filled in), a secure note, a card or an identity, in a vault the user may write to.
  - The arrow keys move between the search box and the rows, Enter opens a row, Ctrl or Cmd+C on a login row copies its password, and Escape goes back. The popup follows the system's light or dark appearance. A login with a one-time password also shows its current code with a countdown ring and Copy; the popup asks `logins.code` again when the code expires. On a page with a one-time code field and no login form, Fill fills the code.
- Fill (popup, or the `fill-login` keyboard shortcut for the first match): the service worker asks the desktop with the frame's URL and fills only the top frame and frames with the top frame's origin; it never fills a frame that is cross-origin to the page, even when the item would match.
- Save: the content script records the username and password of a submitted login form and asks `logins.check`. For `new` or `changed` it shows an in-page bar (shadow DOM, top frame): Save / Update, Not now, Never for this site (stored per origin in `chrome.storage.local`). A form whose password field is a card's security code offers nothing. With an app that announces `save-options`, the bar for a new login has fields for its title, username and vault (`vaults.list`): vault names enter the bar's closed shadow root, and keys typed there stop at the bar, though a page listening in the capture phase still sees them (a title and a username are not secrets). The offer is kept per tab for 2 minutes, so a sign-in that moves to another page shows it there. The offer, and a first-step username remembered for a two-step sign-in, belong to the pairing they were made under: a browser paired again, as after another account signs in, never sends or uses them. The `logins.check`, `vaults.list` and `logins.save` requests that carry them go out only under that pairing's token, compared with the stored token as each frame is sent.
- Toolbar and menus: the toolbar icon counts the logins saved for the tab, asked only over a connection that is already open, so switching tabs never starts the host and Hisar. A right-click menu on editable fields shows Hisar's inline menu there, fills the first matching login, or fills a suggested password. Ctrl+Shift+Space (Cmd+Shift+Space on a Mac) opens the popup.
- Permissions: `nativeMessaging`, `storage`, `scripting`, `activeTab`, `tabs`, `contextMenus` (the right-click menu), `offscreen` and `clipboardWrite` (copying the one-time code after a fill, below), and host permissions for `<all_urls>` (the content script detects logins on every page). The extension stores only its `instanceID`, the token, the "never" list, the two inline menu settings, the features of the app's last `hello`, which content scripts read to offer address, card and identity filling only with an app that does it, and when each item was last filled (vault and item IDs and times, the latest 200), to put recently used logins first.
- Vault-wide requests (`items.*`, `vaults.list`, `items.create`, `unlock.password`) are answered by the service worker only to the extension's own pages (the popup), never to a content script; a page therefore never receives titles of other sites, or a secret it did not ask to fill. The app cannot tell a paired extension's pages apart, so this boundary is the extension's, as with addresses. The master password typed in the popup crosses native messaging to the app, which only the user's own processes reach from a paired browser; the extension keeps no copy, and the app derives the keys from it as its unlock window does and clears its bytes.
- Cards and identities: like addresses, they belong to no website, so the extension asks for one only when the user picks it in the inline menu or the popup, after the same visibility check, and fills only shown, enabled, empty fields of the page's own frames. A card form inside a payment provider's cross-origin frame (such as Stripe's) is therefore not filled.
- Clipboard: the popup copies with `navigator.clipboard.writeText`, which a focused extension page may do without a clipboard permission. A service worker has no clipboard, so the copy after a fill goes through an offscreen document (`offscreen.html`, reason `CLIPBOARD`) that copies with `execCommand("copy")`; that needs `clipboardWrite`, because no user gesture reaches the offscreen document. The offscreen page accepts copy messages only from the extension itself without a tab, so content scripts cannot use it. The extension does not clear the clipboard afterwards (the desktop app's own 90 s clearing of what it copies is unchanged).
- Frame rule in practice: a child frame counts as having the top frame's origin only when every frame between it and the top has that origin too (`location.ancestorOrigins`). The content script checks this again before filling.
- The service worker keeps one native port and reconnects on the next request after a disconnect, not on a timer, because each connection starts the host, and the host would relaunch Hisar after the user quit it.
- Inline menu: when a username or password field of a login form gets the focus, the content script puts a small Hisar icon inside the field's right edge, and opens a menu under the field on focus (setting "Open the menu when a login field is focused") or on a click on the icon or the down arrow.
  - Where it appears: only in frames that the frame rule could fill, never on hidden, tiny, read-only or disabled fields, and never on Hisar's own elements.
  - What it shows: up to 6 matching logins (title, username) and "More in Hisar…", which opens the popup. On the new-password fields of sign-up and change-password forms it shows "Use suggested password" (`password.generate`), which fills every new-password field. Otherwise it shows "Pair with Hisar", "Unlock Hisar" (`unlock`, then polls), "Open Hisar", or "No logins for this site".
  - The menu is an extension page (`inline-menu.html`) in an iframe inside a closed shadow root. Its URL comes from the service worker with the dynamic ID (`web_accessible_resources` with `use_dynamic_url`). Login titles and usernames therefore never enter the page's DOM.
  - The menu page talks to the service worker directly, under a per-field session that the first menu frame claims. The content script only positions the icon and the frame (fixed position, updated every animation frame, highest z-index) and fills the field the menu belongs to when the service worker asks. Fills take the same path as the popup's: the desktop checks the URL again, and the frame rule applies.
  - Clickjacking: before it asks the desktop for a password, the service worker asks the content script whether the menu has been visible and uncovered for 300 ms. The content script checks this on every frame: opacity, visibility, display and filters of the frame and its ancestors, its size and position, and `elementFromPoint` at the centre and corners. The menu page also ignores untrusted clicks and, where IntersectionObserver v2 exists, clicks before it has reported `isVisible` for 300 ms. That second check catches overlays with `pointer-events: none`.
  - Keyboard: the content script passes trusted Arrow and Enter key presses to the menu through the service worker; Enter is taken only after an arrow key selected an entry. Escape, typing in the field, a click outside, losing focus, the field leaving the viewport and `pagehide` close the menu.
  - One-time codes: a field gets the menu with "one-time code" purpose when it has `autocomplete="one-time-code"`, or its name, id, placeholder, aria-label, title or label says otp, totp, 2fa, mfa, one-time, two-factor, 2-step, authenticator, or an authentication, verification, security, SMS or login code (a bare "code", "token" or "pin" counts only with a maxlength of 4–10 or a numeric input mode). Coupon, promo, postal, phone, card and captcha fields never count. Four to eight visible one-character inputs (`maxlength=1`) close together with no other field among them are one split code when there are six of them, or they are numeric, or one of them is marked as a code; the icon then sits beside the last box and the menu spans the boxes. The menu lists only the matching logins with `hasOneTimePassword`; choosing one asks `logins.code` (after the same visibility check as passwords) and fills the field, or types one digit into each box, with input and change events. A code longer than the boxes is not filled.
  - After any fill of a login with `hasOneTimePassword` (popup, shortcut or inline menu), the service worker asks `logins.code` and copies the code to the clipboard, as 1Password does, so it can be pasted on the next page. A failed copy never fails the fill.
  - Addresses: a text field gets the menu with "address" purpose when it is not part of a login form and belongs to an address form: a form (or form-less container) with a field whose `autocomplete` names an address part (`street-address`, `address-line1`–`3`, `address-level1` and `2`, `postal-code`, `country`, `country-name`), or two kinds of address field found by their name, id, placeholder, aria-label or label (street, apartment, city, state or province, postal or ZIP code, country, in English and a few other languages; email, phone, company, search, coupon and web address fields never count). The menu lists up to 6 addresses from `addresses.list` with "More in Hisar…"; choosing one asks `addresses.fill` after the same visibility check as passwords and fills the form. The popup's **Addresses** tab lists them too, and its Fill looks for an address form (the focused one, else the top frame's, else a same-origin frame's) before it asks for the address, so a page without one never receives it. A fill touches only the address fields of the anchored field's group (an `autocomplete` `section-*`, `shipping` or `billing` token, or "billing" or "shipping" in the field's hints), only fields that are visible (not hidden, transparent or moved off the page, since a fill writes every empty field of the group and not only the chosen one), enabled, writable and empty, and lists whose option matches. The street goes whole into a `street-address` field (its lines kept in a textarea, joined with commas in an input), or a line into each `address-line` field with the rest in the last one; a country list matches the saved code or name in English or the browser's language (`Intl.DisplayNames`), and a region list its value or text. There is no URL check: a paired extension can read any address, the same trust it already has with `logins.fill`.
  - Cards: a field of a payment form (one with a card number field, by `autocomplete` `cc-number` or a hint such as "card number") gets the menu with "card" purpose, before it is read as a login, since a security code may be a password field. It lists up to 6 cards from `cards.list` (brand, last four digits, expiry); choosing one asks `cards.fill` and fills the number (into one field, or across split boxes), the name, the expiry (whole, as the field's placeholder or length asks, or month and year as fields or lists) and the security code.
  - Identities: a name, email, phone or birth date field (by `autocomplete`, input type or hints, never a username, company or card holder) gets the menu with "identity" purpose when it is not a login field and belongs to a form with personal details (two kinds of such field, or one named by `autocomplete`), on a checkout or a sign-up form's non-password fields. Choosing one of up to 6 identities from `identities.list` asks `identities.fill` and fills those fields and the address form beside them.
  - Settings in the popup: "Show Hisar in login fields" and "Open the menu when a login field is focused", both on by default and kept in `chrome.storage.local`.

### 9.3 Mobile — gomobile + native UI

- `gomobile bind` compiles `core/` into an iOS `.xcframework` and an Android `.aar`.
- Native UI: SwiftUI and Kotlin/Jetpack Compose.
- System autofill: the iOS AutoFill Credential Provider extension, a second process that shares the app's state and encrypted cache through the app group; Android's `AutofillService`, which runs in the app's own process on the app's backend.
- Memory constraint: the iOS AutoFill extension must stay well below the system limit (Argon2 64 MiB).

**iOS (Phase 4, first).** `mobile/hisarkit` wraps the app backend (`core/app`, shared with the Mac app, which has no Wails import and compiles for `ios/arm64`), so the phone gets the same sign-in, offline cache, matching and sharing logic. `make build-ios-framework` runs `gomobile bind -target=ios,iossimulator -iosversion=26.0` and writes `mobile/build/HisarKit.xcframework` (Swift module `HisarKit`, classes prefixed `Hisarkit`).

- gomobile passes only strings, numbers, `[]byte` and interfaces, so lists and items cross as JSON strings with the desktop's types (`vault.Status`, `VaultSummary`, `ItemDetail`, `browser.Login`).
- `HisarkitNew(stateDirectory, deviceName, secretKeyStore, biometric, clipboard, listener)` keeps `hisar.json` and `hisar-cache.db` in `stateDirectory`, the app group container, so the app and the AutoFill extension share the state and the encrypted cache.
- **Methods:** `Status`, `SignIn`, `SignUp` (returns the status and the new Secret Key), `Unlock`, `UnlockWithBiometric`, `EnableBiometricUnlock`, `DisableBiometricUnlock`, `Lock`, `SignOut`, `ReportActivity`, `ListAllVault`, `ListAllFavoriteItem` and `SetItemFavorite` (§4.7), `ListAllItem` (no secrets), `GetItem`, `SaveItem` (item JSON in, item JSON out), `DeleteItem`, `CopyField`, `GetOneTimeCode`, `GeneratePassword`, `ListAllLoginForService`, `GetLoginForService`, `GetOneTimeCodeForService`, for sharing (§5.8) `CreateVault` (always a shared vault), `ListAllPerson` (the people the user shares a vault with), `InvitePerson` (the server emails a sign-up invite), `ListAllVaultMember`, `ShareVault` (sends a share invitation), `ListAllShareInvitation` (completes accepted shares first, as the desktop's sync does), `AcceptShareInvitation`, `DeclineShareInvitation`, `CancelShareInvitation`, `ChangeVaultMemberRole`, `RemoveVaultMember` (rotates the vault key, as on the desktop), `GetVaultSharing` (one shared vault's sharing screen: the vault, whether the user owns it, its members with role and security code, its sent invitations, and the completion errors for its accepted invitations; completes accepted shares first and refuses a personal vault), for passkeys (§9.8) `ListAllPasskeyForRelyingParty`, `PreparePasskeyRegistration`, `CreatePasskey`, `AssertPasskey` and `DeletePasskey`, and for Settings `SetAutoLock` (30, 60, 300, 600 or 3600 seconds, the desktop's choices, stored as `autoLockSeconds` in `hisar.json`; `Status` reports it), `ListAllDevice` and `RevokeDevice` (another device; this one signs out with `SignOut`), `ExportEmergencyKit(password, directory)` (checks the master password like an unlock, writes `Hisar Emergency Kit.pdf` into `directory` and returns the Secret Key and the file's path), `CopySecretReference` (`hisar://<vault>/<item>/<field>`, §9.4.1), `IsMacApprovalAllowed` / `SetMacApprovalAllowed`, for vaults (§5.10) `GetVaultActions`, `RenameVault`, `DeleteVault` (a `VaultDeletionRequest` as JSON), `LeaveVault` and `ListRemovedVaults`, for organizations (§5.11) `ListAllOrganization` (organizations and invitations), `GetOrganization`, `CreateOrganization`, `RenameOrganization`, `DeleteOrganization`, `InviteToOrganization`, `CancelOrganizationInvitation`, `AcceptOrganizationInvitation`, `DeclineOrganizationInvitation`, `SetOrganizationMemberRole`, `RemoveOrganizationMember`, `LeaveOrganization`, `CreateTeam`, `RenameTeam`, `DeleteTeam`, `AddTeamMember`, `RemoveTeamMember`, `GiveVaultToTeam`, `TakeVaultFromTeam`, `CreateOrganizationVault` and `ListAllWaitingVaultKey` (delivers pending vault keys and rotates pending vaults first), and for export (§9.11) `GetExportOptions` and `ExportAllData(format, passphrase, password, directory)` (Face ID first, or the master password when one is given; writes the file into `directory` and returns its path). Item summaries and logins carry `hasOneTimePassword`, never the secret.
- **Phone approvals (§9.9):** `ListAllPendingApproval` (the open, unexpired requests of other devices, opened and checked, as JSON), `ApproveApproval(requestID, isRemembered)` (asks `Biometric.Authenticate` first), `ApproveApprovalWithPassword(requestID, password, isRemembered)` (checks the master password instead) and `DenyApproval`; `isRemembered` is the sheet's switch.
- **Interfaces implemented in Swift:**
  - `SecretKeyStore`: the Secret Key in the Keychain, in a keychain access group shared with the extension.
  - `Biometric`: Face ID or Touch ID. `StoreKey` writes the biometric unlock key to a Keychain item with `SecAccessControl` `.biometryCurrentSet` and `kSecAttrAccessibleWhenUnlockedThisDeviceOnly`. `Authenticate` evaluates an `LAContext` that `LoadKey` passes as `kSecUseAuthenticationContext`, so the user sees one Face ID prompt.
  - `Clipboard`: `UIPasteboard`.
  - `EventListener`: auto-lock notification, `OnApprovalEvent(name, requestID)` for the approval events of the event stream (§9.9), and `OnRemoteChange()` after the backend synced a `vault-changed`, `vaults-changed`, `account-preferences-changed` or `share-invitations-changed` event (the desktop's `OnRemoteChange`), so the app reloads its vaults, the received invitations and an open sharing sheet.
- **Biometrics after launch:** because iOS enforces the biometric check in the Keychain itself, `IsBiometricAllowedAfterLaunch` is on and Face ID can unlock after a launch without the master password first. The 14-day password interval still applies. Auto-lock defaults to 5 minutes; Settings → Security changes it.
- **Android:** Go there starts in UTC and reads neither every system root nor the CAs the user installed. So the app calls `SetTimeZone` with the device's IANA zone before `New` (a zone change applies at the next launch), then `SetTrustedCertificates` with every `AndroidCAStore` certificate, system and user, as PEM (Go then trusts exactly those for the server and website icons), and `SetBiometricKeyPreparer`. The biometric key is a per-use AES-GCM Keystore key: `PrepareStoreKey` or `PrepareLoadKey` builds its cipher, the `Authenticate` that follows puts that cipher in the `BiometricPrompt`'s `CryptoObject`, and the `StoreKey` or `LoadKey` after it uses it, so each operation shows one prompt. After a master password or Secret Key change, Go shows that prompt once more to keep biometric unlock on; the iOS and Mac keychains store the new key without one.
- **One-time codes:** `GetOneTimeCodeForService` checks the match as `GetLoginForService` does and returns only the code, expiry and period. When a password fill (list, QuickType or without user interaction) is for a login with `hasOneTimePassword`, the extension copies the current code to the pasteboard (`localOnly`, 90 s expiry) before completing the request.
- **AutoFill:** the extension receives `ASCredentialServiceIdentifier`s (a domain or a URL; bare domains are read as `https://`). `ListAllLoginForService` uses the same matching as the browser extension (§9.2), and `GetLoginForService` checks the match again before returning the password. Passkey requests carry a relying party ID instead, and only passkeys with exactly that RP ID are offered (§9.8).
- **Signing:** until the Apple Developer Program membership (§12 #7), the app runs on the developer's own iPhone with a free Apple ID (re-signed every 7 days). App groups and the AutoFill extension need the paid membership on a device, so the extension is developed and tested in the simulator until then.

**iOS app (`mobile/ios`).**

- **Project:** XcodeGen (`project.yml`, pinned 2.46.0) generates `Hisar.xcodeproj`, which is committed so Xcode opens it without XcodeGen; CI fails when the two differ. The team and the bundle identifier prefix live in `Configuration/Signing.xcconfig` (or an ignored `Local.xcconfig`). The extension (`.autofill`), the app group (`group.…`) and the keychain group (`$(AppIdentifierPrefix)….shared`) are derived from the prefix and reach the code through `Info.plist` keys.
- **Targets:** `Hisar` (app with the extension) and `HisarAutoFill` share `Shared/` as sources rather than a framework. HisarKit is a static framework, so each binary links its own copy of Go. `HisarPersonal` (scheme "Hisar Personal Team") is the same app for a free personal team: no extension, empty app group and keychain group, and its own bundle identifier (`….personal`) so the free team never registers the real one. With no app group the state goes to the app's Application Support folder, and Keychain items get no access group.
- **Threading:** Swift wraps `HisarkitHisar` behind a `VaultBackend` protocol. View models call it on a background queue, because Argon2 and network I/O block. Tests replace it with a fake.
- **Keychain:** the Secret Key is `kSecAttrAccessibleAfterFirstUnlockThisDeviceOnly` in the shared access group. The Face ID key is `.biometryCurrentSet` + `WhenUnlockedThisDeviceOnly`; `Authenticate` keeps the evaluated `LAContext` and `LoadKey` passes it as `kSecUseAuthenticationContext`, so the user sees one prompt. The state directory is excluded from backups.
- **Clipboard:** copies are `localOnly` with a 90 s `expirationDate`. Go's own 90 s clearing asks `GetText`, which answers from `changeCount` instead of reading the pasteboard, so iOS never shows the paste banner for another app's text.
- **Lifecycle:** going to the background records the time; returning after the auto-lock interval (Settings → Security, 5 minutes by default) locks. While the scene is inactive, a separate window at alert level covers everything, including sheets, so the app switcher snapshot shows only the logo. The lock screen asks for Face ID once per lock when it is ready.
- **AutoFill:** after every full sync and every save or delete, the app replaces the `ASCredentialIdentityStore` identities: host + username for each login or server with a URL (a server's is its admin console), with record identifier `vaultID/itemID`, an `ASOneTimeCodeCredentialIdentity` (label: the username, or the title) for each such login with a one-time password, and an `ASPasskeyCredentialIdentity` (RP ID, user name, credential ID, user handle) for each passkey in a login. The extension declares `ProvidesPasswords`, `ProvidesPasskeys` and `ProvidesOneTimeCodes`; `prepareOneTimeCodeCredentialList` lists only matching logins with a code, and one-time code requests complete with `ASOneTimeCodeCredential` and never hand out the password. Without user interaction the extension answers only when its process is still unlocked; otherwise it asks for interaction and unlocks with Face ID or the master password. The list shows the logins matching each service identifier; search also covers every other login, which is filled only on an explicit tap. `hisarkit` calls `debug.FreeOSMemory` after every key derivation, so the extension drops back from about 126 MB at the Argon2 peak to about 68 MB once unlocked (simulator debug build).
- **Deployment target:** iOS 26.0 (Xcode 27, iOS 27 SDK), so the app uses the iOS 26 design directly and has no fallback UI for older systems.
- **Interface:** system components only; the app draws no chrome of its own. Light and dark follow the system, text uses Dynamic Type styles, every icon is an SF Symbol with an accessibility label, and the only custom colour is Hisar's accent (`AccentColor`: graphite in light mode, the desktop's `#eceef1` in dark), set as the asset catalog's global accent colour.
  - A `TabView` with the Liquid Glass tab bar: **Vaults**, **Approvals** (badge: the number of waiting Mac requests) and **Settings**, plus a search tab (`Tab(role: .search)`) that searches every item. Each tab is a `NavigationStack` with a large title and the Liquid Glass navigation bar; actions are `.toolbar` items and `Menu`s.
  - **Vaults:** an `.insetGrouped` `List`. Waiting Mac requests come first as a card (the Mac's name and a live countdown, the request's sentence, the key, **Deny** and **Approve**, and "N more requests" opening the Approvals tab); then, as on the Mac, a banner for each share invitation the user received ("Ada shared a vault with you as editor", the inviter's email, **Decline** and **Accept**; after accepting, the vault appears once the owner's Hisar completes the share); then **Favorites** (§4.7), the starred items of every vault with each one's vault name (swipe to remove; empty, "Star an item to keep it here, on all your devices. Only you see your favorites."), then the vaults with their item counts, and **By type** (Logins, SSH keys) across all vaults. The star in an item's toolbar and a leading swipe on a vault's item rows add or remove a favorite. Toolbar: **Lock** and a **+** menu (New login, New vault). Pull to refresh syncs, which also completes the shares people accepted and reloads the received invitations.
  - **Approvals:** the waiting requests with a live countdown; tapping one opens the approval sheet, swiping offers Deny. Empty, a `ContentUnavailableView` says that requests from Hisar on the Mac appear here while Hisar is open and unlocked on this iPhone.
  - **Approval sheet:** a system sheet (medium and large detents) with the request's grouped `LabeledContent` rows, the forwarded-request warning, the encryption note, and **Approve with Face ID** and **Deny** in the bottom toolbar; the master password field appears in the sheet when Face ID is unavailable.
  - **Vault:** the items, searchable, with **+** (writable vaults) in the toolbar. A shared vault has **Share** (owners) or **Members** (editors and readers) in the toolbar; a personal vault's **Share** explains in an alert that personal vaults are private and a shared vault is needed, the Mac's rule. A **⋯** menu has **Rename…**, **Delete vault…** and **Leave vault…** (§5.10); an action the user may not take shows its reason in an alert. Rename is an alert with a text field. **Delete** is a sheet: "These people lose access" for a shared vault, then "Move N items to another vault" (the first writable vault by default, with a **Move to** picker) or "Delete the vault and its N items" with "Type Work to confirm", and the destructive **Delete vault**; an empty vault needs one confirmation. **Leave** is a `confirmationDialog`. When the open vault disappears, the screen goes back to the list.
  - **Removed vaults:** after every refresh the Vaults list shows, at the top, a dismissible row for each vault that disappeared ("“Work” is no longer available: it was deleted, or you no longer have access to it.").
  - **Sharing sheet** (`GetVaultSharing`, medium and large detents): for owners, **Invite by email** (email field, a role `Picker` with the Mac's roles Owner, Editor and Reader, and **Send invitation**; every address gets the same "Invitation sent", whether or not it has an account); a warning row when an accepted invitation could not be completed (e.g. "the signing key of … changed since you last shared with them; stopping"); **Members**, owners first, each with role and security code, and a key-changed warning; **Invited**, this vault's sent invitations ("Waiting for them to accept" or "Accepted, finishing on your next sync"), each with **Cancel** behind a `confirmationDialog`. Tapping a member opens their detail: the security code to compare (with `ShareLink` for the user's own code), and for owners a role `Picker` and **Remove from vault** behind a `confirmationDialog` that says the vault is re-encrypted with a new key. Editors and readers see the members and codes only. The sheet reloads on `OnRemoteChange`, so a person who accepts appears without a refresh.
  - **Completing shares:** the iPhone runs the same Go code as the Mac (`session.CompleteAcceptedShares` through `core/app`): on every sync, every `share-invitations-changed` event, pull to refresh, the People screen and the sharing sheet, it verifies each accepted recipient's signed keys, pins their signing key, refuses on a changed key and wraps the vault key. Only an unlocked app does this; a locked iPhone leaves the share to the owner's next unlocked device.
  - **Item icons** (§4.5): the editor's icon opens a picker sheet with **Automatic**, the presets, the colors and **Choose photo** (`PhotosPicker`). Library photos are often HEIC, which core does not decode, so `AppModel.convertPhotoForIcon` decodes the photo with UIKit, applies its orientation, scales it to at most 512 points and hands HisarKit's `PrepareItemIcon` a JPEG, or a PNG when it has transparency; a photo UIKit can't read shows an error. Lists and details draw the chosen icon, else a login's website icon, which `AppModel` asks HisarKit for once per domain off the main thread and forgets on lock. Presets are SF Symbols (`ChosenIcon.symbolByPreset`) in system colors.
  - **Item detail:** grouped rows for username, password (reveal, copy), one-time code (with a progress ring), custom fields, websites, passkeys and notes. Copying plays a success haptic and shows the 90-second note; each copyable row's context menu also has **Copy secret reference**, and a quiet last row copies the password's reference.
  - **Settings** (what of the Mac's settings makes sense on a phone): **Account** (name, email, server; **Export data…**, §9.11; **Delete account** with the preview, master password and typed email, §5.7.1; **Secret Key and Emergency Kit**, which asks for the master password, then shows the Secret Key and offers the Emergency Kit PDF through `ShareLink`; the file is written to the app's temporary folder and deleted when the screen closes), **Security** (Face ID, Auto-lock picker 30 s · 1 min · 5 min · 10 min · 1 h, Lock now, the note that copied values clear after 90 seconds, **Change master password** and **Replace Secret Key**, which shows the new Secret Key and shares the new Emergency Kit PDF like the Account screen, §5.7), **Mac approvals** (the switch "Approve requests from my Mac", on by default, and the note that approving answers one request and never unlocks the Mac), **People** (invitations for you with a badge, invitations you sent, **Invite to Hisar**, which creates an account, and each person with their security code and a "key changed" warning), **Devices** (each signed-in device with platform and last activity, "This iPhone" first; another device can be signed out after a confirmation), **AutoFill**, **Password generator**, **Help** (Contact support with the address and Copy, Copy diagnostics with the app and iOS versions, the server URL and this iPhone's device ID), **About**, and **Sign out**. The SSH agent, command-line tool, browser extension, agent vault access, open at login, updates and import stay on the Mac.
  - **Organizations** (a Settings row): the same list, organization screen, invitations, banners, grouping, new-vault **Where** and organization-vault sharing sheet as the Mac (§5.11 Screens), as `List`s with `NavigationLink`s, sheets and `confirmationDialog`s.
  - **Sign in, create account and unlock:** `Form`s with the same copy as before (the Emergency Kit wording included), the Server field behind **Use a different server…** (§5.3), and after the new Secret Key the "Unlock with Face ID?" offer (§5.2); the lock screen shows the mark, the account's email, **Unlock with Face ID** and the master password field, and above them the accepted shares waiting for this unlock (§5.8).
- **Simulator limits:** a build without a team is registered as a provider but iOS does not offer it, so UI tests drive the extension's screens through a debug-only preview in the app. Face ID is tested in the simulator by posting the biometric daemon's enrollment and match notifications.

**Android app (`mobile/android`).**

- **Project:** Gradle with two modules. `:model` is plain JVM with no Android dependency: HisarKit's JSON types, `VaultBackend`, the app model (a port of the iPhone's `AppModel`, split into one model per area) and AutoFill's parser and matcher, so their tests run on the JVM against a fake backend. `:app` holds the Compose screens, the platform adapters and the AutoFill service. `make build-android-aar` runs `gomobile bind -target=android/arm64,android/amd64 -androidapi 29 -javapkg com.tryhisar` and writes `mobile/build/HisarKit.aar` (Java classes `com.tryhisar.hisarkit.Hisarkit` and `Hisar`). The script refuses a `libgojni.so` whose LOAD segments are not aligned to 16 KB, which devices with 16 KB pages require. The package is `com.tryhisar.hisar`.
- **Versions and ABIs:** Android 10 (API 29) and later, the same floor as Bitwarden and Dashlane; Android 10 is also the first version that hides the clipboard from apps in the background. The AAR has arm64-v8a and x86_64 (emulators). 32-bit ARM is out: `github.com/bytemare/ksf` v0.5.0, which `bytemare/opaque` brings in, passes `math.MaxUint32` to a `%d` format in `argon2id/argon2.go`, and that untyped constant overflows `int` on 32-bit targets, so the build fails there.
- **One process (D2):** `HisarApplication` builds the process's one HisarKit backend on a background thread when the process starts. The app, the AutoFill service and its activity all use it, so unlocking either unlocks both, and `hisar.json` has one writer and the account one event stream. The iPhone's extension is a second Go runtime with its own unlock instead. Fills do not postpone auto-lock, because login matching uses `requireUnlockedWithoutActivity`. `HisarKitBackend` refuses to run on the main thread, since Go blocks on the network, Argon2 and biometric prompts. The HisarKit calls that only Android makes are under **Android** above.
- **State and keys:** `hisar.json` and the cache are in `noBackupFilesDir/Hisar` (mode 0700). The Secret Key is a file in `noBackupFilesDir`, sealed with AES-GCM under one AES-256 Android Keystore key that needs no authentication (StrongBox when the device has it), with the account ID as AAD: readable after the first unlock and never leaving the device, as the iPhone's keychain item. Nothing is backed up: `allowBackup="false"`, and `data_extraction_rules.xml` and `backup_rules.xml` exclude every domain from cloud backups and device transfers. A new device signs in with the Secret Key and master password.
- **Biometric unlock (D4):** one per-use AES-256-GCM Keystore key per account, which needs a strong biometric for every use (`setUserAuthenticationRequired`, and from API 30 `setUserAuthenticationParameters(0, AUTH_BIOMETRIC_STRONG)`) and which a new enrollment destroys (`setInvalidatedByBiometricEnrollment`). Through `SetBiometricKeyPreparer`, each store and each load shows one `BiometricPrompt` with the cipher in its `CryptoObject`; the prompt's other button is "Use master password". After a new fingerprint, `PrepareLoadKey` fails before any prompt, and Go turns biometric unlock off ("biometric unlock was turned off, unlock with your master password and turn it on again"). Changing the master password or replacing the Secret Key asks for one fingerprint to keep biometric unlock on, since a per-use key cannot be rewritten without a prompt. Approvals and export show a plain strong prompt with no key.
- **Clipboard (D5):** a secret copy is marked sensitive (`android.content.extra.IS_SENSITIVE`), so Android 13 and later show dots in their copy preview. Android has no expiry date for a clip and lets an app read the clipboard only while it has focus, so three things clear a copy: Go's 90 s timer while the process runs; an inexact `AlarmManager.setAndAllowWhileIdle` alarm at copy + 90 s, which also runs in a new process after Hisar was closed; and Hisar's window getting focus after that time. Hisar records the clip's timestamp, never its text. Because Android hides the clipboard from an app in the background, the alarm cannot see whether the clip is still Hisar's and clears whatever is there, including a newer copy another app made. In front, Hisar keeps a newer copy from another app. Measured on emulators: the alarm cleared at 133 to 158 s on API 37 and at 90 s on API 29, and it cleared another app's later copy on API 37. A WorkManager job at 90 s waited until the app was opened again on API 37, so Hisar does not use one. A force-stop cancels the alarm, so the copy then stays until the next time Hisar opens: on API 37, `am force-stop` removed the alarm (`pi_cancelled` in `dumpsys alarm`), the copy was still recorded 196 s after it was made, and opening Hisar cleared it. The copy notice therefore says "It clears in about 2 minutes" (docs/COPY.md). Copies that are not secret, such as a secret reference or a website, are not marked and never cleared. The secure note body is not selectable, because the selection toolbar's Copy would write a plain clip that is never cleared; **Copy notes** is the way to copy it.
- **Screenshots and the app switcher (D6):** Settings → Security → **Allow screenshots**, off by default in release builds and on in debug builds. Off, Hisar's windows are secure (`FLAG_SECURE`), so screenshots, screen recordings and the app switcher show nothing of Hisar. From API 33 the app switcher never shows Hisar's content (`setRecentsScreenshotEnabled(false)`), and while the activity is not in front a cover with the logo hides the screen, as on the iPhone.
- **Network:** release builds sign in and create accounts only over `https://`: the sign-in model refuses an `http://` server or invite link before Go is asked ("This server's address starts with http://, so the connection would not be encrypted. Hisar signs in only over https://."). Debug builds accept `http://`, for a server on the developer's machine. This differs from the iPhone, which allows `http` to local addresses. Go trusts every certificate in `AndroidCAStore`, the user-installed CAs included, so a server behind a company's own CA works (D11). A CA the user installed can read the traffic to the server, but vault contents stay end-to-end encrypted and OPAQUE keeps the master password from the server. The network security config, which governs only Kotlin's own requests (Digital Asset Links), trusts the same CAs and allows cleartext only in debug builds. Client certificates are not supported, as on the iPhone.
- **Lifecycle:** `AppViewModel` follows the whole process (`ProcessLifecycleOwner`): going to the background records the time, and returning after the auto-lock interval locks, as on the iPhone. The minimum version is checked at launch and on returning (§8.7).
- **AutoFill (D3, D10, D15):**
  - `HisarAutofillService` reads the form from the request's `AssistStructure` (`FillRequestParser`): autofill hints first, then the HTML attributes, the input type and words in the view ID. On a web page only the fields under the node that carries `webDomain` count. Each field belongs to the page (scheme and host) of the nearest node at or above it that carries `webDomain`: the input itself on WebView 145 and Chrome, the form on WebView 91 and 113. A browser can put several pages in one form, such as a bank's frame inside another site, so Hisar fills only the fields of one page, the page of the focused field (`FillContext.getFocusedId`), or the only page there is; a form spanning several pages with no focused field among them gets nothing. The datasets, the save info and the unlock target name only that page's fields. A page address is trusted only from a browser whose package name and signing certificate's SHA-256 are both on the privileged-browser allowlist the passkey provider uses (`assets/privileged_browsers.json`, read once by `PrivilegedBrowserList`). Any other app that reports a web domain, including one that takes a browser's package name with another certificate, is treated as that app (D10), so an app cannot get a site's logins by naming the site. Browsers that need compatibility mode are listed in `res/xml/hisar_autofill_service.xml`. Tor Browser, Opera GX, Ecosia and Kiwi are not on the allowlist, so Hisar fills them as apps: Search Hisar, the logins linked to them and those of sites that vouch for them, never a page's logins by its address. Chrome asks Hisar only after the user picks **Autofill using another service** in Chrome; Settings → AutoFill opens that page.
  - **Locked (D3):** a single "Unlock Hisar" suggestion ("Sign in to Hisar" when signed out), which opens the unlock screen and then fills. Android has no credential identity store, and Hisar writes no plaintext index of its own, so a locked Hisar shows no usernames. Unlocked, there is one suggestion per matching login with its username, plus "Search Hisar". Every suggestion carries its own authentication, so a password leaves Hisar only for the login the user picks, after Go checks the match again (`GetLoginForService`, `GetLoginForAndroidApp`). From API 30 suggestions also appear in the keyboard.
  - **Native apps (D10):** Hisar identifies the app by package name and the SHA-256 of its signing certificate (apps with several signers can search and fill, but are never linked). It offers first the logins linked to the app (Android app links, §9.2), then the logins of sites that vouch for the app in their Digital Asset Links. Hisar asks only the sites whose registrable domain's name is a segment of the package name (github.com for `com.github.android`), because asking a site tells it that the vault has a login for it. It fetches `https://<site>/.well-known/assetlinks.json` itself, never through Google's API: no redirects, 2 s timeouts, at most 256 KiB, answers kept for an hour. A `delegate_permission/common.get_login_creds` statement naming the package and certificate vouches. A login picked through search in an app asks "Use GitHub in <app>?" with **Always use for this app**, which adds the app link, and **Only this time**.
  - **One-time codes:** a one-time code field gets the codes of the matching logins. Filling a password of a login with a one-time password copies its current code ("One-time code copied. It clears in about 2 minutes.").
  - **Save (D15):** a form with a password gets a `SaveInfo`, so after a sign-in Android offers to save it to Hisar. Saving opens Hisar's editor, after an unlock if needed, with a new login in the Personal vault: named after the site or the app, with the site as its website, and linked to the app once saved. The typed values reach the app in memory (`SavedLoginInbox`), never on disk; the intent carries only an action, and `MainActivity` opens the waiting login whenever it comes to the front. Android 14 before QPR2 (March 2024) blocks the save sheet from opening Hisar when the page's app targets API 34 or later, so there the editor opens the next time the user opens Hisar, and the login is gone if the process died meanwhile. The iPhone app has no such offer.
  - Passkeys come from the credential provider (§9.8), not from the AutoFill service.
  - The gear next to Hisar in the system's Autofill service screen opens Settings → AutoFill (`android:settingsActivity`, an alias of `MainActivity`), after an unlock if Hisar is locked. Settings → AutoFill always offers the system's settings: Passwords, passkeys & accounts from API 34, the Settings app's root before it, since API 29 to 33 have no public intent for the Autofill service page.
- **Interface (D8):** the iPhone app's screens, sections, copy and accessibility identifiers (as Compose test tags), drawn with Material 3 components restyled with Hisar's palettes (`ThemePalettes.kt`, generated from `assets/themes/themes.json` like the Swift palettes) and never Android's dynamic color. The copy says "this device" and "biometrics" where the iPhone says "this iPhone" and "Face ID" (docs/COPY.md). Sheets that fill the screen on the iPhone are pushed screens; touch-and-hold menus are dropdown menus.
- **Emergency Kit and export files:** the sign-in screen scans the kit's QR code with CameraX and ZXing, which need no Google Play services; the camera permission is asked when scanning, and a refusal shows **Open Settings**. Settings saves the kit or an export through the system's file creator, prints the kit through the print dialog, and sends either to another app through the share sheet, as the iPhone's share sheet does. For a share, Hisar copies the file into `cache/shared/`, the only folder its `FileProvider` serves, and deletes the copy when the screen closes.
- **Tests:** the iPhone's unit and UI tests are ported under their Swift names; `IosTestParityTest` fails when one has no Android test, a rename or a reason (CLAUDE.md, Tests). Instrumented tests run on an emulator against a real `hisar-server`, the sharing partner and `hisar-ui-test-mac`, which plays the Mac for phone approvals (§9.9). CI runs them on API 37.

### 9.4 CLI

`hisar` is the command-line client for the terminal and for scripts: vaults (`vaults`, `vault create|rename|delete|leave`, §5.10), items (`ls`, `get`, `add`, `edit`, `rm`), favorites (`favorite <item> [-v vault]` and `unfavorite` print `Added "GitHub" (Personal) to Favorites.` and `Removed … from Favorites.`; `ls --favorites` lists them in every vault with a VAULT column, or in the vault `-v` names; §4.7), the generator, import of other password managers, Hisar exports and `.env` files (§9.7), export (`export`, §9.11), sharing (`share <vault> <email> [-r role]`, `unshare`, `people`, `invitations [accept|decline|cancel <id>]`, §5.8), invites (`invite <email>`), organizations (`orgs`, `org create|show|rename|delete|invite|cancel|accept|decline|role|remove|leave`, `team create|rename|delete|add|remove`, `vault create <name> --org <org> --team <team>`, `vault give|take <vault> --org <org> --team <team> [-r role]`; organizations and teams are named by name, ignoring case, or by ID, members by email; `orgs` and `org show` first deliver pending vault keys and rotate pending vaults and say so; §5.11), the account (`account change-password`, `account replace-secret-key [-o file]`, `account delete`, standalone only because they need the master password and the Secret Key, §5.7, §5.7.1), share links (`share-link`, `share-links`, §5.9), devices, `read` and `run`, which hand secrets to scripts and commands through secret references (§9.4.1), and `mcp`, the server that lets coding agents use Hisar (§9.4.2). It works in one of two modes:

- **Through the desktop app**, like 1Password's CLI integration: `hisar` asks the running Hisar app for what each command needs over `~/.hisar/cli.sock`. It has no session, state file or keys of its own, and nothing is unlocked in the `hisar` process.
- **Standalone**: its own sign-in, with the session in `state.json` (`HISAR_STATE`) and the Secret Key in the OS keychain (`HISAR_SECRET_KEY` overrides it); every command that decrypts asks for the master password. This is the mode on servers, where no app is installed, and whenever the app does not answer (see "Starting the app").

**Choosing the mode.** `--standalone` (before the command) or `HISAR_STANDALONE=1` forces standalone. Otherwise `hisar` connects to the socket (`HISAR_CLI_SOCKET` overrides the path, for the app and the CLI) and sends `hello`. It uses the app when the app answers. When the socket is missing or refuses the connection, `hisar` starts the app and waits for it (below). It falls back to standalone when no app is installed, when the app answers `integration-disabled`, or when the started app did not answer but a standalone sign-in exists. An error after the app answered (denied, signed out, offline) is reported; `hisar` never retries it standalone. `hisar status` prints the mode, why that mode was chosen, and the account (`email` on `server`, and whether the app is locked). Missing arguments are asked for interactively; flags are for scripts. Standalone, `hisar invitations` completes accepted shares after asking for the master password, as the app does on every sync. `signup`, `login` and `logout` manage the standalone sign-in, and `emergency-kit` needs the Secret Key, which the app never hands out; in app mode it stops with a message that points to the app or to `--standalone`. The removed `recover` and `recovery-*` commands only print `account recovery was removed; sign in with your Emergency Kit` and exit with an error. `hisar --version` prints the version stamped at build time (`-X main.version`), which it also sends to the server (§8.7).

**Starting the app.** SSH clients cannot start Hisar, but `hisar` can. When the socket is missing or refuses the connection, a command that can run through the app (every command except `signup`, `login`, `logout` and `emergency-kit`, which then run standalone as before) starts the app hidden (§9.1.2) and prints "Starting Hisar…" on stderr. It does not when the SSH agent socket (`HISAR_SSH_AGENT_SOCKET` or `~/.hisar/agent.sock`) accepts a connection: then the app is running with the command-line switch off, and starting it again would only show its window.

- macOS: `open -g -a <Hisar.app> --args --hidden` when `hisar` is the binary bundled in a `Hisar.app` (also through the installed symlink), otherwise `open -g -b com.tryhisar.hisar --args --hidden`. `open` failing means no app is installed.
- Linux: the first executable `Hisar` next to `hisar`, on the `PATH`, in `/usr/local/bin` or in `/usr/bin`, started with `--hidden` in its own session with its output discarded. None found means no app is installed.

It then dials the socket and sends `hello` every 200 ms for up to 10 s. Once the app answers, the command runs through it. If no app is installed, `hisar` runs standalone as before. If the app was started but did not answer in time, `hisar` runs standalone when a standalone sign-in exists; otherwise the command fails with "Hisar isn't running. Open the Hisar app and try again." instead of a connection error. `HISAR_NO_LAUNCH=1` (for scripts and CI) never starts the app and keeps the earlier behaviour: standalone whenever the app does not answer.

**Bundled in the macOS app.** `Hisar.app/Contents/Helpers/hisar` is a universal (arm64 + x86_64) build of the CLI. It cannot be `Contents/MacOS/hisar`: the default macOS file system ignores case, so that is the app's own executable `Hisar`. It is built with `CGO_ENABLED=0`, stamped with the app version and signed by `scripts/sign-desktop-app.sh` before the app, and it updates with the app through Sparkle (§9.1.1). Settings → Developer tools → Command-line tool:

- Shows whether `hisar` is installed (a symlink to the bundled binary) and where, and whether the first `hisar` on the user's `PATH` is this app's. Apps started from Finder get a minimal `PATH`, so the app reads the user's from their login shell (`$SHELL -l -i -c`, 3 s timeout, cached for the session) and falls back to `/etc/paths`, `/etc/paths.d` and its own `PATH`.
- **Install command-line tool** creates the symlink `hisar` → the bundled binary in `/opt/homebrew/bin` when that directory exists and is writable, else `/usr/local/bin` when it exists and is writable, else `~/.local/bin` (created, 0755), and then shows a one-line hint when that directory is not on the `PATH`. It never asks for an administrator password. An existing `hisar` there that is a symlink into a `Hisar.app` (an older or moved copy) is replaced; anything else, a file or a symlink to somewhere else, is never touched, and Settings shows what is there.
- **Uninstall** removes only `hisar` symlinks in those three directories that point into a `Hisar.app`.
- **Let the command-line tool use Hisar** (on by default; stored in `desktop.json` and kept on sign-out). Off closes and removes the socket, so `hisar` falls back to standalone (it sees the app running on the SSH agent socket and does not start it again); a request that still arrives is answered `integration-disabled`.
- Development builds have no bundled binary; the section says so and offers no install.

**Installed by the Linux package.** The `.deb` and `.rpm` (`scripts/package-desktop-linux.sh`, `desktop/build/linux/nfpm/nfpm.yaml`) install the app at `/usr/bin/Hisar`, the CLI at `/usr/bin/hisar` and the browser host at `/usr/lib/hisar/hisar-browser-host`, so `hisar` is on the `PATH` without a symlink and finds the app at `/usr/bin/Hisar` (above). Settings → Command-line tool shows "Installed with the Hisar package at `/usr/bin/hisar`" and whether the first `hisar` on the `PATH` is that one, with no **Install** or **Uninstall**: the package manager adds and removes it. A development build that is not installed finds the browser host next to its own executable.

**Socket.** `~/.hisar/cli.sock`, socket 0600 in the same 0700 directory as the SSH agent and browser sockets, served while the app runs and the switch is on. The app accepts only peers with its own user ID (`LOCAL_PEERCRED` on macOS, `SO_PEERCRED` on Linux) and closes any other connection without an answer. Frames are a 32-bit big-endian length followed by UTF-8 JSON, at most 16 MiB. Requests are `{"id", "type", …parameters}`; responses `{"id", "ok": true, "result"}` or `{"id", "ok": false, "error": {"code", "message"}}`. Error codes: `invalid-request`, `integration-disabled`, `signed-out`, `locked`, `denied`, `offline`, `not-found`, `update-required` (§8.7), `internal`. Shared types live in `core/cliprotocol`.
