| Type | Parameters | Result |
|---|---|---|
| `hello` | — | `protocolVersion` (1), `state` (`signed-out`, `locked`, `unlocked`), `email`, `server`, `isApproved` (for this caller's approval scope, or for the agent or launcher connection), `features` (what the app understands beyond the protocol version: `secure-notes`, `credit-cards`, `identities`, `favorites`, `api-credentials`, `tags`, `addresses`, `software-licenses`, `servers`, `databases`, `organizations`, then every launcher request type, §9.4.3; an older app sends none); never needs an approval |
| `account` | — | `accountID`, `name`, `email`, `role`, `server` |
| `vaults.list` | — | `vaults`: `[{id, name, type, role, itemCount}]` |
| `vaults.create` | `name` | `vault` |
| `items.list` | `vaultID` | `items`: summaries `{id, revision, category, title, username, urls, tags, hasOneTimePassword}` (`tags` only from an app whose `hello` lists `tags`, so `hisar ls --tag` refuses an older app rather than matching nothing) |
| `items.get` | `vaultID`, `itemID` | `item`: the summary plus `password`, `notes`, `fields`, `passkeys` (`relyingPartyID`, `userName`), `publicKey`, `fingerprint` |
| `items.code` | `vaultID`, `itemID` | `code`, `expiresAt` (RFC 3339), `period` |
| `items.save` | `vaultID`, `item` (`id` and `revision` for an update; `title`, `username`, `urls`, `password`, `notes`, optional `fields`, optional `tags` (`hisar` sends back the tags it read), optional `category` of a new item: `login` or `secure-note`, sent only to an app whose `hello` lists `secure-notes`, since an older app ignores it and saves a login) | `item`. An update keeps everything else of the item: category, one-time password, passkeys, SSH key, the fields unless `fields` is sent (`.env` import) and the tags unless `tags` is sent |
| `vaults.actions` | `vaultID` | `vault`, `canRename`, `canDelete`, `deleteBlockedReason`, `canLeave`, `leaveBlockedReason`, `membersLosingAccess` (`name`, `email`, `role`), `targetVaults` (§5.10) |
| `vaults.rename` | `vaultID`, `name` | `vault` |
| `vaults.delete` | `vaultID`, `vaultDeletion` (`moveToVaultID`, or `isDeletingItems` with `confirmedName`, the vault's name as typed) | `movedCount` |
| `vaults.leave` | `vaultID` | `{}` |
| `export` | `export` (`format`: `encrypted` or `plain`, `passphrase`, `path`: absolute, must not exist) | `path`, `vaultCount`, `itemCount`. The app writes the file itself (mode 0600, never replacing a file), so private keys and one-time password secrets never cross the socket; always asks first (§9.11) |
| `items.delete` | `vaultID`, `itemID` | `{}` |
| `favorites.list` | — | `favorites`: `[{vaultID, itemID}]`, the user's starred items in the vaults they can open (§4.7) |
| `favorites.add`, `favorites.remove` | `vaultID`, `itemID` | `{}` |
| `items.import` | `vaultID`, `importedItems` (at most 10 parsed items; an app that announces `favorites` stars the ones with `IsFavorite`) | `savedCount`, `failures` `[{title, message}]` |
| `people.list` | — | `people`: `[{accountID, name, email, role, securityCode, isCurrentUser, isKeyChanged}]`, the people the user shares a vault with (§5.8) |
| `vaults.share` | `vaultID`, `email`, `role` | `{}`; sends a share invitation (§5.8) |
| `vaults.unshare` | `vaultID`, `accountID` | `{}` (rotates the vault key, §4.4) |
| `invite` | `email` | `{}`; the server emails the invite (§5.1) |
| `invitations.list` | — | `received`, `sent` as in the app; listing first completes accepted shares |
| `invitations.accept`, `invitations.decline`, `invitations.cancel` | `invitationID` | `{}` |
| `shareLinks.create` | `vault` (name or ID), `query` (item title or ID, matched as by `hisar get`), `shareLink` (`fields`, `expiresInSeconds`, `maxViews`, `passphrase`; or `text` and `title`) | `shareLink` `{id, url, title, fieldLabels, expiresAt, maxViews}`; always asks first (§5.9) |
| `shareLinks.list` | — | `shareLinks`: `[{id, title, fieldLabels, isText, createdAt, expiresAt, maxViews, viewCount, hasPassphrase}]` |
| `shareLinks.revoke` | `shareLinkID` | `{}` |
| `devices.list` | — | `devices` |
| `devices.revoke` | `deviceID` | `{}` |
| `secrets.resolve` | `references` (at most 500 `hisar://` references), `commandLine` (the command `hisar run` starts, as its arguments; empty for `hisar read`) | `values`, in the order of `references`; fails as a whole with `not-found` or `invalid-request` and a message that names every reference that failed and why (§9.4.1) |
| `agent.hello`, `agent.*` | — | requests of `hisar mcp`, only on an agent connection (§9.4.2) |
| `launcher.hello`, `launcher.*` | — | requests of a launcher such as Raycast, only on a launcher connection (§9.4.3) |

The app answers with the bound `app.Service` methods, so the CLI gets the same checks, offline behaviour and errors as the UI. It never sends the master password, the Secret Key, account or vault keys, SSH or passkey private keys, or one-time password secrets: items carry what `hisar get` prints, and `items.code` and `otp` references return only the current code. A `private key` reference is refused (§9.4.1); SSH keys are used through the SSH agent (§9.6). `hisar` parses import files itself and sends the parsed items in batches of 10.

**Approval.**

- Approvals are per caller scope, worked out once when the connection opens. From the peer PID (`LOCAL_PEERPID`, `SO_PEERCRED`) the app reads the session ID (`getsid`), the controlling terminal device and the start time of the session leader.
  - A caller with a controlling terminal is approved per terminal session, so a session ID that is reused after the terminal closed does not inherit an approval. The terminal app and its icon come from `desktop/appinfo`, starting from the parent of `hisar`, because the bundled `hisar` itself lives inside `Hisar.app`.
  - A caller without a terminal (a GUI app, a launcher, a launchd job) is approved per app: the app its process chain resolves to (`desktop/appinfo`), keyed by bundle path and Team ID, and only when macOS verified the bundle's code signature together with its sealed resources (Apple's or a developer's), so an executable added to a signed bundle gets no identity. The result is cached per bundle until anything in it changes on disk; the first check of a large bundle takes from a fraction of a second (Raycast, about 0.2 s) to seconds (Xcode). Every GUI process of the user shares launchd's session 1 and has no terminal, so keying them by session would let one approval cover all of them.
  - Any other caller without a terminal (unsigned, a signature that does not verify, no app at all, Linux) is approved for its own connection only; the approval ends when the connection closes.
  - The dialog says what an approval covers: "this terminal session", "<App> without a terminal, until 10 minutes after its last request", or "this connection only".
- Every request except `hello` needs an approval for its scope. The first one opens the approval dialog of the SSH agent (§9.6) in its full layout with "hisar in Terminal wants to use Hisar", Terminal's icon with the Hisar badge, and the program path and terminal device in the detail box; there is no "Don't ask again" checkbox and never the compact layout. On a Mac with Touch ID, Touch ID starts at once in the dialog; without it, Approve approves.
- If Hisar is locked, the same window unlocks it: with Touch ID when Touch ID unlock is ready (§5.4; the one Touch ID check approves and unlocks), otherwise with the master password typed into the window. A wrong password keeps the request open with a message. Signed out, requests fail with `signed-out` and no window.
- Deny, Escape or closing the window fail the request with `denied`, and `hisar` prints "Hisar denied the request (it was declined)". No answer before the dialog's deadline, 60 s or 2 minutes when the iPhone can answer (§9.9), fails it the same way with "Hisar denied the request (it was not answered within a minute)" or "… within 2 minutes)". Requests from one scope that arrive while its approval is open wait for that one window.
- An approval lasts until 10 minutes after the scope's last request (a connection's also ends when it closes), or until Hisar locks, signs out or quits.
- `export` always opens the dialog, even in an approved session, like a share link (§5.9): "hisar in Terminal wants to export all your vaults", with "Encrypted with an export passphrase · <path>" or "Plain JSON, not encrypted · <path>" and, for plain JSON, the warning "The file will hold every password, one-time code secret and SSH private key in plain text." Touch ID, the master password or the iPhone (§9.9) answer it.
- Same-user code that runs inside an approved terminal session, or in an approved app, can use Hisar through `hisar` until the approval ends, as with 1Password; code outside that scope needs its own approval.
- When the request that opens the dialog is `secrets.resolve`, the dialog says what it is for: "hisar in Terminal wants to run a command with 3 secrets" (`hisar run`) or "wants to read 1 secret" (`hisar read`). The detail box shows the command `hisar run` starts, shell-quoted and verbatim, its program as it was given (a script named `./git` reads `./git`, and `hisar mcp` gives the full path it looked up): a long command wraps and scrolls, never cut, and no literal in it is masked, so nothing it runs is hidden. The dialog shows only on the user's own Mac and phones (a phone request is end-to-end encrypted, §9.9), and a literal written into a command came from whoever wrote it, who already has it, so masking it protects nothing; every rule that masked literals by their names either hid code (`token=os.remove` in a Python script) or showed the rest of a credential it cut short. Only `git`'s own credentials are hidden, in `git`'s arguments alone, as in the SSH dialog (§9.6); a shell or any other program that runs `git` is shown as written. The phone shows the same command; one too long for a phone request stays on the Mac, which says so (§9.9). Then the secrets as "vault › item › field" (names when Hisar is unlocked, the reference as written while it is locked; the first 4, then "and N more"), and the terminal device. The approval still covers the whole terminal session: references that are read later in an approved session, by `hisar run`, `hisar read` or any other `hisar` command, do not ask again.

**Standalone releases.** A run of the workflow "Release" with the app `cli` (README.md, "CI and releases") tags `cli-<version>`, and its workflow "Release CLI" builds `hisar` on a Linux runner for linux/amd64, linux/arm64, darwin/amd64, darwin/arm64, windows/amd64 and windows/arm64 (`CGO_ENABLED=0`, version from the tag) and packs `hisar-<version>-<os>-<arch>.tar.gz` (`.zip` with `hisar.exe` for Windows) and `SHA256SUMS` (`scripts/package-cli-release.sh`). The run checks that every file in `SHA256SUMS` is there and matches, then creates the GitHub prerelease `cli-<version>` with all of them; its Publish job, once approved, turns it into the latest release (not for a custom build `cli-1.2.3-label`).

#### 9.4.1 Secret references, `hisar read` and `hisar run`

Developers, scripts and coding agents name secrets instead of copying them: `.env` files, CI-like scripts and agent instructions hold references, and `hisar run` hands the values to one command without writing them to disk or showing them. `core/secretreference` parses, encodes and resolves references for the app and for standalone `hisar`.

**Syntax.** `hisar://<vault>/<item>/<field>`, for example `hisar://Personal/GitHub/password`, `hisar://Work/AWS%20prod/secret%20access%20key` or an API credential's `hisar://Work/Stripe/secret`.

- Exactly three segments, none empty. The scheme is case-insensitive; nothing follows the field (`?` and `#` are part of a name unless encoded).
- Segments are percent-decoded (`%2F` for `/`, `%25` for `%`); a malformed escape or a result that is not UTF-8 is an error. Spaces may also be written as they are, so `hisar://Work/AWS prod/secret key` works in quotes.
- Encoding (the desktop app's "Copy secret reference") keeps ASCII letters, digits, `-`, `.`, `_`, `~` and every non-ASCII character as they are, and percent-encodes every other byte, so a reference is one shell word and one unquoted dotenv value.

**Resolution.**

- Vault: the vault whose ID equals the segment, else the vault whose name equals it ignoring case (Unicode case folding). Item: the same with the item's ID or title, within that vault. No substring matches, unlike `hisar get`.
- Two vaults with that name, or two items with that title in the vault, are an error that says so and lists the IDs to use instead.
- Field, ignoring case: `password`, `username`, `notes`, `url` (the first website), `otp` (also `one-time code` and `code`: the current one-time code, generated when the request is answered), `public key`, `fingerprint`, `connection string` (a database's, built from its fields; on other items a custom field with that label); anything else is the custom field with that label. A built-in label always means the built-in field; two custom fields with the same label are an error.
- `private key` is refused for every item: SSH private keys never leave Hisar, so the reference fails with "SSH private keys never leave Hisar; use the Hisar SSH agent instead". The label stays reserved, so a custom field labelled `private key` cannot be read through a reference either.
- A missing vault, item or field, and an empty value, are errors. Every error names the reference and says why (`hisar://Personal/GitHub/passwrd: item "GitHub" has no field "passwrd"`); a value is never part of a message. All references of a request are checked, and the error lists every one that failed.

**`hisar read <reference> [--no-newline]`** prints one value to stdout, for scripts. It adds a newline only when stdout is a terminal, and never with `--no-newline`. Nothing is masked: the caller asked for the value.

**`hisar run [--env-file path]... [--no-masking] -- <command> [arguments...]`**

- *Collecting.* Every variable of `hisar`'s environment whose whole value is a reference (it starts with `hisar://`), and every variable of the `--env-file` files, given in order. A later file overrides an earlier one; the real environment overrides the files, except a real variable whose value is itself a reference, which a file overrides. Values that are not references pass through as they are.
- *Env files* use dotenv syntax: `KEY=value` lines, `#` comments on their own line or after whitespace in an unquoted value, blank lines, an optional `export ` prefix, keys `[A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*`. Values are unquoted (trimmed), single-quoted (literal) or double-quoted (`\n`, `\r`, `\t`, `\"`, `\\` and `\$` escapes); quoted values may span lines. There is no variable expansion. A syntax error names the file and line, never the value.
- *Resolving.* All references go to the app in one `secrets.resolve` request, which asks for at most one approval, with the command line, and resolve all or nothing. With no references, `hisar run` prints `hisar: no secret references found` on stderr, contacts neither the app nor the server, and runs the command.
- *Starting.* The command gets `hisar`'s environment with the env files applied and every reference replaced by its value. It is looked up on `hisar`'s `PATH` before anything is resolved: a missing command exits 127 and one that is not executable 126, without asking for an approval. stdin is passed through as it is, so the command can read the terminal or a pipe.
- *Masking* (the default). stdout and stderr go through pipes, and every occurrence of a resolved value is replaced with `<concealed by hisar>`. Matching works on bytes, so binary output is fine. Overlapping and adjacent occurrences of different values are replaced as one run when they overlap. A value that spans two writes is still found: `hisar` writes everything at once except a tail that could still be the start of a value, and writes that tail as it is when nothing completes it within 100 ms or the stream ends. Values shorter than 4 bytes are not masked, because they would garble ordinary output; `hisar run` names those variables on stderr. Because the command writes into a pipe, it sees no terminal: colours, progress bars and interactive prompts may change, and `isatty` checks fail.
- *`--no-masking`* gives the command `hisar`'s own stdout and stderr, so it keeps the terminal and its behaviour, and its output is not changed.
- *Signals.* SIGINT, SIGTERM, SIGHUP and SIGQUIT are forwarded to the command. The command shares `hisar`'s process group, so when `hisar` is the terminal's foreground job, the terminal already sends SIGINT and SIGQUIT (Ctrl-C, Ctrl-\) to both, and `hisar` does not forward those a second time; tools such as `docker compose` treat a second Ctrl-C as "stop now". SIGTERM and SIGHUP are always forwarded.
- *Exit code.* The command's exit code, or 128 + the signal number when a signal ended it. Errors of `hisar` itself (a bad reference, a denied approval, a syntax error in an env file) exit 1 and run nothing.
- *After the command exits*, `hisar` writes what is left of its output and exits. A background process that the command started and that keeps the pipes open gets 1 s more; output after that is lost.

**Limits.** The command gets the real values and can do anything with them: write them to a file, send them over the network, or print them encoded, reversed or in pieces, none of which masking catches. Output written straight to `/dev/tty` is not masked. Masking is a guard against accidental display in terminals, logs and coding agents' transcripts, not a sandbox. Values sit in the command's environment, which other processes of the same user may be able to read (`/proc/<pid>/environ` on Linux). An approved terminal session can read any secret through `hisar` until the approval ends (§9.4).

**Standalone**, `hisar read` and `hisar run` ask for the master password like other commands and resolve the references in the `hisar` process with the same rules; the password is read from stdin when it is not a terminal, before the command starts.

**Desktop.** Every field of an item that a reference can name (username, password, one-time code, custom fields; for SSH keys the fingerprint and public key) has a secondary "Copy secret reference" button (a terminal icon, tooltip "Copy secret reference — use it in a terminal with hisar run or hisar read") next to its copy button. It copies `hisar://<vault>/<item>/<field>`, encoded as above, with the vault's name and the item's title, or their IDs when the name is shared by another vault, or the title by another item in the vault. A custom field whose label is a built-in label or is used twice in the item cannot be referenced, and the button says why. A reference is not a secret, so the clipboard is not cleared after 90 s. The SSH key view shows a "Private key" row that says it never leaves Hisar, without the key and without a reference button.

#### 9.4.2 Coding agents: `hisar mcp`

`hisar mcp` is a [Model Context Protocol](https://modelcontextprotocol.io) server on stdio, so coding agents (Claude Code, Cursor, Codex, VS Code, omp and other MCP clients) can use Hisar. The principle: **agents use secrets without seeing them**. They find items by their metadata and run commands with secrets that are masked in the output, as `hisar run` does (§9.4.1); a value reaches the agent only through `get_secret`, which the user approves for that one item in Hisar's approval dialog. `hisar mcp --no-reveal` does not offer `get_secret` and `get_one_time_code` at all, so no value can reach the agent through MCP; the omp plugin, and the Claude Code and Codex servers Hisar connects, start it that way.

**Connecting.** Settings → Developer tools → Coding agents has a row per harness (omp, Claude Code, Codex) with Connect and Disconnect; each connects the `hisar` inside Hisar.app as `hisar mcp --no-reveal`. omp: `omp plugin link` with the plugin inside Hisar.app, whose MCP server is that `hisar`, and `omp plugin uninstall @tryhisar/omp-plugin`. Claude Code: `claude mcp add --scope user --transport stdio hisar -- <Hisar.app's hisar> mcp --no-reveal`, and `claude mcp remove hisar --scope user`. Codex: Hisar writes `[mcp_servers.hisar]` with `command`, `args`, `startup_timeout_sec = 15` and `tool_timeout_sec = 4200` into `$CODEX_HOME/config.toml` (atomically, keeping every other byte; `codex mcp add` cannot set timeouts, and Codex's default 60-second tool timeout is shorter than an approval and a command), checks it with `codex mcp get hisar --json`, and disconnects with `codex mcp remove hisar`. A `config.toml` that is a link stays one, and the file it points to changes; a link to a file that does not exist is refused ("Codex's config.toml links to <target>, which does not exist. Fix the link and try again"), since replacing it would cut the user's link and creating its target would write into a folder that may no longer hold their dotfiles. A `hisar` server is Hisar's only as Connect writes it. For Claude Code that is Hisar's command and arguments with nothing beside them but the `stdio` type and an empty `env`. For Codex it is a table in `config.toml` with exactly those four keys and values and no table below it, which Codex reports running that way: enabled, with no `env`, `env_vars`, `cwd`, `enabled_tools` or `disabled_tools`, and Hisar's timeouts when it reports them. Any other `hisar` server, Hisar's with a setting added or changed included, is a conflict and is never touched. Both agents pass the server's instructions to the model; neither shows progress notifications.

**Server.** `hisar mcp` uses the official Go SDK (`github.com/modelcontextprotocol/go-sdk`, `cli/mcpserver`) and always works through the desktop app: there is no standalone mode, because the app owns the approvals. It connects to `~/.hisar/cli.sock` on its first tool call (not at start, so an agent that never uses Hisar never opens a dialog), keeps that one connection, and sends its requests one at a time; after a lost connection the next call connects again, which needs a new approval. Server name `hisar`, title "Hisar", the CLI's version, and instructions for the model: find references with `search_items` and use them with `run_with_secrets`; for a command that needs live output, interactive input or the agent's own shell environment, run `NAME=hisar://… hisar run -- <command>` (or `hisar run --env-file .env -- <command>`) in the agent's shell instead; never print a secret or ask the user to paste one; if `hisar` in the shell says a sandbox blocks its socket (§9.4.4), use `run_with_secrets` or ask the user to let the command run outside the sandbox; `get_secret` (without `--no-reveal`) is for when the value itself must be shown. The tools and the shell fallback come within the first 512 characters, which Codex advises keeping self-contained. If the app is not running, does not let the command-line tool use it, or is signed out, a tool fails with a message the model can relay.

**Agent visibility.** Each vault has an "Available to agents" switch, per device, kept in `desktop.json` (`agentVaults`: the vault IDs; Reset at sign-out clears it). It is off for every vault until the user turns it on, in Settings → Developer tools → Coding agents, which lists the vaults with the switch and the line "Agents can find items in these vaults and use their secrets after you approve; other vaults stay invisible to them." Agent requests only ever see vaults with the switch on; every other vault is absent, never "forbidden": `list_vaults` leaves it out, `search_items` never finds its items, and a reference into it fails exactly like a vault that does not exist. With no vault available, the tools say how to turn one on.

**Tools.** Inputs and outputs are JSON with schemas; results are also returned as text.

| Tool | Input | Output |
|---|---|---|
| `list_vaults` | — | `vaults`: `[{id, name, itemCount}]` of the agent-visible vaults; `message` when there are none |
| `search_items` | `query` (matches title, username, URLs and vault name, ignoring case; empty lists everything), optional `vault` (name or ID) | `items`: `[{id, vaultId, vaultName, title, type (login, ssh-key, …), username, urls, hasNotes, hasOneTimeCode, fields: [{label, reference}]}]`, at most 50 with `totalCount`. Never a value and never the notes, which may hold secrets; `fields` lists the referenceable fields (username, password, one-time code, custom fields, public key, fingerprint) with their `hisar://` reference, built as "Copy secret reference" does (§9.4.1) |
| `run_with_secrets` | `command` (argv array; no shell) or `shell` (a command line for `/bin/sh -c`), exactly one; the secrets from `env` (map of variable name → `hisar://` reference), `envFiles` (dotenv files, relative to the working directory) and `stdin` (literal text or one `hisar://` reference), at least one secret among them; optional `workingDirectory`, optional `timeoutSeconds` (default 120, at most 3600) | `exitCode`, `stdout`, `stderr`, `isStdoutTruncated`, `isStderrTruncated`, `durationSeconds`, `isTimedOut`, `warnings` |
| `get_one_time_code` (not with `--no-reveal`) | `item`: a `hisar://` reference to any field of the item, or the item's ID | `code`, `secondsRemaining`, `period` |
| `get_secret` (not with `--no-reveal`) | `reference` | `value` |

- `run_with_secrets` runs like `hisar run` with masking always on, in the `hisar mcp` process. Everything that can fail is checked before anything is asked: the command is looked up on `PATH`, the env files are read, and every reference among `env`, the env files and `stdin` is parsed. The environment is built as `hisar run --env-file` builds it (`cli/internal/dotenv`): `hisar mcp`'s environment, then the env files in order (a value that is a whole reference becomes its secret, other values are set as they are, and an inherited variable wins over the files unless its value is a reference), then `env`, which wins over both. A `stdin` reference is sent as the command's standard input and counts as a secret; without `stdin` the command reads nothing. The approval shows everything the command runs with, since a literal `stdin` script or a value such as `NODE_OPTIONS=--require ./x.js` or `BASH_ENV=x.sh` steers it as much as its arguments: its command line is the shell command that does the same, the argv (`/bin/sh -c <shell>` in shell mode) after `env NAME=value …` for each value the env files set that is not a reference and differs from the inherited one, in environment order, then a literal `stdin` as a quoted here-document (`<<'HISAR_STDIN'`, its delimiter lengthened with `_` until the text does not contain it; the here-document ends the text with a newline) or a `stdin` reference as `< hisar://…`. It also lists every secret, including those from env files and `stdin`. `agent.secrets.resolve` carries them as `stdin` and `environment` (`NAME=value`), which an app announces with the feature `agent-run-inputs`; an older app would approve the command without them, so `hisar mcp` refuses a call with a literal `stdin` or literal env-file values before anything is asked: "This Hisar app can't show the command's standard input or environment in its approval. Update Hisar to run this command." Other calls work with any app. The command runs in its own process group; on Windows, in a job object it joins before it runs, so its children are in it too, and the job ends them if `hisar mcp` exits or crashes while the command runs (`JOB_OBJECT_LIMIT_KILL_ON_JOB_CLOSE`, turned off once the command finished, so a background process it left keeps running as on Unix). Every value is replaced with `<concealed by hisar>` before the output is kept; stdout and stderr are each kept up to 64 KiB (the first and last 32 KiB with "[… N bytes not shown …]" between them). At the timeout the group gets SIGTERM, then SIGKILL after 5 s (on Windows every process in the job ends at once), and `isTimedOut` is set. Variable names are `[A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*`, at most 100 secrets per call; every `env` value must be a reference. A value shorter than 4 bytes is not masked and `warnings` says so.
- *direnv.* When `direnv` is on `PATH` and `direnv status --json` in the working directory reports an allowed `.envrc` (in that directory or above it), the command runs as `direnv exec <directory> <command>`, so it gets the project's environment as in the user's shell, and direnv, not `hisar mcp`, looks the program up on the project's `PATH`; the approval shows that command line. A blocked `.envrc` or none runs the command directly.
- *Progress.* When the tool call carries a `progressToken`, `hisar mcp` sends `notifications/progress`: "Waiting for approval in Hisar" before it asks the app, then every 500 ms the output that arrived since the last one, stdout and stderr together, at most 16 KiB per notification. Progress gets only what the masker has let through, and the masker holds back any tail that could still become a secret, so a secret split across writes never shows in part. The final result is the same as without progress.
- `get_secret` refuses `private key` like every reference (§9.4.1). A reference outside the agent-visible vaults fails as not found.

**App protocol.** The agent requests are on the CLI socket (§9.4). A connection becomes an agent connection with `agent.hello` (`agentName`, `agentTitle`, `agentVersion`: the MCP client's `clientInfo` from `initialize`); after that it may send only `hello` and `agent.*` requests, and other connections may not send `agent.*` requests (`invalid-request`).

| Type | Parameters | Result |
|---|---|---|
| `agent.hello` | `agentName`, `agentTitle`, `agentVersion` | `{}`; never needs an approval |
| `agent.vaults.list` | — | `vaults`: `[{id, name, itemCount}]` |
| `agent.items.search` | `query`, optional `vault` | `items`, `totalCount` |
| `agent.secrets.resolve` | `references` (at most 100), `commandLine`, `workingDirectory` | `values` |
| `agent.items.code` | `references` (one) or `itemID` | `code`, `expiresAt`, `period` |
| `agent.secrets.reveal` | `references` (one) | `values` |

**Approval model.** The app checks every request; `hisar mcp` only relays.

- *The agent connection* needs a first approval, like a terminal session (§9.4), but the dialog names the agent: "Claude Code wants to use Hisar", subtitle "via hisar mcp in Terminal". The agent's name is its `clientInfo` title, else a known display name for its `clientInfo` name (`claude-code` → Claude Code, `cursor-vscode` → Cursor, `codex-mcp-client` → Codex, …), else the name itself (printable characters, at most 60), else "A coding agent". The app around it (`in Terminal`) comes from `desktop/appinfo`, starting from the parent of `hisar mcp`, and is left out when it has the agent's name. The detail box shows the `hisar` path and terminal device. The agent's name is what it says about itself; the app and path come from the system. The approval lasts until 10 minutes after the connection's last request, until the connection closes, or until Hisar locks, signs out or quits. It is keyed by the connection, so it never covers a terminal session, and a terminal approval never covers an agent.
- *After that*, `list_vaults` and `search_items` need nothing more.
- *`run_with_secrets` and `get_one_time_code`* ask per call: "Claude Code wants to run a command with 2 secrets" with the command (shell-quoted and whole, as in §9.4.1, with its literal env-file values and `stdin` as above), the working directory and the secrets as "vault › item › field", or "wants the one-time code of “GitHub”". The checkbox "Allow these secrets for this agent for 1 hour" (off by default) lets later calls of this connection that use only those secrets (vault, item and field) run without asking for an hour.
- *`get_secret`* always asks, per item: "Claude Code wants to read “Stripe › api key”" with the warning "The value will be visible to Claude Code and can end up in its transcript and logs." The checkbox "Allow every field of “Stripe” for this agent for 1 hour" (off by default) lets this connection read any field of that item for an hour without asking. Reading is never covered by a "use" approval, and the reverse.
- Remembered secrets and items skip the dialog only while the connection's own approval is current.
- When the first request of a connection is one of these, its dialog also approves the connection ("Approving also lets Claude Code list and search the vaults available to agents"), so the user sees one dialog, not two.
- References are checked before anything is asked: a reference that does not resolve among the agent-visible vaults, or `private key`, fails without a dialog. While Hisar is locked the dialog unlocks it first, as for the CLI (§9.4), with the references as written; they are checked after the unlock.
- Touch ID, the master password and the iPhone (§9.9) answer these dialogs like the other approvals; the phone request is a `cli` request whose app is "Claude Code (via hisar mcp) in Terminal". Remembered secrets and items end when Hisar locks, signs out or quits, or when the connection closes.
- A denial, Deny, Escape or the timeout fails the tool with "The user denied access in Hisar." (or "… did not answer Hisar's approval dialog in time."), which the model can relay.

**Inside an agent harness.** When the process chain of a caller holds an agent harness (§9.4.4), the agent session, not the connection, keys the agent's approval and its remembered secrets and items: every `hisar mcp` connection and every ordinary `hisar` command inside the same harness process shares them, and they end when that process exits. The dialog then names the harness and its app ("omp in Tern wants to use 1 secret") instead of the MCP client's own name.

**Activity log.** Hisar has no local activity or audit view yet, and the server's audit log (§8.3) must not learn which items an agent used, so agent requests are not recorded; an on-device activity log is a follow-up.

**Limits.** "Use without seeing" holds only as far as the command allows: a command run with secrets has the real values and can write them to a file, send them over the network, or print them encoded, none of which masking catches. That is why every `run_with_secrets` dialog shows the command and its working directory: approve only commands you would run yourself. An agent with a shell can also run `hisar` itself in an approved terminal session (§9.4.1). Remembering secrets for an hour lets the agent run any command with them during that hour.

#### 9.4.3 Launchers: the Raycast extension

`raycast/` is a Raycast extension, installed in Raycast's development mode and not published to the Raycast Store. It matches the 1Password extension's features: it searches Hisar, copies and pastes usernames, passwords and one-time codes, opens or edits an item in the app, shares an item through a share link (§5.9), generates passwords and locks Hisar. It talks to the desktop app on the CLI socket (§9.4) directly from Node, without `hisar`, and never keeps vault data on disk: items live in memory for as long as a command's view is open; only the filter selection and the details toggle are remembered.

**Commands.**

- **Search Hisar** lists every item of every vault the user can open, Favorites first, then a section per vault, each row with a star for favorites and the vault's name. One dropdown filters by vault or by category; search also matches the username, the vault's name and the website's host. Return runs the primary action and ⌘↩ the secondary one (preferences, each one of: open in Hisar, open in browser, copy or paste the username, password or one-time code; an action the item lacks falls through to the next). The other actions, with 1Password's shortcuts: Open in Hisar ⌘⇧O, Edit in Hisar ⌘E, Open in Browser ⌥↩, Copy Username ⌘⇧C, Copy Password ⌘⌥C, Copy One-Time Code ⌘⌃C, Paste Username ⌘⇧V, Paste Password ⌘⌥V, Paste One-Time Code ⌘⌃V, Share Item… ⌘⇧S (fields, expiry and views, then the link is copied), Copy Reference ⌘⇧R (a `hisar://` reference of a field), Show Details ⌘Y and Reload ⌘R.
- **My Vaults** lists the vaults with their item counts; Return opens a vault's items.
- **One-Time Codes** lists the items with a one-time code and the current code with its countdown.
- **Generate Password** copies a password from the app's generator (24 characters, digits and symbols); **Generate Password with Options** shows the password, generates again when the length, digits or symbols change (⌘R), and copies (⌘⇧C) or pastes it.
- **Lock Hisar** locks the app; it never starts the app to lock it.
- The preference "Close Raycast after copying" (on by default) decides whether a copy closes Raycast or shows a toast.
- The approval dialog takes the focus, so Raycast hides its window, and Raycast does not let a command launch itself again. Once the user answered a launcher's dialog (not after a timeout or a closed connection), Hisar opens the launcher's app again (`open -a <bundle>`), which brings back Raycast's window with the command and its loaded list. The approval window is on every Space (`CanJoinAllSpaces`), so every approval, SSH and command-line ones too, shows on the Space the user is on, and answering it never switches to the Space of Hisar's main window.

**Protocol.** After `hello`, `launcher.hello` turns the connection into a launcher connection. It may then send only `launcher.*` requests, and other connections may not send them (`invalid-request`). The app announces every launcher request type in `hello`'s `features`, and the extension checks the exact type before sending it, so an older app turns off only the commands that need what it lacks ("Update Hisar").

| Type | Parameters | Result |
|---|---|---|
| `launcher.hello` | — | `isApproved`; refused with `denied` from a caller with a terminal or without an app macOS verified |
| `launcher.items.list` | — | `vaults` `[{id, name}]` sorted by name, `items` `[{id, vaultID, title, category, username, urls, tags, isFavorite, hasPassword, hasOneTimeCode, references: [{field, reference}]}]`; no value and no notes |
| `launcher.items.code` | `vaultID`, `itemID` | `code`, `expiresAt`, `period` |
| `launcher.items.password` | `vaultID`, `itemID` | `password`, for one paste |
| `launcher.items.copy` | `vaultID`, `itemID`, `field` (`username`, `password` or `one-time code`) | `clearAfterSeconds`; the app copies the value, so it never crosses the socket |
| `launcher.items.open` | `vaultID`, `itemID`, optional `isEditing` | `{}`; the app's window comes forward with the item selected, or its editor open in a vault the user can edit |
| `launcher.items.share` | `vaultID`, `itemID`, `shareLink` (`fields`, `expiresInSeconds`, `maxViews`; no fields shares the username and password) | `shareLink` with its `url`, as `shareLinks.create` |
| `launcher.passwords.generate` | optional `generator` (`length`, `digits`, `symbols`) | `password` |
| `launcher.lock` | — | `{}` |

`core/cliprotocol/testdata/launcher.json` holds an example of every exchange; the Go tests and the extension's tests both read it.

**Approval.**

- A launcher is approved per app (§9.4: bundle path and Team ID of a verified signature), in a memory separate from the command line's, so approving Raycast as a launcher never approves `hisar` run from Raycast, or the reverse.
- The first request that reads the vaults, copies or opens an item opens the approval dialog: "Raycast wants to search Hisar and copy or paste passwords and one-time codes", with Raycast's icon and a note that it cannot change or export anything and that every share link asks first. Touch ID, the master password or the iPhone (§9.9, kind `launcher`) answer it, and a locked Hisar unlocks.
- `launcher.items.share` asks every time, even while Raycast is approved, like `hisar share-link`: "Raycast wants to create a share link for “GitHub”", with the fields, the expiry and views, and the warning that anyone with the link can read them. Approving it creates that one link and approves nothing else: Raycast's general approval still needs its own dialog. A denial runs nothing. The iPhone shows it as one request, without the "until Hisar locks" line.
- The approval lasts until Hisar locks, signs out or quits, or the command-line switch goes off. Settings → Developer tools → Command-line tool → Launchers lists the approved apps with Revoke.
- `launcher.passwords.generate` and `launcher.lock` need neither an approval nor a sign-in: generating reads nothing, and asking before a lock would unlock Hisar.
- Raycast runs every extension in one Node process without an OS sandbox, so once Raycast is approved any of its extensions can send these requests until Hisar locks. That Node runtime lives in Raycast's folder in `~/Library/Application Support`, outside the signed bundle, so the approval trusts the user's own account as much as Raycast does. App identity rests on the bundle's code signature on disk, not on the running process's.

**Clipboard.** Every copy the Mac app makes, from its own windows and for launchers, is marked `org.nspasteboard.ConcealedType` and `org.nspasteboard.TransientType`, so clipboard managers skip it (`desktop/pasteboard`). The 90-second clear reads the pasteboard back only while it still holds Hisar's own copy (its change count), so it does not read what another app copied since; reading and clearing are two steps, so a copy made by another app in the instant between them can still be cleared. A paste needs the value in Raycast: the extension reads one password or code, closes its window and pastes. Generated passwords are copied by Raycast itself with `concealed`.

#### 9.4.4 Agent harnesses

An agent harness is a program that runs a coding agent and the commands it asks for, such as omp, Claude Code or Codex. A request from a process that descends from a harness gets agent rules (§9.4.2) on the CLI socket, whatever it is: `hisar mcp`, or an ordinary `hisar` command the agent runs in its shell. The rule covers those descendants only.

**Finding the harness.** `core/agentharness` holds the table of harnesses. Each row names the harness and the ways to recognize its process: a code signature (signing identifier and team), an executable name (the executable's file name or the first argument), a version folder (the folder a native install keeps its executables in, each named after its version; the executable or the first argument directly inside it matches), a script a runtime runs, or an argument inside a package. A row may also name the argument prefix of the harness's own workers.

| Harness | Signature (identifier, team) | Executable name | Version folder | Script | Package |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| omp | `omp-darwin-arm64`, `VTLHGQC72S` (Tern's) | `omp` | `…/Tern/omp/versions` | `omp` run by Bun | `@oh-my-pi/pi-coding-agent` |
| Claude Code | `com.anthropic.claude-code`, `Q6L2SF6YDW` | `claude` | `…/claude/versions` | `claude` run by Node | `@anthropic-ai/claude-code` |
| Codex | `codex`, `2DC432GLL2` | `codex`, and `codex-<target>` for `aarch64` and `x86_64`, `apple-darwin` and `unknown-linux-musl` | | `codex` run by Node | `@openai/codex` |

omp's workers carry an argument starting with `__omp_worker_`. Codex's release archives name the native executable after its target, and Homebrew's `codex` cask installed that file in most releases before 0.146.1, which installs the archive with `bin/codex` instead; the link the cask puts on the `PATH` is named `codex` either way, but a process started by the file's own path has only the target's name. A native install started by its own path, as an MCP client that resolved the link does, has that path as its first argument too: without a signature, which the CLI does not read, only the version folder names omp in Tern or Claude Code's native install. The names match any program called `claude` or `codex`, with no further guard: a program that only shares the name gets agent rules, which are stricter than a terminal's (hidden vaults, a dialog per secret, no standalone sign-in), and carries no checked mark, while a guard such as a required install path would miss installs nobody measured (a source build). The app reads the caller's chain from the kernel (`desktop/appinfo`, with `kern.proc.pid` and `kern.procargs2` on macOS and `/proc` on Linux) as a snapshot of process instances: each with its PID, start time, parent, path, arguments, working directory and, on macOS, the signing identifier and team of the running process when a certificate Apple issued signs it (`SecCodeCopyGuestWithAttributes` with the PID, then `SecCodeCheckValidity` against `anchor apple generic`; ad hoc and self-signed code has none). The kernel has already checked every page the process loaded, so this took 10 to 30 ms per process; verifying the file instead hashes all of it, which took 1.0 s for Claude Code 2.1.302 (268 MB) and 1.9 s for Codex 0.162.1 (247 MB) and, with the rest of the chain, made the first request from inside Claude Code run past `hisar`'s 5-second wait for `hello`. After the walk it reads every process again; a process that is gone or exited, has another start time or parent, a parent that started after its child, or a walk longer than 64 processes makes the chain unknown, so a signature read from a PID that was reused during the walk never counts. The nearest harness process is the agent session: its PID and start time, so a reused PID is another session, and its working directory as the project. A worker is not a session of its own: it belongs to the session of the harness process that started it.

**Scope.** The agent session comes first in the approval scope (§9.4), before the terminal session and the app. A caller whose chain is unknown may hold a harness, so it gets neither the command line's rules nor a launcher's: `hello` answers, and every other ordinary request and `launcher.hello` fail with "Hisar couldn't check which program is asking. Try again." (`denied`). Reading the chain again on the next connection usually works. Its `agent.hello` (`hisar mcp`) keeps agent rules, approved for its connection only.

- *Ordinary commands inside a harness* get agent rules. `hisar run` (`secrets.resolve` with a command) asks for a use grant per secret; `hisar read` (`secrets.resolve` without one) and `hisar get` (`items.get`) ask for a reveal grant per item, with the reveal warning. `vaults.list` and `items.list` show only the vaults open to coding agents and need the agent's approval; a hidden vault fails like one that does not exist, and its references fail before any dialog. `hello` (`hisar status`) answers as before. Every other request fails with "<harness> can use secrets through hisar run. Change items in the Hisar app." (such as "omp can use…", `denied`).
- *Grants* are keyed by the live agent session. `agentharness.Sessions`, one registry the CLI socket and the SSH agent (§9.6) share, starts a session on the first request either socket gets from inside the harness process and gives it an ID that never repeats, so a second omp, omp after a restart, or omp after a revoke is another session and asks again; an answer to a dialog of a session that ended records nothing and fails, so it never reaches the session the harness's next request started. Grants end at lock, sign-out and quit, and when the session ends: the harness exits (the registry watches it with `kqueue` `NOTE_EXIT` on macOS, a pidfd on Linux, polling elsewhere) or Settings revokes it. Then both sockets drop its grants, SSH approvals and remembered commit signing, cancel its open dialogs and close its connections. They check that the harness instance still runs before they reuse a grant, complete an approval or sign.
- *Launchers.* `launcher.hello` is refused inside a harness.
- *Agent hosts.* The app a harness runs in (Tern, Terminal) becomes an agent host, kept in `desktop.json` (`agentHosts`, kept on sign-out). Recording it ends its app approvals of the command line and of launchers, and denies a dialog for its app approval that is still open when the user answers it. From then on its callers without a terminal and without a harness in their chain are approved per connection, and none of them is a launcher. A connection from the app opened before that is held to the same rules from its next request: it is approved per connection, and as a launcher it is refused.
- *The CLI* reads its own chain with the same table (without signatures) before it would sign in on its own: inside a harness `--standalone`, `HISAR_STANDALONE=1`, and an app that does not answer fail with "hisar runs inside <harness>, so it uses the Hisar app only." (such as "inside omp"), and with an unknown chain with "hisar couldn't check which program runs it, so it uses the Hisar app only. Try again." `hisar status` still answers, with `mode: none (<why the app did not answer>)`. A connection to the socket that fails for permission (`EPERM` or `EACCES`) is a sandbox around the shell, such as Codex's: `hisar` starts no app and, where it would have reported the refusal above or "not logged in", says "hisar can't reach the Hisar app: this shell's sandbox blocks its socket. Run the command outside the sandbox, or use the run_with_secrets tool."

**What the user sees.** The approval window names the harness and its host, "omp in Tern wants to use 1 secret", with the host app's icon and, under the title, the folder the harness works in (the last part of its working directory, the full path on hover). The harness name carries a checked mark only when the process matched its row by code signature; a match by name, path or script, which any program can copy, carries none. An agent outside a harness is named by what its MCP client says in `agent.hello`, labeled as the name the agent gave. **Settings → Developer tools → Coding agents** lists the live agent sessions that used either socket (harness, host, folder, when Hisar first heard from it, and its remembered secrets) and updates when a session starts, remembers a secret, or ends (event `agent-sessions-changed`). **Revoke** ends a session as its exit would, for both sockets: its grants, SSH approvals, remembered commit signing, open dialogs and connections end, and its next request asks again as a new session. Below the sessions, the agent hosts are listed with "Apps that ran a coding agent ask for every command-line request without a terminal." and **Forget**, which removes the app from `agentHosts` so its callers get app scope again until a harness runs in it again.

**Chains measured** on macOS:

- omp 18.12.3 in Tern 0.9.4, the `bash` tool: `hisar` ← `sh` ← omp (`…/Tern/omp/versions/18.12.3`, first argument `omp`, signed by `VTLHGQC72S`) ← `tern daemon` ← launchd. Children of omp have no controlling terminal.
- omp, a managed service: the command ← omp's worker broker (the same signed executable, first argument `…/versions/18.12.3 __omp_worker_daemon_broker`) ← omp ← `tern daemon`. Each omp session starts its own broker, and the broker may start more workers (`__omp_worker_text_predict`). A worker belongs to the session of the omp that started it, so a service shares the session and its grants with the `bash` tool. Any other omp is its own session, even a direct child of omp, such as one started with `sh -c 'exec omp'`.
- omp's PTY path and client terminals were not measured.
- omp installed with `bun add` and started through its bin link runs as `bun ./node_modules/.bin/omp`; its executable is Bun's.
- Claude Code 2.1.302 (native install), the Bash tool: `hisar` ← `/bin/zsh -c …` ← Claude Code (`~/.local/share/claude/versions/2.1.302`, first argument the `~/.local/bin/claude` link, signed `com.anthropic.claude-code` by `Q6L2SF6YDW`) ← whatever started it (measured: a shell of omp in Tern, so Claude Code, the nearer harness, is the session). `hisar mcp` is a child of Claude Code too, so the Bash tool and the MCP tools share one session. The shell has no controlling terminal even when Claude Code has one. An npm install runs as `node …/@anthropic-ai/claude-code/cli.js` (not measured).
- Codex 0.162.1 (npm install), `codex exec`: `hisar` ← `/bin/zsh -lc …` (or the command itself, when zsh replaces itself with it) ← the native `codex` (`…/@openai/codex-darwin-arm64/vendor/aarch64-apple-darwin/bin/codex`, signed `codex` by `2DC432GLL2`) ← `node /opt/homebrew/bin/codex` ← whatever started it. `hisar mcp` is a child of the native `codex`, so the shell and the MCP tools share its session; the Node launcher matches too, but the native process is always nearer. Children have no controlling terminal. The Codex app's `codex app-server --managed-daemon` runs under a `codex app-server daemon` of the same executable; the app-server, the nearer one, is the session (its processes measured, commands under it not).

**What the rule does not cover.**

- *An approved secret is readable by the agent.* `hisar run` puts the value in its child's environment, where the agent's own command can print it encoded or send it anywhere; masking matches only literal values of 4 bytes or more (§9.4.1). The dialog shows the full command, so the user judges each use.
- *Work handed to another program leaves the chain.* A double fork or a launchd job: inside an agent host the caller is approved per connection; elsewhere it resolves as a background process with connection scope. `tmux send-keys` into a pane the user owns, or AppleScript that types into a terminal: the command runs in the user's terminal session and can use its approval, and nothing tells it apart from the user's typing. `open -a`, `launchctl` or `ssh localhost`: the chain has no omp and belongs to the opened app, launchd or `sshd`. A script the agent writes and the user runs later runs with the user's own approvals.
- *omp's subagents share one process*, so they share its grants; so do Claude Code's subagents, and every thread of one Codex app-server.
- *A harness that is renamed and re-signed* is not recognized; the table matches what omp, Claude Code and Codex ship as.
- *Codex's sandbox.* Codex runs shell commands in a Seatbelt sandbox that refuses every Unix socket, so `hisar` there cannot reach the app (it says so; see *The CLI*), unless the user lets that command run outside the sandbox. `hisar mcp` runs outside it, so `run_with_secrets` works, and the commands it runs run outside Codex's sandbox too, once the user approves them in Hisar's dialog, which shows the whole command and its working directory. Codex's own approval policy for MCP tool calls still applies before Hisar is asked.

### 9.5 Go memory handling

Go's garbage collector can copy memory and strings are immutable, so secrets cannot be reliably wiped. Rules:

- Keys and secrets are `[]byte`, never `string`, inside `core/`.
- Long-lived keys (AUK, keyset, vault keys) live in `memguard` enclaves and are opened only for the duration of an operation.
- Plaintext item data is zeroed after use where possible, and the lock action drops all references.

### 9.6 SSH agent (MVP)

Like 1Password, the desktop app is an SSH agent for keys stored in vaults, so private keys never sit in `~/.ssh`.

**SSH Key items**

- Item category `ssh-key` (`item.Overview.Category`). The overview holds the title, the public key as an `authorized_keys` line (`publicKey`), the SHA-256 fingerprint and the key type (`ssh-ed25519`, `ssh-rsa`, `ecdsa-sha2-*`); the details hold the private key as an unencrypted OpenSSH PEM (`privateKey`), protected by the vault encryption like every other secret.
- Generate inside the app (Ed25519 by default, RSA 4096 if requested), or import an existing OpenSSH, PKCS#1, SEC 1 or PKCS#8 key. Passphrase-protected keys are decrypted on import and stored without the passphrase.
- The UI never receives a private key: `ItemDetail` carries only the category, public key, fingerprint and key type. Editing an SSH key changes only its title and notes; the backend keeps the key material from the cached item.
- The detail view shows the key type, fingerprint and public key with "Copy public key" (copied through the backend and cleared after 90 seconds) for `authorized_keys`, GitHub and so on.
- **Add to GitHub / Add to GitLab** copy the public key and open `https://github.com/settings/ssh/new` or `https://gitlab.com/-/user_settings/ssh_keys`, with the hint to paste it and choose the key type there (GitHub: "Authentication Key" for SSH, again as "Signing Key" for commit signing; GitLab: "Authentication & Signing").
- **Set up commit signing** unfolds a panel with the exact commands, each with a copy button, and **Copy all**:

  ```
  git config --global gpg.format ssh
  git config --global user.signingkey "key::<key type> <base64 key>"
  git config --global commit.gpgsign true
  mkdir -p ~/.ssh && echo "$(git config --get user.email) namespaces=\"git\" <key type> <base64 key>" >> ~/.ssh/allowed_signers
  git config --global gpg.ssh.allowedSignersFile ~/.ssh/allowed_signers
  ```

  The key is the public key without its comment, so the commands need no further quoting. Hisar never runs them: the user pastes them into a terminal, and the `allowed_signers` line lets `git log --show-signature` verify the user's own signatures. Signing then goes through the agent and asks as described under Approval.

**Agent**

- Implements the OpenSSH agent protocol with `golang.org/x/crypto/ssh/agent`, including the `session-bind@openssh.com` extension (OpenSSH 8.9 and later, `PROTOCOL.agent`): `string hostkey, string session_id, string signature, bool is_forwarding`. As `ssh-agent` does, the agent verifies the signature of the session ID with the host key (any algorithm the key allows), keeps at most 16 bindings per connection, accepts the same binding again but refuses one for the same session with a different host key or flag, and refuses any binding after one for user authentication (`is_forwarding` false). A user authentication request (`publickey`, or `publickey-hostbound-v00@openssh.com` with its host key) on a connection whose last binding is for authentication must carry that binding's session ID and host key. Clients that never send it (older OpenSSH, other SSH libraries, `ssh-keygen`) get the behaviour without it: no verified destination, and the request counts as local.
- Listens on `~/.hisar/agent.sock` (directory 0700, socket 0600; `HISAR_SSH_AGENT_SOCKET` overrides the path). It starts with the app and stops when the app quits; the app opens at login and keeps running without a window (§9.1.2), because an SSH client cannot start it. If the socket cannot be created (for example another agent is listening there), Settings shows the error.
- Offers the SSH keys of every vault the user can access while Hisar is unlocked; personal vault keys come first. A request from inside an agent harness (§9.4.4) gets only the keys of the vaults open to coding agents (§9.4.2), and a signature with another key fails as one the agent does not hold (see Agent sessions). Locked, a request for keys or a signature opens the approval dialog, which unlocks Hisar (see Approval); signed out, it offers no keys. It is read-only: requests to add or remove keys, and the agent's lock/unlock requests, are refused.
- Settings → SSH & Git shows the status and, only when the user clicks "Use Hisar for SSH", adds this section to the top of `~/.ssh/config` after copying the file to `~/.ssh/config.hisar-backup-<timestamp>` (the file is created with mode 0600 and `~/.ssh` with 0700 if missing):

  ```
  # Added by Hisar: use the Hisar SSH agent
  Host *
    IdentityAgent "~/.hisar/agent.sock"
  # End of Hisar section
  ```

  Nothing changes if the start line is already present. "Remove from SSH config" backs the file up again and deletes the lines between the markers; backups never overwrite each other, and a symlinked config is written through to its target.
- Servers allow about 6 key attempts per connection (`MaxAuthTries`). With more keys, the user points `IdentityFile` in `~/.ssh/config` at the exported public key to pick one per host; the agent still does the signing.
- Works for `git` over SSH and for SSH commit signing (`gpg.format ssh`), since both go through the agent.

**Approval**

Every use of a key asks, unless an approval for the same key and app is remembered. The dialog says who asks, for what and where, like "Terminal wants to push to git.tryhisar.com".

- **The app.** Every approval (SSH sign-in and push, commit and tag signing, the command-line tool, `hisar run`, `hisar mcp`, share-link creation from the command line) names the GUI app responsible for the request, so "iTerm2 wants to sign a commit" rather than "ssh-keygen wants to sign a commit". The agent and the command-line socket read the peer PID (`LOCAL_PEERPID` on macOS, `SO_PEERCRED` on Linux), and `desktop/appinfo` resolves it (`appinfo.Resolver`):
  1. It reads up to 16 processes from the peer to its oldest ancestor before `launchd` (path from the kernel, arguments and working directory).
  2. The first process, nearest first, whose outermost `.app` bundle is a running app with a Dock presence (`NSRunningApplication`, activation policy regular) is the app; so is the app macOS holds responsible for a process (`responsibility_get_pid_responsible_for_pid`, which maps Electron and VS Code helpers and what they start to the app). `Code Helper (Plugin).app` inside `Visual Studio Code.app` resolves to Visual Studio Code; Cursor, Zed, JetBrains IDEs, Terminal, Ghostty, Warp, WezTerm, Alacritty and kitty come out as themselves. Developer tools and toolchains are walked past: anything under `/Library/Developer/` or `*.app/Contents/Developer/` (`/usr/bin/git` is an `xcrun` shim that runs Xcode's `git`), programs outside bundles and Hisar's own helpers (`Hisar.app/Contents/Helpers/`, `Hisar.app/Contents/MacOS/hisar-*`). Without a Dock app in the chain, the nearest bundle that is not a developer tool counts (a menu bar app).
  3. A chain whose oldest process left its parent behind (its parent is `launchd`) is followed further: iTerm2 runs its shells under `iTermServer-<version>` in `~/Library/Application Support/iTerm2/`, a daemon that outlives iTerm2's restarts and whose parent is `launchd`, so the parent walk alone never reached iTerm2 and the dialog showed `ssh-keygen`. A daemon outside any bundle whose code signature is valid and carries a Team ID belongs to the one running Dock app signed with the same Team ID (iTermServer and iTerm2 are both signed by H7V7XYVQ7D); with none or several, it does not count. A `tmux` or `screen` server leads to its clients: when all running `tmux` (or `screen`) clients resolve to one app, that app, with `tmux` last in the chain; otherwise "tmux". An `sshd` (or `sshd-session`) in the chain makes it "an SSH session from <host>" (the remote address of the session's TCP socket, read with `proc_pidfdinfo`), or "an SSH session". Anything else with `launchd`, `cron` or `launchd`-started jobs above it is "a background process"; without a parent chain it is "a process without an app".
  - The headline name and icon come from the bundle on disk, never from `argv[0]`, the environment or the request: the name Finder shows, or the bundle's `CFBundleDisplayName`/`CFBundleName` when that extends it (iTerm.app is "iTerm2"; Visual Studio Code.app's "Code" does not count), and `NSWorkspace iconForFile:` rendered as a PNG, both cached per bundle path. The executable path comes from the kernel (`proc_pidpath`, symlinks resolved), not from the path the process was started with. The code signature of the bundle (`SecStaticCodeCheckValidity` without resources, cached per bundle path and modification time) gives the signer line "<App> is signed by Apple", "<App> is signed by <developer> (<Team ID>)", "<App> isn't signed" or "<App>'s signature isn't valid"; the last two show a warning, so a fake "iTerm2" with a copied icon but no valid signature stands out.
  - The chain under the app is listed by program name (from the kernel's executable path) nearest first, for example `ssh-keygen ← git ← zsh`, without the app's own processes, `login` and the daemon used in step 3; each program's arguments (with `git`'s credentials hidden as for the command) are in its tooltip.
  - The bundle path is also the app approvals are remembered for (below).
- **The purpose.** From the same process chain (path, arguments and working directory of each process; never the environment) and the signed data, the approver derives:
  - `ssh … git-receive-pack '<repository>'` (or `git-lfs-authenticate … upload`): "push to <host>"; `git-upload-pack`, `git-upload-archive` or LFS `download`: "pull from" when a `git pull` is above it, "clone from" for `git clone`, else "fetch from";
  - any other `ssh` to a host: "sign in to <host>";
  - signed data in the SSHSIG format (`ssh-keygen -Y sign`) with the namespace `git`: "sign a commit", or "sign the tag <name>". `git` passes the commit or tag to `ssh-keygen -Y sign -n git -f <key> <file>` in a temporary file; the approver reads it (a regular file of at most 64 KiB, the last argument of an `ssh-keygen -Y sign` process in the chain) for the subject line, and the branch from the repository's `HEAD`. Without it, the dialog says "a commit";
  - anything else: "use your SSH key", with the destination when a session binding names one.
  - The host comes from the `ssh` arguments (the destination, `-p`, `-o Port=`, `ssh://host:port`, port 22 by default), else from the session binding (the host key looked up in `~/.ssh/known_hosts` and `/etc/ssh/ssh_known_hosts`, `[host]:port` entries included), else the host key fingerprint. The command is the outermost `git` process's arguments (or `ssh`'s when there is no `git`), quoted like a shell would, at most 160 characters. Only `git`'s own credentials are replaced by `•••`, and only in a `git` process's arguments: the value of a `-c` option whose name ends in a part that mentions a token, password, secret, header or auth (`http.extraHeader`, `github.token`), never an alias's value (`-c alias.<name>=…`, `git config alias.<name> …`), which runs; and a URL's password made only of characters that are never shell syntax, so a mask hides no command when `git` hands an argument to a shell (an alias, `git bisect run`). Every other program's arguments, a script included, are shown as written (§9.4). The repository is the working directory of the `git` process next to `ssh` or `ssh-keygen` (git runs in the top directory of the work tree), with `$HOME` shown as `~`.
- **The dialog** (`?window=ssh-approval`, a window created hidden at launch, reused, always on top, 480 points wide, its height fitted to the content, about 400): the title "<App> wants to <purpose> <host>", the subtitle `with your SSH key "<key title>"`, the app's icon with a small Hisar badge, under the title a line with the process chain (`ssh-keygen ← git ← zsh`) and the app's signer line ("iTerm2 is signed by Apple", "iTerm2 is signed by George Nachman (H7V7XYVQ7D)"; a red warning line for an unsigned app ("iTerm2 isn't signed") or one whose signature does not verify ("iTerm2's signature isn't valid")), a two-line monospace detail box (the command, e.g. `git push origin main`; then `<repository> · <host>:<port>`; for commit signing the subject line, then `<repository> · <branch>`), the checkbox "Don't ask again for <App> for 1 hour" (or "until Hisar locks", after the Settings choice; checked by default), and a footer bar with the Touch ID glyph (Apple's `LAAuthenticationView`, embedded through `biometric.WindowPrompt` over a slot the page measures), "Touch ID to approve" / "or use your master password", and Deny. Touch ID starts as soon as the dialog shows a request, so touching the sensor approves without a click, as in 1Password; the reason is `allow "<app>" to <purpose> <host>` (one Touch ID prompt at a time). If it fails, the dialog stays open with a message and Try again restarts it. "or use your master password" shows a password field instead (Return submits it; the password is checked against the account, offline from the cache). Without Touch ID, an Approve button approves. Escape, Deny and closing the window deny, and cancel a Touch ID check in progress. The checkbox can be changed while Touch ID waits; its value when the request is approved counts.
- **Compact approvals** (opt-in: Settings → SSH & Git → "Compact SSH approvals", off by default, stored in `desktop.json` and kept on sign-out): a card about 380 points wide at the top right of the display under the pointer, like a notification (16 points inside the visible area: below the menu bar and notch, clear of a Dock on the right; when its content changes height the top-right corner stays put): the app's icon, "<App> · <action>", `<host> with "<key title>"`, the warning line of an unsigned app, and inline "Touch ID to allow for 1 hour" (or "until Hisar locks"; for a commit, "Touch ID to sign") and Deny. Clicking the card expands it into the full dialog in the middle of the same display; the full dialog otherwise opens in the middle of the display under the pointer. Only requests that need nothing else use it: Hisar unlocked, Touch ID available, not forwarded; the others always open the full dialog.
- **Commit signing** asks once per signature by default, and is never covered by a remembered SSH approval: the dialog shows a "Sign a commit" (or "Sign a tag") badge and no checkbox.
- **Remembered commit signing** (opt-in: Settings → SSH & Git → "Remember commit signing": **Always ask** by default, **Up to 10 minutes** or **Up to 1 hour**; `signingRememberSeconds` in `desktop.json`, a device preference kept on sign-out). Only with the setting on does the commit signing dialog show the checkbox "Don't ask again for <App> in <repository> for 10 minutes" (or 1 hour), unchecked every time: the setting is the deliberate opt-in, and the checkbox scopes each use of it. Checked when the signature is approved, later commit signatures are signed without asking while all of these match:
  - the app: the bundle path that app attribution resolved (above), or the agent session for a request from inside an agent harness (see Agent sessions). A request without a resolved app ("a background process", an SSH session, tmux with clients in several apps, a program outside any bundle) and outside a harness never offers it, so it never spans apps or reaches a process the user cannot see;
  - the repository: the working directory of the `git` process next to `ssh-keygen` (the purpose's repository); a signature without one never offers it;
  - the key fingerprint.

  It lasts the chosen time from the approval, not from the last use, and ends earlier when Hisar locks (the lock hook calls the approver's `ClearAllRememberedSigning` next to the agent's `ClearAllApproval`) or when the setting changes. Tag signatures, forwarded requests and keys set to "ask every time" always ask. A `git rebase` (likewise `cherry-pick` or `am`) with `commit.gpgsign` runs `ssh-keygen -Y sign` once per commit, each a child of the same `git` in the same work tree and app, so one approval with the checkbox covers the whole run; without the checkbox it asks per commit as before. The compact card never offers it (its inline Touch ID signs once); expanding the card shows the checkbox. When Hisar is locked, the unlock dialog for a commit signature offers it too, since the signature names its key.
- **Locked.** When Hisar is locked and the user is signed in, a request for keys or a signature opens the dialog with "Unlock Hisar to use your SSH keys" instead of failing, and the dialog unlocks Hisar the way it does for the command-line tool (§9.4): with Touch ID when Touch ID unlock is ready (§5.4; one Touch ID check unlocks and approves), otherwise with the master password typed into the dialog (Return submits; a wrong password keeps the request open with a message). The unlock approves that agent connection, so the signature that follows on it does not ask again, and the checkbox is remembered for the key that then signs. For handed-off work (below) the unlock covers only that first signature: the connection's next one asks, even when its app became an agent host after the unlock. The unlock's approval ends when Hisar locks, signs out or switches account, and a signature whose approval was still open then is refused, since its key was read before. Deny or the timeout leave that connection without keys, as before. Signed out, the agent offers no keys and shows nothing.
- **Forwarded requests.** A connection with a binding whose `is_forwarding` is true is a request from a remote host through agent forwarding (`ssh -A`): the local `ssh` binds the forwarded connection to the host it connected to. The dialog then shows a red banner "Forwarded from another computer — this request comes from <host> through agent forwarding, not from this Mac" (the host of the first forwarding binding from `known_hosts`, else the local `ssh`'s destination, else the host key fingerprint), offers only "Allow once" (no checkbox, never remembered), and Deny is the default button: Return denies, and Touch ID starts only after "Allow once". It never uses the compact layout. The docs recommend `ProxyJump` over agent forwarding.
- **Remembering.** "Don't ask again" remembers the approval per (key fingerprint, app): the app bundle's path, or the program's path when there is no app; a program without a known path is never remembered. It lasts one hour (`DefaultApprovalLifetime`) or until Hisar locks, whichever comes first; the vault backend calls the agent's `ClearApprovals` from its lock hook. **Remember SSH approvals** (Settings → SSH & Git: **Up to 1 hour** by default, or **Until Hisar locks**; `sshRememberSeconds` in `desktop.json`, 3600 or -1, a device preference kept on sign-out) drops the hour: with Until Hisar locks the agent's approval lifetime is 0 and a remembered approval ends only when Hisar locks, signs out or quits. The dialog's checkbox, the compact card and the phone's switch say the chosen period (`rememberPeriod` in the `ssh-approval` event: "for 1 hour" or "until Hisar locks"). Changing the setting applies to approvals already remembered. Auto-lock counts input anywhere on the Mac (§9.1), so the hour holds while the user works in other apps. Forwarded requests, commit and tag signatures (see Remembered commit signing), and keys set to "ask every time" (`SetAllAlwaysAskKeyID`; no UI yet) are never remembered here.
- **Agent sessions** (§9.4.4). The agent reads its caller's chain once per connection, as the command-line socket does (`agent.PeerResolver` over `appinfo.Resolver.ReadChain`), and passes the result in `sshagent.Peer`. A chain that cannot be read (it changed during the walk, or the peer's process is unknown) is refused: the agent closes the connection, so the caller gets no keys and no signature, as the command-line socket refuses an unknown chain. A caller inside a harness joins the harness's live session in `agentharness.Sessions`, the one registry both sockets share (§9.4.4):
  - *Inside a harness.* The dialog names the harness and its app, "omp in Tern wants to sign in to github.com", and "Don't ask again" remembers per (key fingerprint, live session ID), so another omp, omp after a restart, or omp after Settings revoked its session, asks again, and the user's own approvals for Tern do not cover omp. Such an approval lasts one hour at most (`AgentSessionApprovalLifetime`), also under Until Hisar locks, which its dialog never offers; it ends at lock and when the session ends, by the harness's exit or a revoke in Settings. The session's end also closes its SSH connections and cancels their open dialogs, and a signature is refused once the session ended or its harness no longer runs, even right after its approval. Remembered commit signing covers (live session ID, repository, key). The app the harness runs in is recorded as an agent host through the command-line socket (`clisocket.Server.RecordAgentHost`), as when a harness calls `hisar` there, so its app approvals of the command line and of launchers end the same way.
  - *Keys.* The key source sees the session in the request's context (`sshagent.GetAgentSession`) and lists only keys in vaults open to coding agents, so `ssh-add -l` shows those and a signature with another key fails. When a session gets no key at all, Hisar shows one system notification per session, "omp asked for an SSH key. No key is in a vault open to coding agents.", and clicking it opens Settings → Developer tools → Coding agents.
  - *Handed-off work.* A caller without a terminal in an agent host, whose chain has no harness, may be work an agent handed off, such as a double fork under launchd. No remembered approval of its app or program covers it, for sign-ins or commits, and its dialog has no checkbox. Whether its app is an agent host is checked on every request, so a connection opened before the app hosted a harness loses the app's remembered approval once it does. A caller with a terminal, such as the user's own Tern pane, keeps the approvals above.
- **Approving on a phone.** While Hisar is unlocked, the dialog is also sent to the account's phones and its footer adds "or approve on your smart device" (§9.9); a request too long for a phone stays on the Mac and the footer says so instead. A phone's approval counts as a Touch ID approval, a phone's Deny as Deny. Whether a phone's approval is remembered is the choice made on the phone, where the request shows it as a switch ("Also allows iTerm2 for 1 hour"), never the Mac's checkbox (§9.9).
- **Events.** The backend emits `ssh-approval` (with a random request ID and the request as shown), `ssh-approval-failed` and `ssh-approval-closed`, and waits for `RespondSSHApproval(requestID, isAuthorized, isRemembered)` or `RespondApprovalWithPassword(requestID, password)`; `SetSSHApprovalRemembered` follows the checkbox, `SetSigningRemember(seconds)` stores the Settings choice (0, 600 or 3600; the status reports `signingRememberSeconds`), `SetSSHRemember(seconds)` stores how long SSH approvals are remembered (3600 or -1; the status reports `sshRememberSeconds`) and `FitSSHApprovalWindow(isCompact, height)` sizes and places the window (centered, or at the top right). Each request has its own hard deadline, set when it arrives: 60 seconds, or 2 minutes when the iPhone can answer (§9.9). The deadline runs in the backend whatever the window does: shown, hidden, still waiting behind earlier requests, or not drawn at all because the screen is locked or asleep. At the deadline, or as soon as the client disconnects (the agent and the command-line socket read the connection ahead of the request they serve, so they see the peer close while a request waits, also behind bytes it sent after the request; a peer with more than 16 MiB unread is cut off), the request is denied, a Touch ID check in progress for it is cancelled, it leaves the window and its phone request is withdrawn. The window shows requests one at a time in the order they arrived; a stuck request holds the ones behind it at most until its own deadline, and each of them is already on the phone, which can answer them in any order. The window hides when no request is left. Showing the window and sending its events never hold up a request: they run in order on their own goroutine. `ListAllPendingSSHApproval` lets the window show requests that arrived before it loaded. The same dialog handles the command-line tool's requests (§9.4).

**Implementation**

- `core/sshagent` implements `agent.ExtendedAgent` over two interfaces: a key source and an approver. It has no UI dependency and is tested in CI with an in-memory approver and a real `x/crypto/ssh` client and server.
